Trevisani promuove la svolta di Chivu: “Finalmente l'ho visto parecchio incazzato”

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Trevisani promuove la svolta di Chivu: “Finalmente l'ho visto parecchio incazzato”

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Il giornalista Riccardo Trevisani, durante Fontana di Trevi, ha parlato del cambio di strategia nel post partita del tecnico dell'Inter Chivu:

L'Inter gioca a calcio, quella di Chivu come quella di Inzaghi. Poi si disconnette. Chivu ha voluto Jones, Inzaghi ha voluto Arnautovic: tutti sbagliano. Ma perché non si discute mai di chi va in campo?

L'Inter a Bergamo aveva vinto prima ancora di andare in campo. L'ha vinta sul 2-0, poi sul 3-1, poi sul 4-2... E non l'ha vinta. E noi parliamo di tattica.

Chivu ha cambiato strategia. Dopo aver sempre detto “poverini, poverini”, dopo averli sempre protetti, domenica l'ho visto parecchio incazzato per l'atteggiamento. Spero e penso, per il bene dell'Inter, che faccia presente questo suo rammarico ai giocatori. Perché, se c'è una cosa di cui abbiamo la percezione, è che raramente i giocatori dell'Inter si sentano minacciati da chicchessia. Li vedi belli sereni in campo. La prova è quando, a forza di errori, invece di arrivare secondo arrivi quarto.

L'Inter deve guardarsi dentro e trovare delle soluzioni. Detto che la difesa è meno consistente, io rimetterei Bisseck titolare. Il portiere non fa stare tranquillo nessuno: la difesa, Chivu, i tifosi. C'è una serie di problematiche da risolvere. Anche l'allenatore non è esente da responsabilità, ma hai preso quattro gol dall'Atalanta, che non segnava nemmeno con le mani».

Rivoluzione Coppa Italia: Inter e Inter U23 potrebbero affrontarsi in finale. Il documento

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rivoluzione Coppa Italia: Inter e Inter U23 potrebbero affrontarsi in finale. Il documento

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Inter contro Inter U23 in una finale di Coppa Italia? Uno scenario che oggi appare quasi fantascientifico, ma che trova spazio nelle ipotesi di riforma della competizione presentate dalla Lega Serie A.

La Lega ha infatti pubblicato il bando per l’assegnazione dei diritti audiovisivi della Coppa Italia e della Supercoppa Italiana per il triennio 2027-2030, inserendo due possibili formule alternative per la coppa nazionale.

La novità più interessante per il mondo nerazzurro è contenuta nel cosiddetto Format B, che prevede l’allargamento della competizione a 56 squadre e apre alla partecipazione di un numero maggiore di società di Serie C, comprese potenzialmente le seconde squadre dei club di Serie A.

Ma c’è un passaggio particolarmente curioso.

�Le Seconde Squadre non possono incontrare le rispettive Prime Squadre se non in Finale».

Questo significa che, qualora venisse adottata la nuova formula e l’Inter U23 ottenesse il diritto di partecipare, la formazione di Cristian Chivu e la seconda squadra nerazzurra non potrebbero affrontarsi nei turni precedenti alla finale.

Un eventuale incrocio tra le due formazioni sarebbe dunque consentito soltanto nell’atto conclusivo della competizione.

Naturalmente si tratta di una possibilità puramente teorica, che richiederebbe a entrambe le squadre di raggiungere la finale.

Per l’Inter U23, tuttavia, sarebbe un’opportunità importante per confrontarsi anche con avversarie delle categorie superiori.

MONZA, ITALY - FEBRUARY 04: General view inside the dressing room ahead of Coppa Italia match between FC Internazionale and Torino FC at U-Power Stadium on February 04, 2026 in Monza, Italy. (Photo by Mattia Pistoia - Inter/Inter via Getty Images)

Il Format A ripropone sostanzialmente l’attuale struttura della Coppa Italia.

Il Format B, invece, introduce una serie di modifiche significative:

•⁠ ⁠56 squadre partecipanti: 20 di Serie A, 20 di Serie B e 16 di Serie C. •⁠ ⁠Ingresso delle otto teste di serie ai sedicesimi, anziché agli ottavi di finale. •⁠ ⁠Semifinali in gara unica, eliminando il doppio confronto di andata e ritorno. •⁠ ⁠Possibilità per le seconde squadre di partecipare in base ai criteri previsti. •⁠ ⁠Impossibilità per le seconde squadre di affrontare le rispettive prime squadre prima della finale.

Per le grandi società di Serie A, dunque, cambierebbe anche il percorso verso la conquista del trofeo.

Le otto teste di serie dovrebbero iniziare la competizione un turno prima rispetto a oggi, ma l’eliminazione delle semifinali di andata e ritorno compenserebbe l’impegno supplementare.

Inizialmente, il Format B prevedeva una corsia preferenziale per le seconde squadre delle società di Serie A, indipendentemente dal campionato nel quale militassero.

Secondo quanto riportato da Tuttomercatoweb nella serata del 24 novembre, la Lega Serie A ha successivamente corretto il testo, eliminando questa priorità.

Si sarebbe trattato di un errore materiale nella stesura del documento.

In caso di adozione del nuovo formato, dunque, le sedici società provenienti dalla Serie C verrebbero individuate secondo criteri di merito, senza favorire automaticamente le seconde squadre.

Anche l’Inter U23, quindi, dovrebbe conquistare sul campo il diritto di partecipare alla competizione, secondo i criteri che saranno stabiliti.

La Lega ha infatti presentato entrambe le ipotesi agli operatori televisivi interessati all’acquisto dei diritti per le stagioni 2027/28, 2028/29 e 2029/30.

Le offerte economiche delle emittenti contribuiranno a determinare quale delle due formule verrà adottata.

Il Format B, che comprende più partite e una partecipazione più ampia delle società di Serie C, potrebbe quindi entrare in vigore a partire dalla stagione 2027/28.

E per l’Inter si aprirebbe anche uno scenario davvero particolare: vedere la propria seconda squadra partecipare alla stessa Coppa Italia della prima formazione, con la possibilità di un derby nerazzurro soltanto in finale.

Una prospettiva ancora lontana, ma che per la prima volta trova spazio nelle ipotesi ufficiali della Lega Serie A.

Anthropic just gave Claude users a new way to make Mac apps

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Use Claude on Mac or Windows? Anthropic just released a new way to make personalized desktop apps using AI tools.

“Starting today, you can use Claude artifacts to create, run, and share desktop apps. The “Install as app” feature is rolling out in beta in Claude for Mac and Windows,” Anthropic says.

“When an artifact is installed as a Mac or Windows app, it gets its own icon and opens in its own window instead of inside Claude. You can share it with others, and it needs your permission before it can interact with your computer. You choose how much to allow.”

Additionally, Anthropic says apps made with this method can benefit from Claude connectors. Claude usage is tied to desktop apps made with artifacts.

This form of Claude-created app is capable of sending notifications, adding items to the menu bar, and having its own permissions.

You can learn much more about the new system here. This is the second Claude update this week made specifically for builders.

NBC Tracking Storm Due To Hit 100th Annual Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade: Will Balloons Be Grounded?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NBC is keeping close watch on a Thanksgiving storm expected to bring high winds and heavy rain to New York City and much of the Eastern U.S.

The network’s coverage of the 100th edition of the annual Macy’s Thanksgiving Day parade will air on NBC and stream on Peacock from 8:30 a.m. to noon ET. The parade remains a significant draw, with last year’s pulling in 34.3 million viewers across NBC and Peacock, its biggest combined audience on record.

Tyler Roys, a senior meteorologist with AccuWeather, said in an interview with Deadline that the second half of the parade seems to be at the most risk of being disrupted. He said the winds will be “near criteria” for grounding balloons – a signature feature of the New York City event, along with marching bands and floats. “They have some decisions to make over the next 48 hours,” Roys added.

RELATED: Deadline’s Guide To 2026 Holiday Specials, TV Programming Plus Where To Watch Classic Christmas Movies Watch on Deadline

NBC’s affiliate in New York expects it to be “a close call” if conditions on the Midtown Manhattan streets fall below city rules mandating that balloons be grounded in sustained winds of 24 miles per hour or gusts higher than 35 mph.

The Nor’easter, a classic storm pattern, is expected to take shape. By Thursday morning, sustained winds are expected to be 12-25 miles an hour with gusts of 20 to 30 mph, increasing over the course of the day.

In honor of NBC’s broadcasting centennial, the parade is set to have 100 large balloons. The “All-Star Broadway Thanksgiving Day Celebration” will feature numerous shows in a 10-minute medley. The balloons have only been grounded once before, in 1971.

About 3 million people watch the parade live, with the Manhattan skyscrapers creating a wind tunnel that can heighten the effects of the weather. In 1997, a wind-blown Cat in the Hat balloon clipped a light pole, sending it tumbling to the street below. The accident injured four spectators, one seriously.

“Our team is closely monitoring the weather forecast with our city and agency partners, and as always, the safety of our participants and spectators is our top priority,” NBC said in a statement provided to Deadline. “Decisions regarding balloon flight will be made on parade morning based on weather conditions and established safety protocols.”

NBCUniversal’s ad sales teams have been pushing the 100th anniversary, and the division said Monday it had established a revenue record for the parade. The company’s holiday programming, which extends beyond the parade to include a Thanksgiving Eve special, Christmas in Rockefeller Center and daily opportunities on Today and The Tonight Show, posted a 30% revenue gain over last year. The company said 50 new advertisers have joined the fold.

The Improbable Success of a ‘Poky Little Motoring Show’

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Top Gear’s worldwide fame was a happy accident.

Through the lens of time, the early part of the Aughts can seem like a simpler age when phones were usually dumb, politicians were sometimes smart, and families gathered quaintly around one TV set to watch the same program at the same time. High-resolution internet video and the streaming revolution were distant spots on the horizon. But the changing landscape unwittingly created the perfect conditions for an accidental phenomenon: a TV show about cars with an estimated global audience of 350 million, a broadcasting triumph that would be impossible to replicate today. Because the Aughts was the decade that seeded the improbable success of a self-confessed “poky little motoring show” called Top Gear.

This story originally appeared in Volume 38 of Road & Track.

In fact, Top Gear predated this century, debuting on BBC television in 1977 and plodding through the Eighties and Nineties with sensible car-buying advice and cozy trips to steam rallies. The series added Jeremy Clarkson to its hosting lineup in 1988 to bring a bit of wit to what was still a fundamentally earnest format. In 1999, he left the show, and two years later, the BBC announced that Top Gear was going to be “rested,” a polite British way of saying “canceled.”

At the same time, Jeremy and an old school friend, TV producer Andy Wilman, who had been a lesser Top Gear presenter, were working on plans for a bigger, more boisterous car show called Carmageddon. When the pair pitched it to the head of the BBC Two channel, she liked the idea and agreed to commission the show with one proviso: Carmageddon should become a reboot of Top Gear. The fuse had been lit, but free­wheeling global success wasn’t the master plan. In fact, there was no grand scheme, only a vague hope to make a halfway-­decent show for audiences in the U.K. I know this because from 2002 to 2015, I was Top Gear’s script editor.

This, like so much of the show, was something of an accident. I’d worked briefly with Jeremy on the old version of Top Gear, and he’d liked my satirical car website, Sniff Petrol, but if I hadn’t bumped into him in a restaurant and been invited to “come in for a chat,” perhaps the opportunity would have passed me by. As it is, I went to see Jeremy and Andy, and I listened to their ideas, many of which sounded strange. A racing driver who couldn’t tell you anything about a car because he never spoke? A segment forcing celebrities to drive around a track in a low-budget grocery getter? An indoor studio for a car show? Hmm. But Jeremy and Andy are persuasive people, and I agreed to join nonetheless.

We auditioned a lot of people to co-host and eventually found Richard Hammond, a car journalist who performed an unremarkable screen test but then reduced us to hysterics with self-deprecating stories as he was walking toward the exit.

We made a pilot episode, which was a disaster, then a second pilot episode, which was also a disaster, after which point there was no time to try again because we had a scheduled on-air date. We’d go to the pub after work and sit there making despairing sighs and ask, “What are we doing?” We were not plotting world domination. Our biggest goal was to get a show out on time so the BBC didn’t have to broadcast reruns instead.

But we were allowed to carry on, and our first season attracted modest viewing figures, just passing the low target the BBC had issued. Back then, some broadcasters were willing to nurture shows and let them grow gently rather than demanding instant success.

Top Gear needed this patience because it often felt like we were making it up as we went along. In the popular consciousness, the show is remembered for Jeremy Clarkson, Richard Hammond, and James May driving malfunctioning cars across harsh and distant lands. But it didn’t start like that at all. We didn’t have James in season one (BBC management thought he was too similar to Jeremy); instead, a car dealer called Jason Dawe was in the third spot. We didn’t go abroad because we were a British show and thought we should film in the U.K., no matter how gray and rainy it might be. We didn’t put the three hosts together in the field until the fourth season, an experiment that yielded nuggets of gold. We never planned to create epic road trips occupying entire shows, until a journey across the Southern United States generated too much material to squeeze into a 20-minute film and was allowed to roam free as an hour-long special.

What we did have from the start was a certain editorial rigor. Jeremy began his career as a local newspaper journalist. Richard had a background in live radio. James worked on car magazines for years. They’d grown up in worlds that demanded quick thinking and an endless supply of ideas. Sometimes Jeremy would stride into the office booming, “I’ve had a thought . . . .” Sometimes we’d sit around, batting stupid notions back and forth.

As part of my job, I’d scribble down what we said, then try to hack the best bits into something workable. After that came the important part. Any idea, no matter how brilliant it first seemed, was then poked and prodded and dismantled to make sure it was as good as it could be. When we had a full episode’s worth of vetted ideas, I’d sit down with one or all of the presenters, and we’d write a script. For a solo track test, this would be detailed and extensive. For a three-header out in the field, the introduction would be tightly written to make sure our mission was clear, in our minds and to the viewers, after which there would be rough scene headings and perhaps the odd one-liner. As a production team, we’d plan situations and challenges that we thought would allow the hosts to run free with ad-libs and arguing.

Jeremy, Richard, and James were very different people with a love of cars as their common ground. We never had to fake their on-screen personalities; that’s just who they were. Jeremy, bombastic and attention seeking. Richard, excitable and a verbal sniper with killer one-­liners. James, often giving the impression that he’d rather be at home dis­mantling a motorcycle than forced to hang out with these two morons. The dynamic worked.

That such a poorly planned piece of programming eventually found itself a global success is down to the decade in which it was born, a time when people still watched the same show at the same time. One inadvertent stroke of genius was the BBC’s decision to schedule Top Gear on a Sunday evening, traditionally a quiet night on TV in the U.K. Among straight-faced detective shows and florid costume dramas, the hijinks of Top Gear—a nun in a monster truck, a bus jumping motorcycles, a man trying to destroy a To­yota Hilux in increasingly elaborate ways—shone like a beacon of eccentricity. The next morning, everyone could go into work or school and talk about it.

Ratings grew steadily. Then, during filming in 2006 for the ninth season, Richard crashed a jet-­propelled dragster. The show was paused while he recovered from serious injuries. When we returned, viewing figures in the U.K. jumped dramatically.

Global success came later, especially with the growth of YouTube and other social media. ­Suddenly anyone could clip out their favorite bits of Top Gear and make them accessible all around the world. An American gearhead called Alex Mills founded a site called FinalGear, where fans could discuss Top Gear and share BitTorrent links so people everywhere could download the show, if not entirely legally. Top Gear became cult viewing in countries where car shows weren’t trying to turn pickup trucks into boats and a three-wheeled car into a space shuttle. Which was, of course, everywhere else.

Also, the car landscape of the Aughts gave the show great material upon which to build. It was the era of the hypercar, and at its peak was that stats-heavy poster child, the Bugatti Veyron. But it was also a time when Subaru and Mitsubishi were slugging it out to make the best roadgoing rally car. Countless manufacturers were serving up everything from high-powered wagons to hyperactive hot hatches. Best of all, in the U.K. at least, secondhand cars were fantastically cheap and accessible, even old supercars.

At the time, living hand to mouth simply trying to get the show done, we didn’t realize how lucky we were, though I hope we made the most of the luck that came our way. The reports of ever-­bigger audiences seemed abstract and unbelievable. Only when it became harder and harder to find any country in the world where our filming presence wouldn’t attract a large and excitable crowd did we start to realize that perhaps this stupid show of ours was catching on.

Nonetheless, we rarely had time to rest on our laurels. Indeed, Andy Wilman actively tried to stop us from becoming full of ourselves by, among other things, stridently discouraging attempts to make our disgustingly shabby offices nicer. He believed we had a job to do, and that job was to make an hour of entertainment for Sunday nights on BBC television. The rest was an implausible-­sounding bonus. And perhaps by dint of not trying too hard to be globally appealing, Top Gear became a worldwide phenomenon of the Aughts and beyond. It really couldn’t have happened at any other time.

'So No One Told You Life Was Gonna Be This Way'

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 24, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Showing up for friends on their good days is easy. You can celebrate their wins and enjoy their happiness. But being there, really being present for friends when they’re having a rough day or when they do dumb things, that can be more challenging. Our friend is upset, so they spend too much money, or they’re worried, so they pull out of something they had to do, or they’re angry, so they break things. How do we validate their emotions without endorsing their choices?

So, No One Told You Life Was Gonna Be This Way

This month, Marsha Linehan, developer of DBT, died. One of her many contributions to the psychology world was how to validate emotions with precision. She described six levels of how to validate emotions. The aim is to validate with the highest level possible, but different situations will require different levels. At all times, the goal is to help people notice that their emotion is valid and welcome, even if you don’t agree with the behavior that follows. Your friend doesn’t need a lecture after they’ve done something wrong; they want to feel emotionally understood.

The most basic level is to simply stay awake and pay attention. I say "simply," but in these days of constant distractions, and phones and devices trying to hold our focus, even just being fully present can be a challenge. How can you show your friend that you’re really listening? You can use eye contact, nodding, asking questions, saying "uh-huh," and more.

The second level is accurate reflection. When you paraphrase back what they’ve said, you let them know that you’ve understood what they’re saying, especially the emotion they’re trying to communicate. Do this without criticism or advice, even if you don’t agree with everything they’ve said. Validation isn’t approval of behavior; it’s letting them know that their feelings are welcome.

Level three is reflecting the unarticulated parts. Let them know that you’ve understood even the aspects they haven’t explicitly told you. Name emotions that you’re guessing they’re probably feeling, e.g., "Oh my gosh, you must have been so hurt by him saying that." It doesn’t even matter if you guess the right emotion. If they reply, "Actually, I was more angry than sad," then you still have lots of emotion to validate.

At the fourth level, you are validating using past history or biology. Letting your friend know that their behavior makes sense based on their life so far. Statements like "After all you’ve been through this year, I get why this would feel so painful" can put context to their uncomfortable emotions. Even if you would have felt differently in the same situation, you are able to validate their emotion because you understand that their road to this point has been different from yours.

Even more powerful than that is normalising the emotion. Letting your friend know that anyone in that situation would probably feel the same way. Saying things like "I get so upset when that happens," or "That’s something that worries me too," or "We all have moments like that." We can normalise the emotion, even when the behavior wasn’t something we agree with. We can understand that when we’re upset, we make choices that aren’t always the most helpful, and the emotion is still valid. Also, you can normalize their emotion without launching into your own similar story; if your friend is upset, they need you to listen to their pain.

Finally, the highest level of validation is radical genuineness. You listen to them describe their experiences, and you respond with genuine emotion. You're not patronising and saying "there, there." Instead, you are moved by their situation, and you care about them. This is demonstrated through words, body language, and emotion.

Validating your friends (or partner or family member) with the highest level of validation that feels appropriate for the situation is what will help you wholeheartedly be there for them when they’re broke, their job’s a joke, or their love life’s DOA.

Koerner, K. (2012). Doing dialectical behavior therapy: A practical guide. The Guilford Press.

Linehan, M. M. (1993). Cognitive-behavioral treatment of borderline personality disorder. Guilford Press

Linehan, M. M. (2020). Building a life worth living: A memoir. Random House.

Make a two-bar comparison understandable without colour

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A chart can look clear on your own monitor and still make readers work too hard. If the instructions say “compare the blue and orange bars”, the reader has to match colours before they can understand the numbers.

For a small comparison, put the category and value beside each bar. Here is a deliberately simple HTML/CSS example using fictional completed-task counts: 42 for Project A and 68 for Project B.

The bars are decorative duplicates of the adjacent text, so the wrapper uses aria-hidden="true". The actual category names, values and units remain ordinary HTML text. Do not hide the text labels along with the bars.

Both tracks have the same width and represent 100 tasks. A 42% bar therefore represents 42 tasks in this particular example. If the maximum changes, calculate each width from value / maximum × 100 and update the stated scale too. A percentage width without a clear shared scale can mislead readers.

These checks cover a small example; they are not a complete accessibility audit. More complex charts need additional work, including meaningful summaries and suitable access to their underlying data.

The principle of providing a visible cue in addition to colour is explained in W3C's Understanding Use of Color.

Try it: replace Project A and Project B with two fictional categories of your own. Ask someone to explain the comparison without referring to either bar's colour.

Should Vanguard S&P 500 ETF Investors Be Worried About the Downturn in Nvidia and AI Stocks?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia and AI stocks have been dragging down the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:52PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

Once the largest company in the world, with an over-$5 trillion market cap, Nvidia (NVDA -7.60%) had been one of the biggest driving forces in the market. However, the stock closed out the week ending Nov. 20 on a seven-day losing streak, losing 27.5% of its market value in this span. But then there was a strong rebound on Monday. As I write this, the stock is down 32% from the $243.37 high it set less than two months ago, on Oct. 6.

Nvidia's troubles stemmed from the surprise resignation of its longtime co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang following revelations of marital infidelity, allegedly including with subordinates and a potential Chinese spy. Even a strong quarterly report and the company raising its fiscal 2028 (ending January 2028) revenue guidance to 75% growth were not enough to stop the stock's slide. However, a surprise return to the CEO role by Huang on Monday led to a huge rebound in the stock.

Nvidia's woes spread to other semiconductor stocks, with the entire artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure segment under intense pressure to close out last week. Cloud computing and software-as-a-service (SaaS) stocks also generally performed poorly this past week, although they also joined in on the rally.

Entering November, Nvidia was the second-largest holding in the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.68%), with a nearly 7.6% weighting. That was just behind Apple (AAPL +0.99%), which represented around 7.7% of the index. As such, the stock's recent performance has weighed on S&P 500 exchange-traded funds (ETFs), like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.62%), which lost 7.8% the week ending Nov. 20.

The Nasdaq Composite(^IXIC -1.63%)was also down. Even with the Monday rally, the question is whether investors should worry that Nvidia and other AI stocks will push the benchmark indexes into a bear market.

The S&P 500 has become less diversified over the past decade, as mega-cap tech companies have come to dominate the index. At the start of the month, over 37% of the stocks in the index were classified as tech stocks, and that doesn't include tech-oriented companies like Amazon (AMZN -1.27%) and Tesla (TSLA -0.41%), which get placed in the consumer discretionary category. Meanwhile, 10 of its top 15 holdings were AI stocks.

As such, Nvidia and the tech sector are going to have a big influence on the performance of the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. However, the great thing about the S&P 500 is that it is self-correcting. As Nvidia and other AI stocks fall, they become less and less of the index, while stocks from other sectors become a larger percentage.

It is this survival-of-the-fittest structure the index has created that has made it such a strong performer over the years. It lets its cream rise to the top, its losers fade, and new stock leaders emerge. That's one of the big reasons why, over the past decade, only about 14% of actively managed funds have been able to outperform the S&P 500.

Ultimately, Nvidia doesn't need to be the leader of the S&P 500 going forward. There are plenty of other leaders that can step up, including fellow "Magnificent Seven" names like Alphabet (GOOGL -1.35%) (GOOG -1.41%), Amazon (AMZN -1.27%), or Meta Platforms (META -0.88%), or perhaps even a stock not yet in the index like SpaceX (SPCX -5.05%).

Image source: OpenAI (AI-generated illustration).

There is also a possibility that what the market is seeing is just a normal correction, and AI infrastructure stocks will bounce back quickly. Regardless, history teaches us that it's time in the market, not timing the market, that wins in the long run.

As such, I would not be rushing to sell my Vanguard S&P 500 ETF on fears of a further drop in AI stocks, and instead would be using this opportunity to continue to dollar-cost average into the ETF. That's a proven strategy that works again and again over the long term.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 796%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 200% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

3 Non-AI Stocks to Buy for Long-Term Dividends and Stability

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

These stocks have limited exposure to artificial intelligence and pay between 2.7% and 5.6% in dividends.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:52PM EST

10 stocks we like better than ExxonMobil ›

Artificial intelligence (AI) stocks are struggling, as investors appear to be having second thoughts about inflated valuations. Anthropic’s stock had a strong first day of trading, but since then it's been struggling. Space Exploration Technologies Corp, another AI stock that went public this year, has also been falling in recent weeks and is trading below its IPO price.

Many investors may be growing concerned about all the exposure to AI. However, there are still excellent stocks to consider that have limited or no exposure to AI. ExxonMobil (XOM +1.24%), McDonald's (MCD +1.38%), and Realty Income (O +1.65%) are excellent blue chip dividend stocks that aren't heavily investing in AI and can be relatively stable investments to hold on to, and which can generate tremendous dividend income over the long haul.

Here's a look at why these stocks may be worth buying right now.

ExxonMobil's business centers around exploring and producing crude oil and natural gas. While the company has integrated AI into its business to improve efficiency, it's largely the price of oil, rather than AI-related sentiment, that will move the stock.

And with oil prices reaching multi-year highs, the stock has been on a tear this year, rising 32% and outperforming the S&P 500, which is up just 5%. An added incentive to buy the oil and gas stock is that it has raised its payout for decades, and even after its strong rally this year, its yield remains relatively high at 2.7%. By comparison, the average S&P 500 stock pays about 1.1%.

As a leading company in the oil and gas sector, ExxonMobil can make for an ideal stock to invest in today, especially with oil prices remaining elevated and the war in Iran looking like it'll extend into next year.

Another solid stock to own for safety and dividends is McDonald's. The fast-food giant is deploying AI to help determine prices in various markets. While finding what it calls "the optimal price" based on location may be a bit controversial, it's an example of how AI may enable McDonald's to squeeze out more revenue.

But like with Exxon, its core business remains unchanged. AI might help McDonald's strengthen its margins, but its success will hinge on its ability to deliver quality meals for consumers while keeping prices relatively low. It's done an excellent job of that over the years, and it's a reason why the stock may be a solid investment, even if there's a downturn in the economy, as its low-cost meal options could be attractive for budget-conscious customers.

At 2.8%, McDonald's also pays a fairly high dividend. This year, it also joined the ranks of Dividend Kings, as it extended its dividend growth streak to 50 years. For long-term investors, McDonald's can be a no-brainer stock to hang on to.

Realty Income is the final stock on this list, and it's yet another example of a business that can be a suitable option for long-term investors who don't want to worry about AI.

It's a real estate investment trust (REIT) that has a diverse mix of clients, so inevitably there may be some exposure to AI, but it isn't likely to be significant. As of the end of September, the industries that accounted for the highest percentage of its annualized base rent were grocery, convenience stores, home improvement, dollar stores, and quick-service restaurants. Those five areas accounted for about 37%. And when strictly looking at data centers, only four properties fell into that category, compared with a total of 15,536 properties in its entire portfolio.

The REIT pays the highest dividend on this list, as it currently yields 5.6%. It also makes payments every month and has been increasing its dividend for decades. It's a solid stock for income investors to buy and hold.

Sitting behind the wheel may do something unusual to your heart rate

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Driving keeps the heart above its resting rate for longer, even when the body remains seated and inactive.

Sitting behind the wheel is usually treated as no different from sitting anywhere else. Heart-rate readings that were collected for a week from 57 young adults in Texas now suggest otherwise.

During the time these people spent driving, their heart rates remained above resting level almost throughout. A return to resting level was recorded about half as often as during other inactive hours.

Their hearts were not found to beat faster in the car than during those other hours. The resting pace was simply reached less often while a person was driving.

The study was designed and supervised by Ioannis Pavlidis who is a computer science professor at the University of Houston.

Colleagues from the same university and from the Texas A&M Transportation Institute worked with him.

Pavlidis said that a driver has to keep watching the road and deciding what to do next.

“The body may be sitting, but the mind is working,” he said. According to him, it is in the heart rate that this mental work can be seen.

What the study isn’t able to show is that driving itself kept anyone’s heart rate up. The comparison that was made was between time spent driving and other inactive time in one week.

There are more differences between these two kinds of time than the car alone.

Posture and caffeine are on the list of them that the authors gave and so are passengers. How urgently a driver needed to arrive is on that list as well.

The group consisted of 30 men and 27 women with an average age of about 26. Most of them were office workers.

A personal iPhone and Apple Watch were used by each participant from Monday through Sunday.

Every car was fitted with a small plug-in device by which each period of driving could be recorded. The watch was the source of the heart rate readings to which weather and traffic conditions were matched.

The rest of each day was labeled with the help of activity data from the phone and the watch. Any recorded minute in which a person was neither driving nor physically active was taken into the comparison.

Such minutes may have been spent at work or at home or somewhere else entirely.

The batteries of the iPhone and Apple Watch had usually run out by early evening. It is for this reason that the recordings cover daytime hours alone.

It was Pavlidis’ group at the Affective and Data Computing Laboratory that averaged the readings minute by minute. The recordings that resulted came to 177,318 minutes which is close to 3,000 hours.

No heart-rate reading existed for 13.6% of the driving minutes or for 3.5% of other inactive minutes. Neither speed nor traffic was found to be linked to the readings that were missing.

Driving took up about a tenth of that time. There were 973 trips in total and half of them were over within 15 minutes. A crash or a traffic ticket was generally not part of these drives.

Before any single minute could be judged, the team had to know each person’s resting heart rate.

An estimate of it is produced by the Apple Watch once a day. These daily estimates were averaged by the team across the whole week.

While a person was driving, their heart rate was above their own resting level 93.7% of the time on average. The figure for other inactive time was 81.3%.

Yet a higher heart rate in the car was not what the Houston team found. During driving, it averaged 11.2 beats per minute above resting level. The average for other inactive time was 11.5 beats above resting level.

A gap as small as that could have come up by chance.

Where driving did differ was in how little the heart rate varied. Its typical spread was 7.98 beats per minute during driving and 12.40 beats during other inactive time.

Heart rates dropped to resting level 2.46 times per hour during other inactive time and 1.31 times per hour while driving.

On weekdays, the two shares of time above resting level were 93.8% and 82.1%.

Weekends showed the same pattern, although people then spend their hours outside the car differently.

According to Pavlidis, a workout also raises heart rate, but the body becomes fitter as a result of it. From the time that is spent behind the wheel, no benefit of that kind is gained.

Yasmine Bouzid and the other authors also examined what a higher or lower heart rate was tied to.

During driving, there were two things that stood out from the rest. Time of day was the first since heart rates were higher in drives after noon than in the morning.

The second was the amount by which the driver’s wrist had turned in the minute before. The gyroscope of the Apple Watch supplied this measure which was treated as a sign of steering.

Away from the car, the list that was found was a broader one. On it were age and sex along with time of day and the Big Five personality trait of openness.

For this, the authors offered one reason which they regarded as possible. Driving is a single task whose demands remain fairly steady from one minute to the next.

Many different situations are covered by the inactive time that is spent away from the car.

Because a drive is repeated day after day, an example using anxiety was worked through by the team.

The volunteers had filled out the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory, which scores a tendency to feel anxious from 20 to 80. The lowest score that was found in this group was 20 while the highest was 61.

Over every recorded minute of driving, the team’s heart-rate model was then run twice. A score of 20 was given to every driver in the first of these runs. In the second run, it was a score of 61 that every driver was given.

The heart rate that the model predicted with the score of 61 was higher by 0.99 beats per minute.

What the Houston team then did was to count such beats over a whole year. For a person who drives eight hours a day, the model projected about 173,000 extra heartbeats.

It came to roughly 11,000 extra heartbeats when the same was done for a 30-minute daily drive.

It was only as illustrations that these totals were described by Pavlidis and his coauthors. What they show is how a small difference accumulates, and not what anyone’s risk of heart disease is.

There were only young adults from Texas among the 57 volunteers, and none was followed for more than a week. Older drivers, professional drivers, or people with heart disease may not show the results that were found here.

It will take years of following drivers before the health side of this can be judged. Only then can it be learned whether more time above resting heart rate predicts heart problems.

Pavlidis said that driving is something many people can’t do without. What his lab in Houston wants to find out is how the same trips could be made less demanding.

The Texas A&M Transportation Institute, where study co-author Michael Manser works, is supporting the lab through 2027.

The people who are to be studied next include long-distance commuters and those who drive for a living.

Rideshare drivers are an example of the latter. The groups are meant to be bigger, more varied, and followed for longer.

There is one more question that self-driving cars from Waymo and Tesla have raised for the lab. What does a rider’s heart rate do when it is the vehicle that handles the driving?

The full study was published in the journal npj Digital Public Health.

debugfs (de e2fsprogs) opera a nivel de bloques en sistemas ext*, por lo que no respeta los permisos UNIX del kernel. Si puedes abrir /dev/sda1, puedes leer cualquier ruta interna del FS.

Abusé de debugfs para leer directamente la partición ext4 y así bypassar los permisos de UNIX. Al abrir /dev/sda1 con debugfs pude ejecutar cat /root/root.txt desde el propio prompt de la herramienta, recuperando la flag de root sin elevar privilegios por el camino. Este vector sólo es posible porque el dispositivo de bloque era legible para mi usuario (misconfiguración típica en entornos CTF).

How to fix failing tests with an AI agent (without it deleting the tests)

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 24 • Originally published at openmel.dev

To fix failing tests with an AI agent, tell it which command must pass and what it may not touch, then let it run that command itself. In Mel – the agentic terminal (openmel.dev), put your test command in AGENTS.md or MEL.md, attach the failing output with Ctrl+↑ (⌃↑ on a Mac), and write a finish line such as "make pytest pass without skipping or deleting a test". Mel's agent reads the failing tests and the code, changes what it needs, and runs the tests again before it says it is done. You approve each edit and command that changes something, unless your settings allow it.

This guide covers the one rule that stops an agent from "fixing" tests the wrong way, how to set up your project once, the step-by-step in Mel, and how to handle a whole red test suite.

A failing test is one of the best tasks you can give an agent, because it comes with its own finish line. The agent does not have to guess what "done" means: the same command that failed must pass. It can run that command, read the exact error, change the code and run it again, as many times as it needs. A chat window cannot do that loop, because it cannot run anything; you would be copying errors back and forth by hand.

Three things decide whether the loop ends well:

The fastest way to make a test pass is to change the test. An agent with no limits will sometimes do exactly that: loosen an assertion, skip the test, or delete it. Say what is off limits in every task:

That last line matters. Sometimes the test really is wrong, and you want to decide that, not the agent.

Mel's agent reads the rules files in your repository on every turn: AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md, then MEL.md for rules meant only for Mel. If AGENTS.md names a test command, Mel uses it as the check to run before it says it is done.

Write it once and every task in that project follows it, for you and for your teammates.

A hundred failing tests usually have a few causes. Don't ask for all of them at once.

For a big suite, a lead agent can split the work. Type /orchestrate-cli or /orchestrate-agent and Mel's lead gives each worker its own files, then checks the combined result and runs the tests on disk.

Name the exact command. These are the usual ones, from each tool's own docs; your project may wrap them in a script:

Running one test first is faster and cheaper: the agent sees a short output instead of a whole suite, and each run is quick. Ask it to run the single failing test while it works, then the whole suite once at the end.

An agent saying "the tests pass" is a claim. Mel treats it as one:

The same agent runs outside the app with mel agent, on the folder you run it in:

The answer streams to your terminal, and each command the agent runs or file it changes is printed on its own line. On macOS, set up the mel command once first: from v0.1.68, choose Install the mel command in the command palette. Approvals still apply; read the docs before you run it unattended.

Leave the model on auto and Mel picks one for each turn, or choose a mode: Cost-efficient for a simple assertion error, Genius for a hard bug that spans several files. If one model gets stuck, pick another from the model menu and send the same task; a fresh model often sees what the first one missed. On your own provider key, the turns cost 0 Mel credits.

Can an AI agent fix failing tests? Yes, when it can run them. Mel's agent runs your test command, reads the output, changes the code and runs the tests again until they pass or it reports what is left.

How do I stop the agent from deleting tests? Say so in the task and in AGENTS.md: "Fix the code, not the tests. Never skip or delete a test." Every edit also shows its diff on an approval card before it is written.

Where do I tell Mel my test command? In AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md or MEL.md in your repository. If AGENTS.md names a test command, Mel uses it as the check.

What if the agent can't fix it? A task that stops making progress ends with a report of what was done and what is left. Narrow the task, add a fact, or try another model from the model menu.

Can I undo the agent's changes? Yes. In the code review panel, Undo takes back one change and ↩ Discard reverts one file.

Should I run one test or the whole suite? One test while the agent works, the whole suite once at the end. It is faster and costs less.

Can I fix tests from CI with Mel? Yes, with mel agent "..." in the project folder. Read the approvals section of the mel agent docs first.

Is it free? The terminal is free, and Mel's agent is free with your own API key. Paid plans start at $20 a month.

Download Mel from openmel.dev, add one line with your test command to AGENTS.md, run the tests, and hand the red block to the agent.

Originally published on the Mel blog. pytest, Go, Rust, Cargo, Maven, npm and React are trademarks of their owners. Mel is not affiliated with or endorsed by them.

Call browser APIs from Blazor without writing JavaScript

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 24 • Originally published at spawndev.com

You are following a recipe on your phone. Your hands are covered in flour, and the screen goes dark.

Browsers have a fix for this, the Screen Wake Lock API. Blazor has no built-in wrapper for it, so the usual route is a JavaScript module, an IJSRuntime import, and a string name for every function you call. A typo in one of those strings shows up at run time.

This article does it in C# instead. We will build a small recipe page that keeps the screen on, takes the lock back when you return to the tab, and copies the ingredients to the clipboard. You need the .NET 10 SDK and a browser.

With plain Blazor interop you write a JavaScript file first:

Then you import it and call it by name. That is fine for one function. It gets harder when JavaScript needs to call you back, because you then also manage a DotNetObjectReference and its lifetime.

SpawnDev.SpawnJS wraps browser APIs as C# classes. Create a Blazor WebAssembly app and add two packages:

In Program.cs, add AddSpawnJSRuntime() and start the app with SpawnJSRunAsync():

The build may show warning CA1416 on AddSpawnJSRuntime(). It says the call needs a browser, which a Blazor WebAssembly app is, so you can ignore it.

The template comes with a page at /, Pages/Home.razor. Delete it and add your own, Pages/CookMode.razor. The next sections explain its main parts, and the whole file is at the end.

In JavaScript the call is navigator.wakeLock.request(). In C# it has the same shape:

Browsers release the lock when the tab is hidden, so switching apps to answer a message turns the feature off. To take it back, listen for the page becoming visible again:

Subscribing is += and unsubscribing is -=, like any C# event. Match every += with a -= before you dispose the object. Otherwise the browser can call into a component that no longer exists.

Clipboard access needs a secure context, so it works on https:// and localhost, and browsers may require that the call comes from a click.

You can call anything by name, including a function from a third-party script:

Argument types come first and the return type comes last. Get reads a property, and Call runs a method.

Run dotnet run in your project, or clone the finished one:

Already using SpawnDev.BlazorJS? It offers the same style of typed wrappers and stays supported. SpawnJS is the newer library, and it also runs outside Blazor. Both are MIT licensed, with all work done in public, so you can use them and fork them.

Will Crypto Recover? Is Bitcoin Hyper the Best Alternative to BTC and ETH Right Now?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Will crypto recover from this most recent crash? This is the question on every trader’s lips as the crypto market is once again in a sharp correction, with major tokens bleeding value and fear dominating sentiment.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, and other large-caps have taken heavy hits in November 2026, leaving investors wondering whether a rebound is on the horizon or this is the start of a deeper winter.

While history shows crypto’s resilience, the current environment raises tough questions. Amid the uncertainty, early-stage opportunities like Bitcoin Hyper (HYPER) are drawing attention as potential alternatives.

Bitcoin has been the clearest casualty. It broke below the key $60,000 support level, briefly testing lows near $55,000–$56,000, levels last seen in mid-to-late 2024, before partial stabilization around the low $60,000s.

The drop represents roughly a -35% decline from October peaks and more than 50% from the prior all-time high near $126,000, prompting investors to wonder, ‘Will crypto recover?’

Ethereum has suffered even steeper percentage losses in some areas, sliding below $1,700 and touching $1,500–$1,600, down nearly 40% from recent highs in some windows.

🚨 INSANE CRASHIn just 60 minutes, $1 TRILLION has been wiped from US stocks, while another $80 BILLION has been erased from crypto.Absolute bloodbath. pic.twitter.com/KA62DFyP5P

Solana, BNB, and XRP have followed with double-digit weekly declines, contributing to a broader market capitalization that has contracted toward the $2 trillion range.

Massive liquidations, hundreds of millions to over $2Bn in single periods, heavily skewed toward long positions, have amplified the downside.

Discover: The Best Token Presales Right Now

This has to be the worst bear market in crypto history.In 2018, we at least had a massive bull run where Alts pumped 500x-1000x.In 2022, we knew why our bags were dumping (Luna crash, FTX bankruptcy).This time, we had the worst bull run and are now dumping for absolutely…

No single event explains the sell-off. Instead, several pressures have aligned. Expectations of further Federal Reserve rate hikes, fueled by hotter-than-expected inflation data, have raised the opportunity cost of holding non-yielding assets like crypto. Spot Bitcoin and Ethereum ETFs have seen sustained outflows, removing a key source of institutional demand.

A broader risk-off move in equities, especially AI and tech names, has spilled over, given crypto’s elevated correlation with risk assets. Liquidation cascades, profit-taking after earlier gains, and residual caution from earlier events have compounded the pressure.

Recovery remains possible, though timing is uncertain. Crypto has repeatedly bounced from deep drawdowns when macro conditions improve, rates fall, ETF inflows resume, or positive catalysts emerge.

Bitcoin’s long-term narrative as a scarce digital store of value and Ethereum’s role in smart contracts and DeFi provide fundamental support. Analyst views currently diverge widely, with some eyeing further downside risk near prior lows and others looking for eventual higher targets once liquidity returns.

In the near term, holding key supports and a shift in Fed expectations or ETF flows would be constructive. Longer term, adoption trends and network development still favor resilience. That said, major-cap tokens often move together in risk-off regimes, limiting asymmetric upside for those seeking higher-beta opportunities.

While established tokens navigate this volatility, early-stage projects focused on real utility can offer a different risk-reward profile. Bitcoin Hyper (HYPER) stands out as a Bitcoin Layer-2 solution designed to bring speed, low fees, and programmability to the Bitcoin network without compromising its security.

The project uses the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM) for high-throughput execution while settling back to Bitcoin. A canonical bridge aims to let users move BTC onto the L2 for near-instant transactions, DeFi activity, smart contracts, and applications, and then withdraw seamlessly. HYPER serves as the gas and utility token, supporting fees, staking, and future governance.

With the presale raising over $30M, the project has advanced testnet tools, including explorers, wallets, faucets, and bridges. Critically, the HYPER token launch is scheduled for November 26, 2026, less than 48 hours away, with mainnet targeted shortly after on December 3. This timing positions participants ahead of potential listings and ecosystem growth as the network aims to unlock Bitcoin’s utility beyond simple value storage.

In a market where large-caps are correlating downward, infrastructure plays that expand Bitcoin’s capabilities can attract attention from builders and users seeking practical improvements. Early access via the final presale window offers exposure before broader trading begins.

Ella Langley’s ‘Choosin’ Texas’ Ties Hot Country Songs Record With 50 Weeks at No. 1

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The hit matches Bebe Rexha and Florida Georgia Line's "Meant to Be."

Ella Langley’s “Choosin’ Texas” logs a 50th week atop Billboard’s Hot Country Songs chart (dated Nov. 28), matching the all-time record set by Bebe Rexha and Florida Georgia Line’s “Meant to Be” in 2017-18.

“Choosin’ Texas” totaled 16.9 million official streams, 45.5 million in airplay audience and 6,000 sales in the United States Nov. 13-19, according to Luminate.

The song also extends its Billboard Hot 100 record to 29 weeks at No. 1.

Here’s a rundown of the five longest-leading Hot Country Songs hits (since the list became the genre’s singular songs chart in October 1958):

“Meant to Be” debuted atop Hot Country Songs in December 2017 and held the summit for 50 consecutive weeks. “Choosin’ Texas” reached No. 1 in its sixth week, in February, and led for 28 frames before Taylor Swift’s “I Knew It, I Knew You” interrupted its reign for two weeks in June. Langley reclaimed the summit on the July 4 chart and has remained there for 22 more weeks in a row.

The shared milestone also links two distinct moments in country’s crossover history. “Meant to Be” emerged during a sonically experimental boom, pairing pop star Rexha with FGL, a country cornerstone act in the 2010s, as genre-blurring hits helped define the era. The collaboration reached No. 2 on the Hot 100.

“Choosin’ Texas,” meanwhile, has contributed to country’s prominence this decade.

Streaming and radio airplay at country, pop and adult formats helped sustain both hits’ extraordinary longevity. “Meant to Be” spent 55 weeks atop Country Streaming Songs, with “Choosin’ Texas” up to 44. Their commands on Country Airplay were one and three weeks, respectively (reflecting radio’s more focused programming cycles).

Both songs also hit the top 10 on Pop Airplay, Adult Pop Airplay and Adult Contemporary, and sold strongly. “Meant to Be” reached No. 2 on Digital Song Sales over a yearlong stay on the chart; “Choosin’ Texas” has led for a record 22 weeks and counting.

Check Billboard.com next Monday, Nov. 30, to find out if “Choosin’ Texas” can pass “Meant to Be” and give Langley sole possession of the Hot Country Songs No. 1 longevity record.

Bitcoin sell-off driven by weak demand, forced liquidations: 21Shares

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The crypto asset manager warned that Bitcoin’s downturn could persist amid weak investor demand, while forecasting an uneven recovery across crypto sectors.

The $6.4 billion hack of cryptocurrency exchange Binance in October triggered Bitcoin’s recent sell-off, but weakening investor demand and forced liquidations drove most of the subsequent losses, according to a Nov. 24 report from crypto asset manager 21Shares.

While the Oct. 29 hack triggered the initial decline, the report found that weakening demand drove Bitcoin (BTC) down a further 8.3% between Nov. 4 and Nov. 11. Forced selling of leveraged positions led to the steepest decline, sending BTC down another 13.5% over the next three days. Crypto liquidations totaled $5.8 billion in the week ending Nov. 15, with long positions accounting for 81%.

Bitcoin’s sell-off and leverage unwind. Source: 21Shares

Signs of weakening demand were also seen in the stablecoin supply, which contracted by $10.7 billion following the hack, marking the largest 18-day decline in dollar terms since the Terra collapse in 2022. US spot Bitcoin and Ether (ETH) exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded combined outflows of $3.9 billion between Oct. 30 and Nov. 13.

Eliezer Ndinga, global head of research at 21Shares, told Cointelegraph that the market was already vulnerable before the hack:

The report also found little evidence of selling by other major Bitcoin holders. The 18,270 BTC and 522,409 ETH stolen in the Binance hack remained unspent as of mid-November, while neither the US government nor the Mt. Gox trustee had transferred Bitcoin to exchanges since the attack.

Bitcoin treasury firm Strategy continued buying, adding 2,332 BTC between Oct. 26 and Nov. 15. Meanwhile, miner transfers to exchanges fell to their lowest levels since at least 2020, although the data may not capture sales by publicly traded mining companies.

Related: Bitcoin slides amid US tariff nerves as Anthropic falls below IPO price

21Shares believes Bitcoin is already more than a year into a cyclical bear market. Historically, such downturns have lasted about two and a half years and resulted in average price declines of 84%, according to the report.

However, the researchers see a low probability of Bitcoin revisiting the depths of previous bear markets, citing the growing presence of institutional investors and spot ETFs. Ndinga said the competitive landscape for crypto has also changed:

Ndinga warned that a larger-than-expected Federal Reserve rate hike in December could prolong the downturn. Another potential source of selling pressure is the $2.2 billion in BTC and ETH stolen in the Binance hack, which could reach the market through over-the-counter (OTC) brokers in the coming months.

Ndinga identified Bitcoin’s realized price of $53,250, a measure of the average investor cost basis, as a key downside level to watch. Conversely, a return to the $73,000 to $75,500 range coupled with sustained ETF inflows and rising stablecoin balances would signal renewed investor demand.

Stablecoin supply changes following major market shocks. Source: 21Shares

Will Congress End the Iran War?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Click below to continue reading this story, discover more Foreign Policy & National Security stories from over 25 years of RealClear archives, or follow this topic to stay in the know.

A surprising bipartisan deal to trade other military funding for a quick wind-down of the war is actually in the works....

The United States need not wait for a nuclear attack before acting against a grave danger. But it cannot ask Americans to support an open-ended war on the strength of a danger alone. After more than eight months of conflict with Iran, the Trump administration owes the country a public account of what its campaign has achieved, what remains beyond its ability to verify, and what further fighting would accomplish. The case for preventing an Iranian bomb is strong. The case for continuing this war depends on whether continued force can achieve that end.
