Compass Minerals (CMP) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pricing gains and a sharp Plant Nutrition margin rebound powered the turnaround.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:41PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Compass Minerals (CMP +16.72%) reported a return to net income for fiscal 2026, driven by improved production efficiency and pricing discipline. Management stated that the company implemented a back-to-basics framework to reduce expenses and strengthen core mining operations. The company reported margin expansion in the Plant Nutrition segment and reduced net leverage through debt repayment. Management indicated that priorities for fiscal 2027 include asset utilization and converting increased highway salt pricing into higher margins.

Operator: Hello, everyone. Thank you for joining us, and welcome to the Compass Minerals Fiscal Fourth Quarter and Full Year 2026 Earnings Call. [Operator Instructions] I will now hand the conference over to Tripp Sullivan, Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Tripp Sullivan: Thank you, operator. Good morning, and welcome to the Compass Minerals Fiscal Fourth Quarter and Full Year 2026 Earnings Conference Call. Today, we will discuss our results for the quarter and full year and our outlook for fiscal 2027. We will begin with prepared remarks from our President and CEO, Edward C. Dowling Jr.; followed by Benjamin (Ben) Nichols, our Chief Commercial Officer; and our CFO, Peter Fjellman. All 3 will be available for the question-and-answer portion of the call. Before we get started, I will remind everyone that the remarks we make today reflect financial and operational outlooks as of today's date, November 24, 2026.

These outlooks entail assumptions and expectations that involve risks and uncertainties that could cause the company's actual results to differ materially. A discussion of these risks can be found in our SEC filings located online at investors.compassminerals.com. Our remarks today also include certain non-GAAP financial measures. You can find reconciliations of these items in our earnings release or in our presentation, both of which are also available online. And with that, I will now turn the call over to Ed.

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Thank you, Tripp. Good morning, everyone. Thank you for joining us. Almost 3 years ago, we laid out a back-to-basics framework for the company. Its objective was to employ and strengthen core capabilities to drive improved operational and financial performance. We have delivered on that framework, and the numbers show it. Since fiscal 2024, we've gone from a net loss of $206 million to a net income of $17.5 million, taken more than 20% out of SG&A, more than tripled Plant Nutrition adjusted EBITDA. In the last year alone, net leverage came down from 3.9 to 3.2x. These gains are built to last. Our work is now about controlled, predictable costs and better efficiency across every part of the business.

We're a better company and have built capability for the future. You can see that work across the company. Within Salt at Goderich, mine productivity has improved after we took the necessary steps. In addition, as we discussed last quarter, production unit costs are continuing to come down. Our maintenance programs are delivering on improved equipment availability and better utilization, which improves asset efficiency. In our supply chain, we continue to optimize our distribution network and tighten how we manage MRO site-level spend to support maintenance and improve productivity. And across procurement and SG&A, our spending is moving in the right direction as we invest in people, processes and systems that support the business.

The result is a more resilient, more capable business. This work shows up in our results. For the second consecutive year, we did what we said we would do. We carry that momentum into 2027. Fiscal 2026 adjusted EBITDA was up 18% from fiscal 2025 and above both initial and final midpoints of our guidance range. Excluding a noncash Fortress gain in the prior year, we were up 23%. We finished the year with a strong fourth quarter, delivering a 5% year-over-year increase in adjusted EBITDA. Peter will take you through the details shortly. We also delivered on safety, which comes first in everything we do.

Our total recordable injury rate was 0.87 for the year, well ahead of our target and a world-class number. Our Goderich mine surpassed 1 million man-hours worked without a recordable injury. Our key safety metrics are about 50% better year-on-year. This underpins the culture and performance that we're building in the company. I want to thank our employees for this wonderful achievement. For the last 3 quarters, you've heard us talk about the performance expectation at Goderich mine and the effect it has had on our results and anticipated trajectory. That situation has improved considerably. As you can see in our 2027 guidance, the P&L now reflects multiyear production cost improvement we told you was coming. Here's what's changed.

We entered the 2027 year running at full production with higher utilization, stronger fundamentals at the mining face. At both Goderich and Cote Blanche, better utilization and labor efficiency are spreading our fixed costs over more tons, and the production cost per ton is coming down as last year's investments into maintenance and labor pay off. You will recall that in August, we made a change in operational leadership to promote Brandon Risner to Chief Operating Officer. Brandon and his team are bringing real discipline to the operations, and I'm confident with the direction. As an example, we are continuing to optimize the Goderich mill project.

Rather than advancing the earlier, more complicated designs, we took a step back and restarted from first principles. That removes complexity and improves capital efficiency of the project, giving a simplified facility fit for purpose instead of a replica of the past. We expect to share more details on our Goderich plans later in fiscal 2027. Goderich had a strong August and a recent record in September, a strong October and a good start to November. November 16, we had a ground fall at the mine. This occurred in a conveyor entry, not a personnel passageway, and no one was hurt.

The team at Goderich displayed our commitment to discipline and safety of properly actioning early detection from our ground monitoring system, ensuring that the area was isolated prior to the fall. Falls of ground happen from time to time in the normal course of underground mining. It damaged some production infrastructure, which did interrupt production for a few days. Brandon was immediately on site and coordinates the work with the team there. We're back up and running, albeit at a reduced rate. The team is well underway with the remaining work within the affected area. Our fiscal 2027 guidance includes an allowance for operational variability. We'll update you on the progress as appropriate. Plant Nutrition deserves special mention today.

In 2024, we laid out our plan to improve this business. The segment has gone from $17 million adjusted EBITDA in fiscal 2024 to $54.8 million in fiscal 2026, more than 3x higher. And segment adjusted EBITDA margin expanded by 950 basis points in the last year alone. This improvement came from price and operating cost working together. It's the clearest example in our company of what this team is doing with commercial and operations executing as one. The next step is already underway. The Ogden dryer and compaction project completes in fiscal 2027, adding capacity, lowering cost, improving product quality. We're excited about what that means for this business in 2028 and beyond.

With that, I'll turn it over to Ben to walk through the bid season.

Benjamin (Ben) Nichols: Thank you, Ed. Good morning, everyone. As a reminder, we went into the 2026, '27 North American highway bid season with a clear objective: expand margins through both price and customer mix. I'm pleased to say we achieved what we set out to do. On price, the results were well above what we typically see in a year. When we roll up the regional mix of customers in our fiscal 2027 plan and compare it with fiscal 2026, North American highway pricing is up approximately 12%. Our U.S. markets saw increases in the mid-teens, with some regions well above that, while Canada, where more of our business sits in multiyear contracts, came in at mid- to high single digits.

Price is only one part of our expanding margins. We also improved the quality of our commercial contracts. Guaranteed terms rose to 78% of our committed U.S. portfolio, up from 65% a year ago. That gives us a strong level of sales demand confidence heading into the winter and better predictability whatever the weather brings. We gained share in key markets such as Wisconsin and Michigan, where we have strong margins, and we chose to step back in areas where the economics or terms did not meet our standards. Our focus is to enable our contracts to expand margins regardless of the winter weather. That brings me to volume.

The strong 2025, '26 winter drew down inventories across the entire industry, and market tender sizes this season are up roughly 5% year-over-year. Our assets are running at full production, and we sized our commitments to the salt we will have available. As a result, and as previously discussed, our fiscal 2027 sales volumes are expected to be lower than this past year, and Peter will give you the range. Market demand is strong. While our 2027 sales volumes are supply constrained in the short term, the improvements underway at our assets give us a path to rebuild inventory for the 2028 season and manage growth of volumes over time. Finally, fuel.

Like most industrial companies, we are feeling the impact of elevated fuel costs. Our exposure runs across every mode we use to move product and, to a lesser degree, through our operations, most notably at Ogden. Because we manage many different fuel programs across North America, we use Brent crude as our proxy. Our fiscal 2027 guidance assumes Brent at $105 per barrel through the first half of the fiscal year, moving to $90 per barrel through the second half. It is important to note that timing and the mix within our business matter, with our greatest exposure falling in the first half during the heavier winter shipping season.

We continue to work with both our direct customers and our logistics partners to reduce long-term fuel exposure to our P&L. Despite the inflationary environment, our highway margins are expanding, and that is the result we are focused on. Peter?

Peter Fjellman: Thanks, Ben. Good morning, everyone. I'll start with our full year results and then walk through our fiscal 2027 outlook. All comparisons are to the prior year unless otherwise noted. Before I walk through the details, I want to put fiscal 2026 in context. Fiscal 2026 was a year of delivery. We returned to profitability with net income of $17.5 million, and adjusted EBITDA grew 18% to $235 million, or 23% excluding last year's noncash Fortress gain. We lowered net leverage from 3.9 to 3.2x after redeeming the remaining $150 million of our 2027 notes. Adjusted EBITDA has grown from $199 million in fiscal 2025 to $235 million in fiscal 2026.

And the midpoint of our 2027 outlook takes that to $270 million, an increase of 36% over 2 years, or about 17% compounded annually. On a per-ton basis, Salt adjusted EBITDA rose from $20.21 to $21.72 in fiscal 2026. And our 2027 guidance implies about $28 at the midpoint, up about 29%. For the full year, revenue was up 6%. Net income improved nearly $100 million, reversing a prior year loss. Adjusted earnings per diluted share was $0.76 compared with a loss of $0.48 in the prior year. Adjusted EBITDA was up 18% and above the midpoint of our guidance ranges we increased throughout the year. The improvement runs through the P&L.

Gross profit rose 17%, SG&A declined 6% and adjusted operating margin expanded 190 basis points to 8.8%. We finished strong, with the fourth quarter adjusted EBITDA growing 5%. The quarter benefited as customers moved to build inventory, along with our continued pricing gains and cost control. In Salt, full year revenue was $1.09 billion, and segment adjusted EBITDA was up 8% and higher than guidance. Adjusted EBITDA per ton rose 7%. Highway pricing was up about 8%, and C&I pricing was up about 2%. In Plant Nutrition, full year segment adjusted EBITDA was up 57% on higher pricing and lower per-unit costs at Ogden, and segment adjusted EBITDA margin expanded from 16.9% to 26.4%.

Excluding the contribution from the Wynyard asset sold in March, the segment's fiscal 2026 adjusted EBITDA was approximately $51.9 million, which gives us an apples-to-apples comparison to the 2027 outlook. Turning to the balance sheet. During the year, we redeemed the remaining $150 million of our 2027 notes early. And as of September 30, total liquidity was $247.2 million, and net debt was $748.6 million. Operating cash flow for the year was $108.1 million, and capital expenditures were $91.5 million. Operating cash flow was below fiscal 2025, which benefited from the $87 million onetime inventory reduction.

This year, we also absorbed the settlement of the previously disclosed Ontario tax dispute, with a final cash payment of $24.8 million in November 2025. Cash from operations, along with the $23.3 million of the proceeds of the Wynyard sale, was reinvested in the operations and towards reducing debt, which is consistent with our stated priorities. Now to our fiscal 2027 outlook. We expect total company adjusted EBITDA midpoint of $270 million, which represents approximately 15% growth over fiscal 2026. We expect revenue of $1.2 billion to $1.28 billion, and the headline is that we expect to sell about 12% fewer Salt tons and still grow adjusted EBITDA about 14% at the midpoint.

As we discussed last quarter, revenue reflects selling fewer tons due to lower inventory at the start of the season, but we expect to earn meaningfully more on each ton. At the midpoint, more than 29% adjusted EBITDA per ton, with Salt adjusted EBITDA margin up about 400 basis points to 25.6%. In fiscal 2027, we are focused on higher inventory by year-end to facilitate stronger 2028 volumes. By segment, we expect a Salt adjusted EBITDA midpoint of $268 million. Our guidance assumes a highway sales volume midpoint of 7.7 million tons and a C&I sales volume midpoint of 1.85 million tons for a total Salt sales volume midpoint of 9.55 million.

Now let me address a question many of you asked last quarter: How can production costs be improving when reported product cost per ton sold rose 2.6% to $50.42 in fiscal 2026? The answer is timing. Production cost is what it costs us to mine a ton today. Reported cost reflects the tons we sell, and those can come out of inventory produced earlier at higher costs, along with a shift in the mix of products and sites and regions we sell into. With inventories now low, most of those higher-cost tons have flowed through. Our fiscal 2027 outlook reflects the lower production costs we are now achieving at our largest assets.

Turning to the Salt segment cost walk in our supplemental presentation. On a sold basis, Salt product cost per ton was $50.42 in fiscal 2026, and our fiscal 2027 guidance takes that to about $49.90. The largest driver is the operational improvement Ed described. Higher utilization and cost efficiency across all our production assets, led by Goderich, takes out roughly $4.90 per ton. That improvement is partially offset by factors outside the mine gate. Sales mix adds about $2.30 per ton as fewer highway tons shift our mix towards products with higher cost per ton. The U.K. inventory reduction adds about $1.40 per ton. Other costs impact about $0.70 per ton, primarily due to fuel and warehouse inflation.

Put together, the cost improvement at our assets more than covers these headwinds, so we lower our product cost per ton sold even on fewer tons. It's also important to note that both the lower highway mix and the U.K. inventory reduction are temporary. As we build inventory and our highway volumes recover, those headwinds should reverse, and even more of our production cost improvement should show up in the reported cost per ton. For the Salt adjusted EBITDA per-ton bridge from fiscal 2026 to our fiscal 2027 midpoint in our supplemental presentation, which excludes depreciation, we start at $21.72 per ton in fiscal 2026. Price and mix add about $9 from the highway bid season results Ben described.

Lower product costs add about $2.10. At Goderich, our largest salt asset, production cost per ton was down about 7.5% last year and projected to have similar improvements this year, and that benefits both our highway and C&I products. The U.K. is the exception. After 2 soft winters, we are managing down inventory, which is moderating some of the production cost per-ton improvement. Logistics takes away about $4.20, largely fuel, consistent with the Brent assumptions Ben outlined. SG&A continues to improve, but takes away about $0.50 per ton as we spread those costs over fewer tons.

This brings adjusted EBITDA per ton to about $28.10 per ton at the midpoint, up more than 29%, and expands the Salt adjusted EBITDA margin by about 400 basis points to 25.6%. The Plant Nutrition midpoint of $54 million is roughly in line with the $54.8 million we reported in fiscal 2026 and above the $51.9 million we reported excluding Wynyard. We are planning for modestly higher pricing, holding SOP's premium to MOP, with marginally higher input costs on a sales volume midpoint of about 280,000 tons. The dryer and compaction project Ed described is primarily a fiscal 2028 benefit. The midpoint of our corporate and other costs at $52 million is down from fiscal 2026 on cost controls.

Our interest expense midpoint of $62 million is down from $66.1 million in fiscal 2026. The 26% to 30% effective tax range excludes the effect of the valuation allowance on U.S. deferred assets. Including it, the effective tax rate may be higher depending on the level of U.S. income or loss. On capital, our approach is simple: we invest in the business first. We expect fiscal 2027 capital expenditures of $105 million to $125 million, up from $91.5 million in fiscal 2026. That includes growth and cost improvement projects such as the Ogden dryer and compaction project, a new bagging line at Lyons and groundwork at Goderich that supports more efficient mining under any mill design.

The rest is sustaining capital to keep our core infrastructure reliable, including the Cote Blanche headframe. Beyond reinvesting in the business, our priority for free cash flow remains reducing debt and continuing to lower leverage while staying prudent about the inherent variability of our end markets. In closing, we enter 2027 with real momentum: highway pricing up about 12%, guaranteed terms up to about 78% of our committed U.S. portfolio from 65%, lower production costs at our largest asset and a Plant Nutrition business ready for its next step. Our outlook calls for approximately 15% adjusted EBITDA growth at that midpoint from a business that is earning more money on every ton and carrying less debt. Compass Minerals is stronger.

It's a more resilient company than it was years ago, and we're staying disciplined for the road ahead. That concludes our prepared remarks. Operator, we're ready for some questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Your first question comes from the line of Joel Jackson with BMO Capital Markets.

Joel Jackson: I wanted to -- we've seen what's happened with the Morton mine in Ohio, Fairport. Can you talk about if that mine is out for a little while, a few months, is there any way you can wring out more tons this next few months, even if it's a higher cost, to help meet some demand? Let's say that, that mine is out for 2, 3 years or it's gone forever, unfortunately, are there things you can do to help service that demand, and how would that impact your view of the industry?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: We're not really going to comment on what's going on at Fairport. They produce about 1.4 million tons a year kind of historically. We'll focus on Goderich. The good news here is Goderich is the world's largest underground salt mine. We've been proving that scale and the benefits of that recently. You can see in our notes that in preparing for 2028 and beyond, we are building inventory to be able to serve whatever the market looks like at that time. And as you know, we go through a pretty sophisticated approach to that. And we have a high degree of confidence in that market and now our stability of the operations.

Could we take advantage of that during fiscal year 2027? Well, we could perhaps reduce some of our inventory build that we have planned, for example, to take advantage of that. We're just going to have to see how it plays out. I think we'll update you as the year progresses in terms of what that market looks like.

Joel Jackson: Okay. Can we talk about free cash flow? So you've got EBITDA up on your own midpoint-to-midpoint, kind of $35 million this year in '27. CapEx is up on average midpoint about $25 million. Can you talk about what we should expect for free cash flow from Compass if all your guidance hits all the midpoints?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Yes. Let me just have Peter answer that question here for us.

Peter Fjellman: Yes. From a similar cash flow really to 2026, what we'll find is with the 2 items adjustments in there, Joel, it would be roughly about the same from about a $30 million range from an operating adjusted free cash flow.

Joel Jackson: Okay. And just one more for me, if you don't mind. We all read the news and we all see El Nino, and I live in Toronto, it's been a very warm November. Can you talk about what your guidance for volumes and EBITDA, like what does it bake in for different scenarios around the winter? Like what's your base case? I assume your base case is 100% of contracted volume goes out the door, but can you give some sensitivities if the winter was 60%, 70%, 80% of normal?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Yes. The answer to that is yes. We do our targeting in terms of our base production using, as I said, a pretty sophisticated approach to it with a lot of input factors and statistical analysis of that, looking at history and demand take-up and all those sort of things. So that does include provisions for, you might say, a weaker winter. That's why our guidance has a range on it. In the end, it's where it ends up snowing. We can think of years, last year at this time, and we're reporting early, it really hadn't snowed yet and was just sort of getting going in early December.

And so we'll just see how it plays out, but we're prepared for that. I think, importantly, one of the things that Ben and his team have been able to do this year is, in addition to expanding margins and through price, that side of it, it's been to get more of our contracted volumes guaranteed. That number is up considerably to about 78% or a little less than 80% from some number in the mid-60s. So those tons are going to be taken up no matter what. That sort of underpins our business. Ben, anything else you want to add to that?

Benjamin (Ben) Nichols: No, I think you're right, Ed. Joel, you're thinking about our approach correctly. We use our level of commitments through the bid cycle as the baseline, and then our guidance and range incorporates kind of statistical winter variability.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of David Begleiter with Deutsche Bank.

David Begleiter: Ed, on that guaranteed portion of the portfolio, what's driving the increase in '27 versus '26? And would you like it to be higher in the future? Would you like it to be fully guaranteed, the portfolio, or would you like some variability in that number going forward?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Well, recall in the past, these contracts are all a little bit different with the municipalities and states that we deal with. In the past, these numbers, kind of a rough approach, our customers were committed to taking about 80% of the committed volumes come hell or high water, and we would have to serve that to kind of 120% if you have sort of a big winter. And we've seen all that behavior over the years. That is an inherent risk that we manage in the company. Given the market conditions now, with a much tighter management of inventory here and presumably elsewhere, you might say the negotiation strength is more with the suppliers at this point in time.

And Ben and his team have taken advantage of that to really derisk our business for this term. We'll see how it plays out in the future, but one thing we do know is that we've proven that active inventory management is a key to really a successful business, and we're going to continue doing that.

David Begleiter: That was very helpful. And just on your Salt volume guidance for the year, highway deicing, you're down roughly 1 million tons, maybe a little bit more. How do you get that share back next year? Do you have to use price or other mechanisms to gain that share back?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Yes. Well, look, that's sold tons, the assumption that you have. Our production tons are higher than that. As I said, we're building some level of inventory, and the good news is we've got the balance sheet to do that now. And so -- but for us, it's always going to be value over volume. And so the bidding is competitive, but we're going to continue to press margin in this company. Peter?

Peter Fjellman: Yes, just to add to that, when we talked about free cash flow before, one of the assumptions we have in there is obviously a build in inventory for the 2028 cycle. Our assets are able to produce a lot more tons, as Ed said, and we're preparing to have a stronger balance sheet, including more inventory, to put us in a position for a competitive 2028.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Jeffrey Zekauskas with JPMorgan.

Jeffrey Zekauskas: If I remember correctly, the cost of a new mill was maybe $47 million back in 2021. I imagine that those numbers are higher now, maybe they're $60 million or $70 million. Is that a fair appraisal? And will that spending begin in 2028?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Well, I don't know where that number came from, first of all. Let me just say that.

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Yes, okay. Yes, it was years before I was involved with the company, so. Anyway, what we're doing is we've taken a step back to really look at the right way to build this mill and rather than replicating potentially what we have in place now. There's some reasons for that. And so the design that we have in terms of the conceptual design is much simpler and would be lower cost than what we had in mind. We'll guide on that as we develop that later this year. But there is work been going on for years to do this.

Recall the East Mine Drive, which accesses that part of the mine to the shaft infrastructure, we should break through on that here in days is what we're talking about. The 3B108 project, which drives out from the shaft infrastructure through an area of the mine to tie in to the East Main Drive, that project is really just beginning this week. In addition, we're doing earthworks and groundworks at the site where the mill is being built. That's basically putting in roof control and preparing kind of the earthworks and things like that for a new mill going into that area. And we're doing the engineering work for the new mill design.

So there's a lot of work actually going on around that. It's not like we're just starting here. It's been going on for a long time. So what we're just talking about is really more of our taking a step back and looking at this from first principles, giving us a really a more fit-for-purpose design for that mill.

Jeffrey Zekauskas: Does your Shaft #3 need to be relined over the next, I don't know, 3 to 5 years?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: No, there's nothing in our capital plan for that.

Jeffrey Zekauskas: Okay. In the December quarter, is it easy or difficult to appraise what you think your road salt volumes might be, or it really will hang on December? Last year, you had a very good first quarter. I don't know, are you thinking as a base case maybe you do 2.1 million tons of road salt in the first quarter?

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Yes, every year is a bit different in kind of when and where the snow shows up. Last year, after kind of a slow start, December was huge. And -- but then second quarter got light. Year before, it was all quiet early, and then the second quarter was big. So every year is a bit different with that. And boy, I tell you what, if I could predict that on my crystal ball, I probably wouldn't be a mining engineer. So anyway, what we do is we plan using the best information we have and some sophisticated statistical analysis to preposition our material where it's most likely to generate the margin.

Operator: There appear to be no further questions. I will now turn the call back to Ed Dowling for closing remarks.

Edward C. Dowling Jr.: Okay. Well, thank you for your interest in the company. 2026 was another improved year for us. We did what we said we were going to do. The Plant Nutrition turnaround project has succeeded, and the profitability that we said we were going to restore in 2024 is in place. PennantPark (PFLT) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Core income covered the dividend while the second JV nears its target.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:41PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

PennantPark (PFLT -1.36%) reported core net investment income that supported its base dividend and allowed for the declaration of a supplemental distribution. Management stated that the company is prioritizing the expansion of its second senior secured loan joint venture toward a $1 billion target while maintaining a portfolio heavily weighted toward first lien floating rate debt. The company reported a significant concentration in the government services and defense sectors, citing durable federal funding priorities and resilient cash flow profiles as key investment drivers. Management indicated that the core middle market remains a source of attractive risk-adjusted opportunities despite uneven M&A activity and ongoing macroeconomic uncertainty.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to the PennantPark Floating Rate Capital's Fourth Fiscal Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. Today's conference is being recorded. [Operator Instructions] It is now my pleasure to turn the call over to Mr. Art Penn, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of PennantPark Floating Rate Capital. Mr. Penn, you may begin your conference.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thank you, and good morning, everyone. Welcome to PennantPark Floating Rate Capital's Fourth Fiscal Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. I'm joined today by Rick Allorto, our Chief Financial Officer. Rick, please start off by disclosing some general conference call information and include a discussion about forward-looking statements.

Richard (Rick) Allorto: Thank you, Art. I'd like to remind everyone that today's call is being recorded and is the property of PennantPark Floating Rate Capital. Any unauthorized broadcast of this call in any form is strictly prohibited. An audio replay of the call will be available on our website. I'd also like to call your attention to the customary safe harbor disclosure in our press release regarding forward-looking information. Our remarks today may also include forward-looking statements and projections. Please refer to our most recent SEC filings for important factors that could cause actual results to differ materially from these projections. We do not undertake to update our forward-looking statements unless required by law.

To obtain copies of our latest SEC filings, please visit our website at pennantpark.com or call us at (212) 905-1000. At this time, I'd like to turn the call back to our Chairman and Chief Executive Officer, Art Penn.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thanks, Rick. I'll begin with an overview of our fourth quarter results, including the continued expansion of our PSSL II joint venture. I'll then discuss the current market environment and how we believe PFLT is positioned going forward. Rick will follow up with a detailed review of our financial results, after which we will open up the call for questions. For the quarter ended September 30, core net investment income was $0.25 per share. This exceeded the current base dividend of $0.08 per share per month or $0.24 per share for the quarter. In accordance with our revised dividend policy, PFLT will pay an aggregate supplemental dividend of $0.005 a share over the next 3 months.

The supplemental dividend represents 50% of the excess of the net investment income above the base dividend. As of September 30, our NAV per share was $10.15, which is down approximately 1% from the prior quarter. Despite the ongoing macroeconomic and geopolitical environment, our portfolio remains conservatively positioned with a median debt-to-EBITDA of 4.5x, interest coverage of 2.2x and loan-to-value of 43%. Additionally, PIK, pay-in-kind income remained among the lowest in the industry and equaled just 1.3% of total interest income for the quarter. As of September 30, our portfolio totaled $2.4 billion. And during the quarter, we invested $222 million in both new and existing investments at a weighted average yield of 9.6%.

We invested $116 million into 5 new portfolio companies with a median debt-to-EBITDA of 4.8x, interest coverage of 2.2x and a loan-to-value of 36%. Our existing portfolio continues to drive capital deployment. During the quarter, we invested additional capital totaling $106 million into existing portfolio companies. 1.8% of the portfolio is on nonaccrual at cost and 0.7% at market value. Out of 159 companies, 7 are on nonaccrual. On an overall basis, the percentage of our portfolio on nonaccrual remains low, and we believe this reflects the consistency of our underwriting process and our disciplined approach to credit selection. Our 2 joint ventures remain an important component of our investment strategy.

As of September 30, the PSSL portfolio totaled $1.1 billion, and that joint venture continues to reinvest repayment proceeds into new investments. The PSSL portfolio is performing well and for the quarter ended September 30, generated an annualized cash yield on invested capital of 11.5%. As of September 30, the PSSL II portfolio totaled $386 million, and we remain focused on scaling that JV in a measured and disciplined manner. Since quarter end, PSSL II invested an additional $40 million, increasing the total portfolio to $426 million. The PSSL II portfolio has grown 33% since the June quarter, and we are pleased with its income generation.

Over time, we expect to grow the JV to more than $1 billion in assets, consistent with PSSL. Based on the current conditions, we expect this expansion to occur over the next 12 to 18 months while maintaining our disciplined underwriting standards. For the quarter ended September 30, PSSL II generated an annualized cash yield on invested capital of 11.8%. Turning to portfolio positioning. I'd like to highlight our government services and defense investments. Government services and defense remains one of our highest conviction sectors and has historically been among our best-performing investment areas. We remain highly constructive on the long-term outlook for government services and defense because the sector possesses several characteristics that align well with our investment philosophy.

Demand has historically been supported by durable federal funding priorities and long-term contracts that provide meaningful revenue visibility and stability. Many of these businesses exhibit resilient cash flow profiles, variable cost structures and are generally less sensitive to broader economic cycles than many commercial industries. In addition, the sector continues to benefit from active M&A markets and strong valuation support, thereby providing multiple avenues for value creation. One example of this is Aechelon Investco. Through our equity co-investment in Aechelon Investco, we have an ongoing equity investment in a fast-growing defense tech company, Shield AI.

Shield AI recently announced an acquisition and a capital raise at an indicative valuation for our investment of $7 million above the value on September 30 of $5.5 million. This increase equates to $0.07 per share. Within the government services and defense industry, our investments are focused on businesses supporting the Department of War and other mission-critical government agencies. We focus on companies addressing high-priority national security initiatives, including modernization of defense systems and digital infrastructure, cyber and electronic warfare capabilities, modeling and simulation, counter drone technologies and next-generation autonomous systems. We believe these priorities will remain central to U.S. defense spending for years to come, creating a favorable backdrop for continued investment opportunities.

As of September 30, government services and defense represents approximately 18% of PFLT's portfolio. And given our experience of sourcing capabilities and the attractive opportunity set, we intend to maintain or increase that exposure over time. Turning to software. Our exposure represents approximately 4.1% of the portfolio. These investments follow our core middle market strategy and consist of cash pay loans with financial covenants, moderate leverage and relatively short durations. The businesses provide mission-critical enterprise software to regulated industries, including defense, health care and financial services. Let me now turn to the broader market environment. M&A activity has increased over the past 6 to 9 months, although overall conditions remain uneven.

Private equity sponsors remain active, and we're seeing a growing pipeline of attractive opportunities across both new originations and add-on investments. We're optimistic that activity levels will remain elevated throughout the remainder of this year and into next year. We expect increased transaction activity to drive repayments across the portfolio, including opportunities to monetize equity co-investments and redeploy capital into income-generating investments. In the core middle market, the pricing for high-quality first lien term loans remains attractive, typically ranging from SOFR plus 500 to 550 basis points with leverage of approximately 4.5x EBITDA. Importantly, these structures continue to include meaningful covenant protections in contrast to the covenant-light structures prevalent in the upper middle market.

We believe the current environment favors lenders with established private equity sponsor relationships, consistent access to deal flow and disciplined underwriting. These are long-standing strengths of the PennantPark platform. We continue to believe that the core middle market offers attractive risk-adjusted opportunities. Companies in this segment generally have EBITDA of $10 million to $50 million and often operate below the practical threshold of the broadly syndicated loan and high-yield markets. As a result, lenders can typically conduct extensive diligence, negotiate meaningful financial covenants, structure transactions with appropriate leverage and equity cushions and maintain regular access to company financial information. Our credit quality since our inception over 15 years ago has been excellent.

PFLT has invested $9.5 billion in over 550 companies, and we've experienced only 27 nonaccruals. Since inception, PFLT's loss ratio on invested capital is only 13 basis points annually. As a provider of strategic capital, it fuels the growth of our portfolio companies, in many cases, we participate in the upside of the company by making an equity co-investment. Our returns on these equity co-investments have been excellent over time. Overall, for our platform from inception through September 30, we've invested over $635 million into equity co-investments and have generated an IRR of 25% and a multiple on invested capital of 2x. Looking ahead, our experienced team and broad origination platform position us well to generate attractive deal flow.

Our mission remains consistent, which is to deliver a stable and well-covered dividend while preserving capital. Everything we do is aligned to that objective. We continue to focus on investing in high-quality middle market companies that generate strong free cash flow. We capture that value through first lien secured loans, and we pay out those contractual cash flows in the form of dividends to our shareholders. With that overview, I'll turn it over to Rick for a more detailed review of our financial results.

Richard (Rick) Allorto: Thank you, Art. For the quarter ended September 30, GAAP and core net investment income was $0.25 per share. Investment income was comprised of $58 million in interest income, $5.9 million in dividend income and $0.8 million in other income. Our operating expenses for the quarter were as follows: interest and expenses on debt were $24.8 million, base management and performance-based incentive fees were $12.5 million. General and administrative expenses were $2.2 million and provision for taxes was less than $0.1 million. Net realized and unrealized change on investments, including provision for taxes, was a loss of $10.5 million for the quarter. As of September 30, NAV was $10.15 per share compared to $10.26 per share last quarter.

At quarter end, our debt-to-equity ratio was 1.5x, and our capital structure is diversified across multiple funding sources, including both secured and unsecured debt. As of September 30, our key portfolio statistics were as follows: the portfolio remains well diversified, comprising 159 companies across 32 industries. The weighted average yield on our debt investments was 10.2% and approximately 99% of the debt portfolio is floating rate. PIK income equaled only 1.3% of total interest income. The portfolio is comprised of 89% first lien senior secured debt, less than 1% in second lien and subordinated debt, 3% in equity of PSSL and PSSL II and 8% in equity co-investments. With that, I'll turn the call back to Art for closing remarks.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thanks, Rick. In conclusion, I'd like to thank our exceptional team for their continued dedication and our shareholders for their trust and partnership. We remain focused on delivering durable earnings, preserving capital and creating long-term value for all stakeholders. That concludes our remarks at this time. So AI is obviously a critical question we ask of every potential investment we make is what is AI going to mean for this particular industry? What's AI going to mean for this particular company? And we try best we can, and we have a lot of expertise internally. We have a lot of external expertise that we access to try to help us find areas where we have some -- let's put it this way.

If we feel like we have tailwinds behind the particular industry or the particular niche or the particular company that then we'll be more positive and supportive of making investment versus where we think there's wind in our face and we have a headwind, whether you define it as software or AI or technology, these are all different kind of potential shades of gray. But like some of our North Stars are in defense and government services, if the companies that we finance can provide excellent services at a lower cost to the government, that's usually a winner, assuming those services have a defensive moat around it. Same thing, by the way, for health care.

Health care does have a reimbursement question. So our North Star is can we find companies that have a defensive moat that generate a high-quality service for a lower cost so that whoever the payer is, whether it's the government or insurance companies, that company can be a winner. So I'm trying as best I can answer your question, Robert. It's sometimes hard to kind of select where software begins in AI, where software ends and AI begins, but kind of the rubric in the North Star is finding companies that have a moat that have some tailwind that is defensible and provide high-quality products and services at a lower-than-normal cost. And so there tends to be a seasonal uptick in calendar Q4 and calendar Q1 tends to be lighter. That's the historical. By and large, we think our portfolio is positioned to handle interest rate increases.

We have something like 160 companies in this portfolio. We have companies or so around the platform. In terms of spread, we kind of think we've stabilized here in the 500 to 550. Every so often, a particularly strong credit may get a 475. They've kind of left the space due to all the reasons you know and the cash flows out of their systems in the private wealth channel. So that 25 to 50 space has really been a really nice space.

And as you look at our -- the deals we've been doing recently in our pipeline, we've been exercising that opportunity in that 25 to 50 of EBITDA because of the deal flow and because the spreads are widening a touch.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. Look, the only thing there that connects them, and we've talked about this in the last few calls, this is just the seasoning of the post-COVID vintage. So these were deals that were done in '21, '22, a, interest rates were really low then; and b, business models, the world was kind of had different business models. Like, for instance, one of the companies sell shoes on the Internet. Guess what? Shoes were being sold on the Internet very heavily post-COVID. They're still being sold on the Internet, but less so, more shoes as we revert to the mean and society shoes are getting bought in actual physical stores. So we have a car wash nonaccrual post-COVID.

People had a lot of disposable income to keep their cars squeaky, clean and they bought subscription to car wash companies. And today, that kind of mid-level, lower-level consumer is a little bit more squeezed in this K-shaped economy and car wash revenues have gone down across the industry. So I think these are kind of the examples kind of connecting the handful of nonaccruals we have, that's kind of the one theme. It's kind of that post-COVID 2021, 2022 vintage.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. We're seeing kind of upper single digits, 8%, 9% growth of revenues and EBITDA across the portfolio. Again, that blended in there is our handful of underperformers. But by and large, we're seeing reasonable growth. Again, a lot of health care, a lot of government services, defense, a lot of stable business services, some home services that are doing well. Who would have thought that garage door repair would be the high-growth industry, but it is. We've had some nice -- as one example, garage door consolidation -- garage door repair consolidation has been a nice theme we've taken advantage of.

Arren Saul Cyganovich: And then on the PSSL II ramp, you're making good headway there, still a decent ways away from your around $1 billion targeted size. How much additional earnings accretion do you think you'll generate from that piece whenever we get fully invested there?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Well, we would hate to take away the modeling exercise from excellent research analysts at Truist and elsewhere. But I guess if you wanted to kind of model it out, it would probably be at least a couple of cents a share a quarter.

Operator: We'll go next to Christopher Nolan with Ladenburg Thalmann.

Christopher Nolan: Art, what are the target leverage levels you're looking to run these JVs at?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. If you look at our other 2 JVs, they're roughly at 2x, obviously, higher than, let's say, the 1.5x we run PFLT. We think these -- the raw material we have, which are lower risk 4.5x levered covenanted core middle market loans are a good match for securitization technology. So we finance these JVs and in many cases, the BDC parent with securitization technology. We really think it's nice and safe. And we do have -- in our institutional side of our business, we do put these in for institutional investors.

There are institutional investors who like these assets, again, a different risk-adjusted return, 4 or 5x leverage, and they can get an upper teens to 20% return on their junior capital, again, with the securitization box, which can be -- we view as safer than, in some cases, a credit facility because you don't have to worry about a credit officer in a corner office having a bad hair day. If there's issues, it's orderly, it's not anyone yanking your chain. So we like for our lower-risk senior loans and securitization technology, and we use it in these JVs.

Christopher Nolan: And then on the JV, is there a mark-to-market risk where if interest rates go up and then the discount rate used in the fair value, the portfolio investments also go up? Does that result in you and your JV partner having to kick in more equity into the fund?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: That's a nice thing about securitization is not mark-to-market. So it's a box. It's a long-term box. It's a 10-year box. It's locked in. If you have credit underperformance, it's a very orderly wind down. It's not a fire sale or having to sell assets at exactly the worst time. And we've been operating these securitization boxes going back to 2019, which included... [Technical Difficulty]

Richard (Rick) Allorto: One second, Chris. I think Art just ran into a little bit of technical difficulty.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Am I back on? Sorry, I had some technical difficulty. Crazy things are going on out there. So as I was saying, we've been using this technology since 2019 and dealt with it during COVID. So we kind of saw kind of the worst possible case and it's a very robust box.

Christopher Nolan: Great. And then I guess, I know that you guys -- on the dividend, I know you guys have committed to the current dividend payout through the end of the calendar year. Any comment you can give beyond that?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Look, we don't -- on the dividend, our base dividend is $0.24. We think we should be earning a reasonable amount above that, and we've committed to our supplemental being 50% of the excess. So if you model it out, if you grow the JV, you want to throw in a base rate increase or 2, we don't think of any -- there's any real risk to that base dividend of $0.24 or $0.08 a month, and we think there's some nice upside to it.

Operator: We'll go next to Paul Conrad Johnson with KBW.

Paul Conrad Johnson: There was another fairly meaningful write-down in PSSL I this quarter. And that JV has been, I think, a little bit of a headwind this year in terms of some write-downs in the equity. But I'm just curious, balance sheet credit quality remains fairly good. Nonaccruals are only 1.8%, PIK is relatively low. So I'm just curious, what has kind of been the driver of losses there? And why has PSSL I underperformed, I guess, relative kind of to the balance sheet, whether that's just been vintage or further seasoning that portfolio? Any sort of color you have there?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. So it's a good question, Paul. And many of the same names and themes at PFLT, a car wash company a company that sells shoes on the Internet. So it's the same kind of names that you see. And so there's no difference other than to the early point, a little bit more leverage. So it's going to be a little bit more exacerbated. But that said, it also can swing to the other side. This particular quarter, we had those handful of names. We're hoping we're working through the challenges from that 2021, 2022 vintage, and we'll be out of the other side pretty soon here.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Got it. And I mean, in terms of like new deployments, I guess, what has been maybe the pickup from last year? You kind of mentioned spreads where they are but is structure materially better? Or is it just pricing in your opinion, kind of in terms of the deployment that's going into the JVs in the portfolio today, how -- what's, I guess, better about this vintage versus the prior vintage?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. Look, I think it's kind of what I said earlier, Paul, which is in that upper portion of our space, the $25 million to $50 million of EBITDA because the big brands have receded, we're seeing a greater opportunity there where we can lend to $25 million to $50 million EBITDA companies at wider spreads than we could, let's say, a year ago. When the big brands were still raising a lot of money and every so often they come down and compress kind of in that $25 million to $50 million EBITDA range. So that's where some of the funds flows are helping us a bit as we execute on our model.

So again -- and we feel good about kind of our main industries that where we have real domain expertise, we're feeling good about the tailwinds behind those particular industries in this environment. So that's maybe where it's a little bit better than it was a year ago.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Got it. And I mean, in terms of relative to the seasoning that you're talking about in the stock, the credit seasoning I'm talking about, both in the JVs and the portfolio, the stock trades at now one of the lowest valuations in years. balance sheet is a little bit constrained from a leverage standpoint, clearly, there's growth left in the PSSL II JV. But why is -- I guess, there are no consideration of a buyback even if it's not something that's aggressively executed. But just to have something like this available when the stock is trading at such a big discount to NAV, why can't that be balanced, I guess, alongside just continued deployment?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yes. No, it's a great question. And I just want to say all options are always on the table, including a buyback, including other ways to add value. So we're not saying there's not a discussion of that. We agree with you. It's a really attractive valuation right now for investors with a steady income with floating rates going up here with a manageable nonaccrual rate with among the lowest PIK in the industry. So we agree.

Amid 60 Minutes Concerns, CBS News’ Bari Weiss Just Suffered Another Setback With The Show

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Why Bari Weiss’ CBS News Is Going Through More Staff Shakeups Months After 60 Minutes Firings

Ex-60 Minutes Editor Breaks Silence On The Show’s Bari Weiss Era, And He Has One Question

60 Minutes Source On Why There’s Been ‘Utter Incompetence’ Since Bari Weiss Began Running The Show

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

This is less-than-positive news for the long-running show.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

CBS News Editor-in-Chief Bari Weiss has made headlines for multiple reasons since being hired for the job in October 2025, but one specific aspect of the job she continues to receive backlash for is 60 Minutes. This past summer, Weiss and executive producer Nick Bilton cut ties with several high-profile employees and, more recently, the show has seen losses in viewership. Now, some fresh numbers regarding the news magazine show’s 59th season have arrived, and they mark yet another setback for Weiss and her staff.

Going into this latest season of 60 Minutes, which premiered in September amid the 2026 TV schedule, there was much speculation as to how the show would fare ratings-wise. This latest Nielsen data update from The Wall Street Journal (via The Independent) suggests that the show has indeed been underperforming with its broadcasts. Per the report, the series averaged 8.3 million viewers for the nine episodes that aired between September 9 and November 16. That marks a 16 percent drop from the previous season.

Months ago, Season 59 premiered to 6.5 million viewers on CBS, and that sum differed from the initial report of 7.7 million (which was not finalized at the time). One of the key commonalities between both reports, however, is that the news program has seen losses within the 25-54 demographic. In that demo, 60 has apparently seen a 25 percent decline compared to last season. This demographic is critical, especially when considering on-air advertising.

These ratings mark another less-than-desired situation for CBS News during what’s been a major year of change for 60 Minutes. In May, correspondents Cecilia Vega and Sharyn Alfonsi were fired along with executive producer Tanya Simon and several others. Just recently, Vega said she found it “disturbing” that she still didn’t know the exact reason she was let go. And, shortly after those moves were made, Scott Pelley was also fired from the show after allegedly accusing Weiss of “murdering” the series.

Another topic of discussion revolves around the claims of “journalistic interference” at 60. Series alums like Pelley and Vega have alleged that they were encouraged to insert what they considered to be news bias into their reporting. CBS veteran Dan Rather also chastised Weiss and co. for their reporting. The E-I-C has denied that employees’ editorial autonomy has been taken away from journalists at the company.

With the team at 60 Minutes being aware of the chatter that’s surrounded the show as of late, Bari Weiss recently addressed concerns. She recently spoke at a conference organized by The Information, where she said the show had been doing “tremendously well.” She also emphasized that her main objective for CBS News as a whole was to build a news media organization built on trust. In doing so, she’s made changes to several programs, with the aforementioned show really receiving a makeover.

Bari Weiss Apparently ‘Arrived Like A Conquering Hero’ At CBS News. Why Things Reportedly Changed

After parting ways with Pelley and co., Weiss hired several other broadcasters to fill out 60 Minutes’ new broadcasting team. Ross Douthat, Gianna Toboni, Sebastian Junger, Sir Trevor Phillips and Ariel Levy were ultimately brought on. Those newcomers are now working alongside the show’s three on-camera veterans: Lesley Stahl, Bill Whitaker and Jon Wertheim. Just recently, Stahl addressed the backlash she faced due to remaining with the show under Weiss’ leadership and said she did so in order for the series as they know it not to “vanish.”

There may still be some level of continuity from a talent perspective, but the ratings still stand. As of this writing, neither Bari Weiss nor anyone else from CBS News has addressed this latest viewership update on 60 Minutes. Time will tell how Weiss, Nick Bilton and more might seek to remedy the situation.

Biden admin weighed quitting Twitter after Elon Musk purchase, new HHS doc drop reveals

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Medicare Chief Dr. Mehmet Oz announce the first 15 organizations receiving federal funding to combat chronic disease through nutrition and lifestyle care.

Amid a push to bolster transparency, the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) launched a searchable trove of public records, including previously unreleased memos from the Biden administration and Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s calendars.

While the website will be updated over time, the initial drop includes a Biden-era memo detailing how the National Institutes of Health (NIH) considered no longer having a presence on Twitter after billionaire Elon Musk acquired the platform in October 2022, renamed it X and promised to scale back moderation amid free expression concerns.

Also included within the department's so-called Radical Transparency dashboard are Kennedy’s calendars, which reporters have previously requested and have not been publicly disclosed before now.

HHS BUREAUCRATS ACCUSED OF 'SABOTAGING' RFK JR'S EFFORTS TO EXPOSE FAUCI RECORDS

Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. delivers remarks during an event at HHS headquarters Aug. 10, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Win McNamee/Getty Images)

"When President Trump asked me to lead HHS, I promised to bring transparency back to the American health agencies," Kennedy said in a video announcing the launch of the website. "If the government makes decisions that affect your health, you deserve to see the evidence behind them. Not simply to be told, ‘trust the science.’"

Kennedy claims that HHS’ previous method of presenting public records was disorganized and confusing to use. He said public accountability would be aided by consolidating the documents in one simplified dashboard.

The Cabinet secretary noted that his department plans to upload more documents to the website during his tenure and the first drop includes previously available documents that are now easier to search and locate.

TRUMP ORDERS FEDERAL CONTRACTORS TO ALERT WHITE HOUSE ON CONGRESSIONAL RECORD REQUESTS

Though many on the political right are likely to hail the move as HHS’ increasing transparency efforts, Democrats have criticized the agency for lacking transparency under Kennedy's leadership.

Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. speaks during an announcement at HHS headquarters in Washington, D.C., on July 21, 2026. (Tierney L. Cross/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Congressional Democrats, for instance, have repeatedly said Kennedy stonewalls their oversight inquiries and claim that HHS, under his leadership, has provided incomplete responses to their questions. A letter sent by 18 Democratic lawmakers on Nov. 19, 2026, called out "an alarming lack of public transparency" following a delay to the disbursement of rural healthcare grants.

In early 2025, HHS reduced staff working on Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) requests and public records at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and Food and Drug Administration (FDA) — another move Democrats claim stifles or at least slows transparency.

Previous releases of public records by the Trump administration have enabled journalists to uncover millions of dollars in welfare fraud.

President Donald Trump has charged Vice President JD Vance, seen by some as his heir apparent, with leading the White House charge against fraud, signaling the high importance of the issue to the administration.

WHITE HOUSE LAUNCHES INTERACTIVE MAP TRACKING BILLIONS IN SUSPECTED FRAUD

Agriculture Secretary Brooke Rollins, left, President Donald Trump, Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Education Secretary Linda McMahon attend an event at the White House on May 22, 2025. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

In addition to public records releases, HHS has also pushed regulations requiring that healthcare companies make more information public about their pricing practices.

While the administration focuses on fraud, concerns about corruption within its ranks have taken root with voters amid press reports regarding conflicts of interest and alleged self-dealing. A Fox News survey found that 60% of Americans were concerned about government corruption ahead of the recent midterm elections.

HHS did not respond to a request for comment when reached by Fox News Digital on Tuesday.

Bitcoin Losses Are the Worst Since FTX. Is the Bottom Near?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Losses Are the Worst Since FTX. Is the Bottom Near?

Bitcoin investors are locking in their heaviest losses since the collapse of FTX in November 2022, according to a report published Tuesday by blockchain analytics firm Glassnode.

The world’s largest cryptocurrency slipped below $60,000, trading at $59,992, more than 50% from its October 2025 peak.

“Bitcoin losses are the heaviest since FTX,” Glassnode wrote on X. “Set against the money that flowed into Bitcoin over the past year, they have almost doubled in a week.”

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Glassnode tracks realized losses by looking at coins that move on-chain at prices lower than when they previously moved. Measuring those losses against the capital that entered Bitcoin over the preceding year gives analysts a way to compare sell-offs across market cycles, even as the cryptocurrency grows in value.

The last time the indicator reached these levels, Sam Bankman-Fried’s crypto empire was collapsing and Bitcoin was trading below $16,000. This time, the selling pressure has coincided with a much broader retreat from risky assets, and even good news for the tech industry hasn’t been enough to keep Bitcoin afloat.

Bitcoin rallied 5% in about half an hour on Monday after Nvidia announced that Jensen Huang was returning as CEO. Less than six hours later, Bitcoin had given back the entire rally, while the Nasdaq 100 still held roughly three-quarters of its gains. In trading, this is known as a bull trap, and happens on strong bearish trends.

Bitcoin’s correlation with the Nasdaq 100 is now at its highest since September 2022, Glassnode said. Over the past 60 trading days, Bitcoin has tended to gain less when tech stocks rise and lose more when they fall.

U.S. investors have also been pulling money out of the market. Spot Bitcoin ETFs have lost $4.3 billion on net over the past 20 trading days, while Coinbase traders have been heavy net sellers over the past month. Buying and selling on Binance, meanwhile, have been roughly balanced.

“Spikes like this often came close to past lows, but some came early,” Glassnode said. The firm pointed to the sell-offs of early 2019, March 2020, and November 2022, when losses surged near market bottoms, but also to earlier episodes in 2018 and 2022 when prices had further to fall.

Long-term holders—investors who have held their coins for roughly five months or longer—accounted for most realized losses from mid-April to mid-November. Nearly 90% of the long futures positions vulnerable to liquidation between last week’s Bitcoin price and $53,200 are also gone, reducing the potential for another cascade of forced selling within that range.

Glassnode is watching Bitcoin’s realized price, the average price at which circulating coins last moved, which currently stands at $53,200. Bitcoin traded below that metric before bottoming in each of the previous three major bear markets.

Applying the discounts seen at the lows of 2022 and 2018 to today’s realized price would put Bitcoin at roughly $41,000 and $37,000, respectively.

For now, the more immediate concern is whether Bitcoin can close above its June closing low of $58,500. Glassnode said a recovery above $60,000 accompanied by renewed ETF inflows would help establish whether the summer lows are holding.

German Women Climb New Route on Sharphu VI

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Last month, five women with the German Alpine Club established a new route on the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI in eastern Nepal. It was only the second overall ascent of the 6,076m peak near Kangchenjunga.

In October, Steffi Feistl, Anna Gomeringer, Kristin Hinkelmann, Kathi Huber, and Fenja Kochl set up their base camp at 4,000m. A huge amount of snow had fallen earlier that season, guaranteeing arduous slogging in some areas.

Their preliminary plan had been to attempt either Sharphu II or Sharphu VI, but Sharphu II’s condition looked too dangerous due to unstable rock, threatening seracs, and hazardous snow conditions.

Sharphu II and VI. Photo: Xtreme Climbers/labels by Gunter Seyfferth

On the other hand, the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI seemed feasible. The team acclimatized over several reconnaissance trips, overnighting up to 4,800m. They then set up their advanced base camp on a moraine ridge at that altitude. Then Kochl, Hinkelmann, and Huber set off over initially easy but deep snow-covered terrain.

They eventually reached a steep section at around 5,300m where powder snow overlying bare rock made placing protection difficult. “The mixed pitches had looked easy, but were steeper than expected,” recalled Huber. “Nasty, granular snow, and no hold on the rock underneath.”

Their work complete, the trio rappelled down, and the following day, Feistl and Gomeringer took their turn. They inched up a difficult mixed pitch followed by 200 vertical meters of burrowing through waist-deep powder.

The route up the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

After a rest day, the five women set off for the summit. They reached a glacier plateau via a 5,800m, previously unclimbed sub-peak. Here, they bivvied. On October 21, they began the final two kilometers to the main summit. The recent sun had hardened the snow, so they sank at most knee-deep. Eventually, they reached the summit ridge. They had to take turns standing on the summit, because that final ridge was so narrow.

Gomeringer and Kochl on the summit of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

Sharphu VI was first climbed on March 25, 2025, by a Chilean-Nepalese team who thought they’d climbed a different mountain. They went up via the east flank. Before that, Sharphu VI had been attempted only once, in 2023.

The German women’s route featured 1,300m of elevation gain, graded M5, 55°. The expedition was the culmination of the German Alpine Club’s three-year women’s training program.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

11 Things Jensen Huang Actually Said at His First All-Hands Back at Nvidia

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang is going to therapy for 90 minutes a day, rethinking his management style, and says that violating Nvidia’s code of conduct isn’t necessarily a fireable offense. That’s just some of what the newly returned Nvidia CEO told staff during his first all-hands meeting on Tuesday.

Here are some of the most striking things Huang said during the meeting, according to remarks reviewed by WIRED. Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

On self-improvement: “I go to therapy every day for an hour and a half. And it's been helping me a lot.”

On accountability: “There are rumors on the internet that apparently I'm a tech bro, and therefore I’m back. Apparently, I'm a tech bro, and because I wear a super nice jacket, that I'm above the law, and I’m above the code of conduct. That is not true. It won't be tolerated. That is not true. It won’t be tolerated, and it wasn't tolerated here.”

“If one of you were to violate a code of conduct, what does that mean? If you were ever to violate a code of conduct, it is not appropriate. I just wanted you to know, it is not right, it's not appropriate, it won't be tolerated. But ‘it won't be tolerated’ does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door. Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments. And your management team will still make the decision. That is what is in the best interest of the company and its employees, with all the facts and circumstances.”

On his management style: “The intensity I bring elevates everybody else. But I can go too far. When I go too far, it hurts people, it belittles them, it disempowers them. And that’s not right. And so I need to go figure out how I'm going to bring the intensity, the crazy intensity, to the field.”

On women at Nvidia: “I believe we need to create and continue to sustain a safe environment for all of our women. And if I did anything that put that in jeopardy, I cannot tell you how sad I am, and I'll do everything I can to fix it. We will not tolerate that.”

On succession: “All day long, this company can run autonomously, and you saw it. I promise you, whether I'm here or not, this company is wired up to execute, execute, and execute, and deliver on financial results and financial results.”

“I also didn't believe in succession planning. Let that sink in. I don’t believe in succession planning. I think it’s flawed. The idea of succession planning is not flawed. What people think succession planning means is flawed.”

“There’s a reason why we don’t have succession planning, it’s not because we are unaware of the word. I’m the longest-running tech CEO in history.”

On politics: “There [were] also rumors that apparently I'm back because the highest levels of our government wanted me back. That is also completely untrue. Our board of directors performed their duty as they should have.”

“It is absolutely true that President Trump really, really loves our company,” Huang told employees. “He gloats about Nvidia. You know he does. But there’s a question about politics. Are we now political? Are we partisan? I wanna be very clear with you. President Trump is the United States president. He is my president. And I’ll do everything I can to help this president be informed about how he could make decisions that are in the best interest of our country. … We have to do it, and whoever the next president is will be my president, and I will do exactly the same.”

“Don't think for a second that politics ever play a role in this company, not even a little bit. We are the most apolitical company. I don't love that we talk about politics here. Take it home. I love that we talk about work here.”

Frame TVs are one of the more interesting home theater innovations in recent years, mostly because they prioritize “form” as much as “function.” They take a two-pronged approach: offering high-definition 4K entertainment and decor in one fell swoop. In other words, you can finally get use out of your TV, even when you’re not, well, using it. That’s where the art function comes in.

We’ve recommended quite a few art TVs over the years, but one stands out amongst the pack: Samsung’s The Frame. It’s high-performing, sleek, and boasts a curated art gallery for when you’re not streaming or playing GTA VI. And right now, for Black Friday, you can get one for under $1,000.

Now’s the perfect opportunity to upgrade your living room, master bedroom, or mancave with Samsung’s best-selling 4K TV. The latest model is almost 30% off—and under $1,000. All things considered, this is one of our favorite Black Friday deals so far. And the two-day shipping definitely helps.

The Frame offers a discreet, tasteful centerpiece option. It’s a pioneer of sorts, first debuting back in 2017. But Samsung has upgraded it quite a bit since then. GTA 6 chapter 1 is so good that Im afraid to move on to chapter 2

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I've run into a very stupid problem with Grand Theft Auto VI, a game I love so far.

Namely, I'm having a surprisingly difficult time moving on from chapter 1 of the game's sprawling crime story. For a couple of real-life days now, I've been sitting on the edge of finishing GTA 6's opening stanza with very little motivation to move on. At the time of writing, I have literally one story mission left to go before the first chapter ends, and almost nothing of substance left to do otherwise.

But as much as I know I need to break through and see the rest of what GTA 6 has to offer, it's hard to resist the call to just live out Jason Duval's mundane daily routine for eternity.

Warning: There are some light spoilers for early parts of GTA 6's story in the following section.

For those who haven't played GTA 6 yet or are still in the early stages like me, chapter 1 follows Jason's meager existence as a sort of paid goon for Brian, a local boatyard owner who dabbles in drug trafficking on the side. The other protagonist, Lucia, gets out of prison partway through the chapter, but for most of it, she's not playable yet. Most of the story and side missions in chapter 1 focus on Jason doing little odd jobs that occasionally spiral into chaotic violence. He spends time with his girlfriend or buddies and otherwise just kind of chills when he's not doing all the crime stuff.

GTA 6 chapter 1 immediately establishes an endearingly ordinary routine for Jason, at least by action video game protagonist standards. Every in-game day I wake up, feed the pet snake, drink coffee, work out, drink a protein shake, and take a shower before heading out to do whatever it takes to get enough money to set up a nest egg for Jason and Lucia.

While there are some minor gameplay incentives to doing all of that, GTA 6 doesn't outright demand any of it from the player. I'm just doing it because it's a legitimately interesting loop, especially by the standards of big-budget mainstream games. I'm not just endlessly traversing between icons on a map and shooting some bad guys when I get to each one. I feel like an active participant in the everyday lives of two adults, which at the very least is novel for this type of game. It also helps that Brian is voiced by Stephen Root, so you spend most of chapter 1 being yelled at by Buck Strickland, and that's lovely.

I mean, most of my favorite missions so far have barely involved crime at all. The one where Jason and Lucia go to a bar after she gets out of prison is a remarkable virtual rendition of a romantic night out, while I've already lavished the Carly Rae Jepsen mission with praise on this website.

All of that is why I'm having such a hard time moving forward with the main story. I know in my heart that chapter 2 will likely introduce a new setting with new stakes, new mechanics, and an increased focus on crime. And all of that will probably be fantastic in its own right, but it won't be the same as the game I've loved up until now.

And if that's the feeling Rockstar intends to elicit in players when chapter 1 draws to a close, I would say they pulled it off. Maybe even a little too well.
