Scientists Build 6,986 Digital Brain Twins to Study Dementia

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An international consortium has created personalized biophysical “digital brain twins” for 6,986 individuals across 16 countries to uncover how aging, neurodegenerative diseases, and environmental conditions shape neural dynamics. The computational models simulate excitation-inhibition balance across 90 connected brain regions, revealing distinct mechanistic signatures for Alzheimer’s disease, frontotemporal degeneration, and environmental exposures ranging from air pollution to democratic governance.

Standard magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) provides an invaluable window into the living human brain, yet it records only indirect proxies of neural activity, typically relying on group averages that obscure individual variation and say little about the underlying cellular mechanisms driving those signals.

Now, an international research team led by corresponding author Dr. Agustín Ibáñez (Scientific Director of BrainLat at Universidad Adolfo Ibáñez, strategic director of core scientific assets at the BarcelonaBeta Brain Research Center, and faculty at the Global Brain Health Institute at Trinity College Dublin) and first author Dr. Rubén Herzog (BrainLat and Distinguished Professor at the University of the Balearic Islands) has transformed static brain scans into dynamic, personalized virtual laboratories.

Published today in Nature Biomedical Engineering, the study introduces biophysical “digital brain twins” constructed from resting-state functional MRI (fMRI) scans of 6,986 individuals spanning ages 18 to 100 across 16 countries.

Rather than treating brain scans as passive statistical snapshots, biophysical brain twins apply mathematical equations grounded in neurobiology to simulate how populations of excitatory and inhibitory neurons interact across 90 connected brain regions.

For each participant, the investigators tuned their computational model until its simulated fMRI output closely matched the individual’s actual resting-state brain recordings. Independent validation showed that model-derived regional excitability closely correlated with structural grey matter volume, confirming that the virtual simulations capture real biological architecture.

“Every brain ages in a different context. Our biophysical twins help us study how the variations in our biology and the social and environmental conditions of our lives relate to brain function,” explained Ibáñez. “The importance of biophysical brain twins is that they connect what brain scans record with the underlying processes that could produce those signals. Each twin acts as a personalised virtual laboratory: researchers can adjust how its neural populations interact and examine how those changes affect the whole brain. This opens a route from observing differences to testing possible explanations, mechanisms, potential treatments and interventions.”

“MRI records indirect measures of neural activity. Biophysical twins let us simulate the neural processes that might generate those signals and explore why they differ from person to person,” added Herzog.

Applying these nearly 7,000 personalized models across diverse clinical cohorts exposed clear mechanistic boundaries between normal aging and dementia:

To overcome the historical divide between neuroimaging and real-world lived context, the team linked individual brain models with macro-level environmental indicators across 10 domains alongside personal socioeconomic histories.

Robust associations emerged in seven of the ten environmental domains, most prominently in indicators measuring democratic conditions (free choices, civic participation, political representation, and rule of law), soil and water quality, and air pollution. Complementary analyses of individual life circumstances further linked brain dynamics to healthcare access, childhood experiences, and financial stability. These relationships persisted even after controlling for age, sex, clinical diagnosis, country, and MRI scanner hardware.

By releasing their open-source code and model parameters, the authors have established a global testbed for in silico experimentation. Future studies can overlay neurotransmitter receptor maps onto these digital twins—simulating how targeted modulation of glutamate (excitatory) or GABA (inhibitory) signaling, or public health improvements in air quality and healthcare access, might shift an aging brain back toward healthy functional patterns.

What is a biophysical “digital brain twin”?

A digital brain twin is a personalized computer simulation built from an individual’s fMRI scan using biological equations. By modeling how 90 connected brain regions interact, it allows researchers to estimate underlying cellular processes, such as the balance between signals that excite or inhibit neurons—and test virtual treatments safely on a computer.

How do brain dynamics differ between normal aging and dementia?

In the study of nearly 7,000 people, normal aging primarily changed how regularly or unpredictably brain activity fluctuated over time. In contrast, Alzheimer’s disease and frontotemporal degeneration disrupted overall activity levels, variability, and the balance between excitatory and inhibitory brain signals all at once.

Can environmental and social conditions really affect how the brain functions?

Yes. Even after accounting for age, sex, diagnosis, and scanner differences, researchers found that environmental factors, especially democratic conditions, soil and water quality, air pollution, healthcare access, and financial circumstances, were measurably linked to underlying simulated brain dynamics.

Biophysical brain twins map the phenotype–expotype architecture of ageing and dementia

Biophysical whole-brain models may reveal causal links between neural dynamics and biological–environmental variation, yet no framework has explained how these factors become embedded in mechanistic dynamics.

Here we develop individualized biophysical digital twins mechanistically linked to phenotypes and expotypes (that is, combinations of exposomal influences) leveraging resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging data (n = 6,986, 16 countries) of healthy controls and participants with mild cognitive impairment, Alzheimer’s disease and frontotemporal dementia.

Personalized twins improved goodness-of-fit over group-level models. They reproduced structure–function coupling between regional excitability and brain volume, captured age, sex and neurodegeneration, and encoded 68 expotypes.

Associations remained robust after controlling for demographics, data quality, model fit and scanner/site, with the strongest effects for social and physical (pollution) exposomes. We release an open resource (empirical data, code, >3 million simulations) for future research.

These findings ground phenotypic–exposomic variation in individualized biophysical twins, enabling in silico testing of mechanisms and potential model-informed interventions.

I Don't Think Enough People Talk About What A Masterpiece From The '90s 12 Monkeys Is

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

There Is One Thing About The Original Blade That I HAVE To Talk About

One Of Paul Thomas Anderson's Best Movies Is Always Overshadowed, Despite Starring A Legend

Why The Twist In The Sixth Sense Is Still The Best In Cinema History

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Pulp Fiction is great and all, but what about 12 Monkeys?!

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When discussing movies from the ‘90s, there are some films that get mentioned all the time. I'm talking about movies like Pulp Fiction, The Silence of the Lambs, and, of course, The Shawshank Redemption (Even though I think it's just “good” and not great).

I'd also be remiss if I didn’t bring up films that came out during the Disney Renaissance, such as The Lion King and Aladdin. Why am I bringing up those movies? You already know them and have probably already seen them. In fact, you can find many of the most beloved films from the ‘90s on our list of the 100 best films from that decade.

However, do you want to know what movie ISN’T on our list? The 1995 masterpiece (Yes, masterpiece) 12 Monkeys. I actually think it’s one of the best films from that decade, and here’s why.

A lot of people like to bring up great opening scenes in films, but do you know what I don’t think gets enough credit? Great opening songs, and look, I get it. Many older, typically black-and-white films (or westerns!) began with opening credits and stirring music before we met the characters. But nowadays, most films jump right into the action (in medias res, as it were).

However, 12 Monkeys probably has my favorite intro from the ‘90s, and it’s entirely because of that accordion-driven song, “Suite Punta del Este” by the Argentine composer, Astor Pantaleón Piazzolla. This is after we get a green text scrawl telling us that 5 billion people will die from a deadly virus, and that the survivors will move underground. Oh, and these are the words from a diagnosed paranoid schizophrenic.

This text is then followed up by the aforementioned, driving accordion song, and then a close-up of a child’s eyes as the kid watches somebody get gunned down in an airport. Absolute cinema!

I love a good time travel story, and 12 Monkeys has one of my favorites of any film. The story concerns a convict named Cole (Bruce Willis) who is sent back in time to investigate what caused the virus that killed 99% of the human population. He’s tasked with learning who the “Army of the 12 Monkeys” are, since they’re suspected of releasing the virus, as there are records of them writing, “We did it!”

Sounds simple enough. But the problem is, Cole’s time traveling gets overshot, and he ends up in 1990 rather than 1996. Because he sounds crazy - talking about the end of the world and whatnot - he gets committed to a mental institution. It’s here that he meets another patient, played by Brad Pitt, but is then yanked back into the future, only to be sent to World War 1.

There’s a lot more wonky time travel shenanigans, but we learn what the message of “We did it!” meant (and it has nothing to do with the virus). We also learn that the child from the beginning who witnessed the shooting is actually Cole witnessing himself as an adult getting shot! Trippy!

Lastly, Brad Pitt is just so good in this movie. He plays a mental asylum patient, and the closest thing I can compare his performance to is Johnny Depp wigging out in Fear and Loathing in Las Vegas.

Pitt’s character, Jeffrey Goines, looks both extremely tired and also hopped up on goofballs at the same time. He’s erratic, unpredictable, and simultaneously both funny and scary. It’s little wonder why he was nominated for Best Supporting Actor for this performance.

In every way, the acting, the storytelling, the music, the everything! just makes 12 Monkeys one of the best movies of the ‘90s, and an absolute gem that more people need to talk about.

Rich is a Jersey boy, through and through. He graduated from Rutgers University (Go, R.U.!), and thinks the Garden State is the best state in the country. That said, he’ll take Chicago Deep Dish pizza over a New York slice any day of the week. Don’t hate. When he’s not watching his two kids, he’s usually working on a novel, watching vintage movies, or reading some obscure book.

Coldcard reveals how Bitcoin wallet flaw went undetected for over 5 years

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Coldcard maker Coinkite explained why years of security checks failed to catch the flaw and said it has yet to decide on compensation for affected customers.

Bitcoin hardware wallet maker Coinkite has detailed how a security flaw in its Coldcard devices went undetected for more than five years despite several independent security reviews, including those using artificial intelligence (AI).

In a Nov. 23 postmortem report, Coinkite said that while its security checks confirmed the wallets had the correct system for generating secure recovery phrases, the company never verified whether the wallets were actually using it. As a result, a software error caused the wallets to use a weaker system instead, making the recovery phrases easier for hackers to guess.

A Bitcoin hardware wallet is a physical device that stores private keys offline, while a recovery phrase is a series of words that allows users to restore access to their funds if the device is lost or damaged.

Coinkite said at least 15 independent security reviews and research efforts failed to catch the flaw before the thefts began. It added that the weaker system went undetected because the recovery phrases looked normal and could still pass some security tests.

The flaw allowed hackers to steal at least 1,789 Bitcoin (BTC), worth around $114.7 million at the time of the thefts, Galaxy Research reported in August. Coinkite said it fixed the flaw on July 31 and introduced further security measures in August.

The report says that as of Nov. 18, Coinkite had received around 300 loss-related support tickets associated with 242 accounts. The company also said that the actual number of victims was likely higher and that it is still exploring ways to help affected customers but has yet to decide on a compensation plan.

Coinkite said it still does not know who stole the funds or how the hackers found the flaw. The company said law enforcement authorities are also investigating the thefts, and the Canadian Centre for Cyber Security has opened a separate case.

The company continues to urge affected users to update their software, create new recovery phrases and move their funds to new wallets, warning that software updates cannot make previously affected recovery phrases safe.

Cointelegraph contacted Coinkite for comment on its compensation plans but had not received a response by publication.

I do not write about all offers that are available -- instead focusing on miles, points, and cash back (and currencies that can be converted into the same). Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

American Airlines has the best overall U.S. program for making credit card spending count toward status, and you don’t need one of its expensive premium cards to take advantage of it.

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® [See rates and fees] earns one Loyalty Point per eligible dollar, without a cap. You can combine those points with what you earn flying, shopping and through many of American’s other partners, all the way up to Executive Platinum. Meanwhile, the card’s checked bag benefit can cover its annual fee on a single family trip.

I recently covered the card’s refresh, which adds Walmart+, an inflight credit and travel protections while raising the annual fee. Those changes take effect January 24, 2027. They improve the card for some customers, but the reason to put spending on it remains earning status.

American’s current offer is 50,000 bonus miles after $2,500 in purchases within the first three months. There’s no annual fee for the first 12 months then $99, increasing to $150 with renewals starting January 24, 2027. That means for new applications, after the introductory year, the card’s annual fee is $150. Citi’s initial bonus restriction looks back 48 months. You aren’t eligible if you’ve received a Platinum Select new account bonus during that period, or converted another Citi card on which you earned a new account bonus during that period into a Platinum Select. A bonus on a different American Airlines card doesn’t, by itself, disqualify you. The miles are worth having. I’ve written about American’s partner award sweet spots, like 70,000 miles for business class between North America and the Middle East or Indian Subcontinent. And I find the miles useful for domestic travel too, often uncovering good mileage deals when cash prices are high (my sense that American doesn’t want to discount the fare, but doesn’t expect to fill the plane without doing so). Why American Is Best Overall For Spending Toward Status The card earns one Loyalty Point per dollar of eligible purchases. There’s no annual cap on that earning and no minimum flight requirement to qualify for American’s published status levels. Here are the thresholds, and the spending that would reach them if you earned nothing from flights, partners or separate Loyalty Point bonuses: AAdvantage Status   Loyalty Points   Card Spending Alone Gold   40,000   $40,000 Platinum   75,000   $75,000 Platinum Pro   125,000   $125,000 Executive Platinum   200,000   $200,000 American’s qualification year runs March 1 through the last day of February.If flights and other activity get you to 110,000 Loyalty Points, another $15,000 in eligible card purchases gets you to Platinum Pro. You can fill the gap with expenses you were going to incur anyway. The same Loyalty Points also move you toward Loyalty Point Rewards, including opportunities to select systemwide upgrades. Compare this with the UnitedSM Explorer Card (See rates and fees), which earns one Premier qualifying point per $20 and caps that earning at 1,000 points annually. That’s only part of the 6,000 points needed for United’s points-based Silver qualification path, which also requires at least four United or United Express flights. The similarly priced Platinum Select can take you all the way to American’s top published tier. Even Delta’s premium Reserve card takes $255,000 in purchases to earn Diamond through spending plus that one card’s annual head start. That’s 28,000 qualifying dollars, less the card’s 2,500-dollar head start, earned at one qualifying dollar per $10 spent. American’s $200,000 Executive Platinum threshold is available on this much less expensive card. Alaska’s Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® credit card ($395 annual fee) earns one status point per $2, without a cap, plus 10,000 status points annually beginning with the first anniversary. It can require less spending for particular tiers. American is my overall pick because of the combination of inexpensive cards, uncapped earning, and the ability to combine spending with so many other sources of status credit. What You Earn On Purchases The January refresh expands the bonus categories: Eligible Purchases   Today   From January 24, 2027 American Airlines, restaurants and gas stations   2 miles per dollar   2 miles per dollar AAdvantage Hotels and AAdvantage Cars bookings through their respective sites   1 mile per dollar   3 miles per dollar Electric vehicle charging, select streaming services, and eligible rides and rail purchases   1 mile per dollar   2 miles per dollar Other purchases   1 mile per dollar   1 mile per dollar Two miles per dollar on American Airlines tickets still doesn’t impress me. The Citi Strata Premier® Card (See rates and fees.) earns three points per dollar on airfare, hotels, restaurants, supermarkets, gas and electric vehicle charging. Its points transfer to American, and its annual fee is $95. If I just wanted redeemable American miles, I’d generally prefer Strata Premier for those categories. The value in spending on this card is progress towards AAdvantage status. Free Checked Bags Can Pay For The Card The primary cardmember and up to four companions on the same reservation get their first checked bag free on eligible domestic American Airlines itineraries. American currently charges $45 online or $50 at the airport for the first checked bag on a standard domestic ticket. Basic economy costs $5 more. For two people each checking one bag on a domestic roundtrip, the savings are $180 compared with paying the standard fee online. That’s already more than the coming $150 annual fee. A family of four doing the same saves $360. You don’t have to buy the tickets with this card to receive the bag benefit. Your AAdvantage number needs to be on the reservation. The whole itinerary must qualify as domestic American Airlines or American Eagle travel. The primary cardmember must travel. An authorized user doesn’t get an independent bag allowance when traveling alone. If your status or cabin already includes a free bag, holding the card doesn’t add another one. You also get Group 5 preferred boarding, including up to four companions on the same reservation. That puts you ahead of later economy groups and improves your chances of finding overhead bin space. There are no foreign transaction fees, and authorized users can be added without an additional annual fee. Their spending earns miles and Loyalty Points for the primary cardmember. Holding an eligible AAdvantage card also keeps the miles in the primary cardmember’s account from expiring. What The January Refresh Adds The annual fee rises $51. Key incremental benefits are: Walmart+ annual membership. The primary cardmember gets the $98 annual plan, including delivery benefits, fuel savings and a streaming choice. Someone already paying for it can come out ahead from this benefit alone. Up to $100 in annual inflight credits. Eligible food, drinks and Wi-Fi purchases qualify; Admirals Club purchases don’t. This replaces the existing 25% food and beverage rebate. Half off an annual FOX One subscription. I’d assign this value only if it’s something you already want to buy. The inflight credit is better than the old rebate for someone making a modest amount of purchases. Spend $100 on eligible food and drinks and you’d receive $100 back instead of $25. The added travel protections may be more useful than the subscription benefits. Trip delay: Up to $500 per covered traveler for eligible expenses after a covered delay of at least six hours. Paying just the taxes on an AAdvantage award doesn’t limit reimbursement to that small cash payment. Trip cancellation and interruption: Coverage is limited to the lesser of $5,000 per trip or the covered amount paid with the card and/or AAdvantage miles. Lost baggage: Up to $3,000 per covered traveler, with a $10,000 total per trip; a lower per-bag limit applies to New York residents. Rental cars: Up to $75,000 for covered damage, for eligible rentals up to 31 days. Coverage is secondary in the U.S. and primary outside it. These protections begin with the refresh. Required payment methods and covered reasons differ by benefit. The card also retains up to $180 in Turo credits per calendar year, limited to $30 per eligible completed trip. You need six rentals to use the full amount, must link the card to your Turo account, and need to pay directly with it rather than through a digital wallet. There’s also 10% off eligible American Airlines Vacations packages. The $20,000 Spending Bonus Is Useful, But Do The Math Currently, spending $20,000 during your cardmembership year and renewing the card earns a $125 American Airlines Flight Discount. The refresh increases that to $150. The new discount adds 0.75% of the $20,000 in spending, assuming you use its full value. The increase itself is only another 0.125%. This isn’t an automatic annual flight credit just for holding the card. Under the published terms, the certificate expires one year after issue and can’t pay award-ticket taxes. Your cardmembership year also isn’t necessarily the same as the March-to-February status year. I’d count this as an extra return if I were already putting $20,000 on the card for Loyalty Points. I’d also consider moving a little spending to finish the threshold if I were close. I wouldn’t move $20,000 away from much better earning elsewhere simply because the flight discount happens to match the annual fee. Which American Airlines Card Should You Have? Platinum Select doesn’t include Admirals Club access or a general discount on award redemptions. The higher fee and extra benefits don’t change that. For an occasional American Airlines flyer without status, I’d choose it when checked bag savings and benefits I’d actually use cover the renewal fee. The first year’s $0 annual fee and initial bonus make trying it particularly easy. For someone who already has status, or another American Airlines card, I’d compare these options: Citi® / AAdvantage® Globe™ Mastercard® [See rates and fees] The $350 annual fee buys four Admirals Club Globe passes each calendar year, among other benefits. More interesting to me, the Flight Streak Bonus earns 5,000 additional Loyalty Points after every four qualifying American Airlines flight segments, up to 15,000 per qualification year. You don’t need to put spending on Globe to earn that flight bonus. Citi® / AAdvantage® Executive World Legend Mastercard® [See rates and fees] At a $695 annual fee, this is the card for an Admirals Club membership. It also awards 10,000 bonus Loyalty Points when you reach each of 50,000, 90,000, 165,000 and 240,000 Loyalty Points in a qualification year. Those thresholds can be reached through flights, partners or spending on other American Airlines cards. Citi® / AAdvantage BusinessTM World Elite Mastercard® [See rates and fees] Business owners should compare its separate initial bonus and spending benefits. Through December 31, 2028, eligible employee-card spending earns a Loyalty Point per dollar for the employee and an additional Loyalty Point for the primary cardmember. That’s status credit for two people from the same spending, rather than two points for one person. For instance, someone holding Executive can put dining and gas purchases on Platinum Select for two miles per dollar, earn the same basic Loyalty Point per dollar, and still trigger Executive’s account-wide Loyalty Point bonuses. They’d need enough value from Platinum Select’s extra miles, benefits and flight discount to justify its separate annual fee. The inflight credit is better than the old rebate for someone making a modest amount of purchases. Spend $100 on eligible food and drinks and you’d receive $100 back instead of $25. The added travel protections may be more useful than the subscription benefits. Trip delay: Up to $500 per covered traveler for eligible expenses after a covered delay of at least six hours. Paying just the taxes on an AAdvantage award doesn’t limit reimbursement to that small cash payment. Trip cancellation and interruption: Coverage is limited to the lesser of $5,000 per trip or the covered amount paid with the card and/or AAdvantage miles. Lost baggage: Up to $3,000 per covered traveler, with a $10,000 total per trip; a lower per-bag limit applies to New York residents. Rental cars: Up to $75,000 for covered damage, for eligible rentals up to 31 days. Coverage is secondary in the U.S. and primary outside it. These protections begin with the refresh. Required payment methods and covered reasons differ by benefit. The card also retains up to $180 in Turo credits per calendar year, limited to $30 per eligible completed trip. You need six rentals to use the full amount, must link the card to your Turo account, and need to pay directly with it rather than through a digital wallet. There’s also 10% off eligible American Airlines Vacations packages. The $20,000 Spending Bonus Is Useful, But Do The Math Currently, spending $20,000 during your cardmembership year and renewing the card earns a $125 American Airlines Flight Discount. The refresh increases that to $150. The new discount adds 0.75% of the $20,000 in spending, assuming you use its full value. The increase itself is only another 0.125%. This isn’t an automatic annual flight credit just for holding the card. Under the published terms, the certificate expires one year after issue and can’t pay award-ticket taxes. Your cardmembership year also isn’t necessarily the same as the March-to-February status year. I’d count this as an extra return if I were already putting $20,000 on the card for Loyalty Points. I’d also consider moving a little spending to finish the threshold if I were close. I wouldn’t move $20,000 away from much better earning elsewhere simply because the flight discount happens to match the annual fee. Which American Airlines Card Should You Have? Platinum Select doesn’t include Admirals Club access or a general discount on award redemptions. The higher fee and extra benefits don’t change that. For an occasional American Airlines flyer without status, I’d choose it when checked bag savings and benefits I’d actually use cover the renewal fee. The first year’s $0 annual fee and initial bonus make trying it particularly easy. For someone who already has status, or another American Airlines card, I’d compare these options: Citi® / AAdvantage® Globe™ Mastercard® [See rates and fees] The $350 annual fee buys four Admirals Club Globe passes each calendar year, among other benefits. More interesting to me, the Flight Streak Bonus earns 5,000 additional Loyalty Points after every four qualifying American Airlines flight segments, up to 15,000 per qualification year. You don’t need to put spending on Globe to earn that flight bonus. Citi® / AAdvantage® Executive World Legend Mastercard® [See rates and fees] At a $695 annual fee, this is the card for an Admirals Club membership. It also awards 10,000 bonus Loyalty Points when you reach each of 50,000, 90,000, 165,000 and 240,000 Loyalty Points in a qualification year. Those thresholds can be reached through flights, partners or spending on other American Airlines cards. Citi® / AAdvantage BusinessTM World Elite Mastercard® [See rates and fees] Business owners should compare its separate initial bonus and spending benefits. Through December 31, 2028, eligible employee-card spending earns a Loyalty Point per dollar for the employee and an additional Loyalty Point for the primary cardmember. That’s status credit for two people from the same spending, rather than two points for one person. For instance, someone holding Executive can put dining and gas purchases on Platinum Select for two miles per dollar, earn the same basic Loyalty Point per dollar, and still trigger Executive’s account-wide Loyalty Point bonuses. They’d need enough value from Platinum Select’s extra miles, benefits and flight discount to justify its separate annual fee. Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® Topics on this pageAmerican AirlinesCredit cardLoyalty programAAdvantageAlaskaAlaska AirlinesAmerican Eagle OutfittersCitibankDelta Air LinesIndian subcontinentMiddle EastNew YorkNorth AmericaSilverTuroUnited AirlinesWi-Fi+14 more More From View from the Wing

The primary cardmember and up to four companions on the same reservation get their first checked bag free on eligible domestic American Airlines itineraries. American currently charges $45 online or $50 at the airport for the first checked bag on a standard domestic ticket. Basic economy costs $5 more.

For two people each checking one bag on a domestic roundtrip, the savings are $180 compared with paying the standard fee online. That’s already more than the coming $150 annual fee. A family of four doing the same saves $360.

You don’t have to buy the tickets with this card to receive the bag benefit. Your AAdvantage number needs to be on the reservation.

You also get Group 5 preferred boarding, including up to four companions on the same reservation. That puts you ahead of later economy groups and improves your chances of finding overhead bin space.

There are no foreign transaction fees, and authorized users can be added without an additional annual fee. Their spending earns miles and Loyalty Points for the primary cardmember. Holding an eligible AAdvantage card also keeps the miles in the primary cardmember’s account from expiring.

The annual fee rises $51. Key incremental benefits are: Walmart+ annual membership. The primary cardmember gets the $98 annual plan, including delivery benefits, fuel savings and a streaming choice. Trump wanted Jensen Huang back. Now, the president is all alone in Washington

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump wanted Jensen Huang back at the helm of Nvidia. Now that the chip mogul is reinstated, Trump is cheering alone in Washington.

Trump celebrated and claimed credit after Huang was reinstated by the Nvidia board on Monday, a stunning return to power for the embattled CEO. In a nearly hour-long phone call with CNBC, the president went as far as saying he "made the deal" to bring Huang back.

But Trump's jubilation stands in stark contrast to the reaction from even his staunchest allies on Capitol Hill, who recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang — whose relationship with a potential Chinese asset they fear may have jeopardized U.S. national security.

"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States."

Trump's rare isolation from his party over Huang comes after the GOP was throttled in the midterm elections, as Democrats rode a blue tsunami to capture the Senate and the largest House majority this century. And it suggests Republicans, who have spent a decade in lockstep with the president, may have found an issue on which they can't bring themselves to take Trump's side.

"I can accept what the president has done," Cramer said. "But we have to put guardrails around to protect the United States of America."

Many midterm voters identified Silicon Valley's most visible outputs, artificial intelligence and data centers, as top concerns motivating them to cast ballots for Democrats. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey in October found 63% of registered voters oppose an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% support one.

By timing his extraordinary intervention in Nvidia so soon after the GOP's historic losses, Trump could spark a national debate about corporate governance and responsibility, former Under Secretary of State Richard Stengel said.

"With the repudiation of Trumpism in the midterms and voter revulsion with corruption, I think there will be a turn back to a more value-based duty of care for boards of directors and away from the current maximization of shareholder value," Stengel told CNBC in an email.

"Directors are citizens first, shareholders second. The idea that you put profit above national security should disgust shareholders, not please them," Stengel, who worked in the Obama administration, wrote.

Bipartisan lawmakers are now racing to pass a series of export control bills before the year ends, a call that has only grown louder since Huang was reinstated.

There's "not just frustration or political worry, but actual, substantive national security concern here from Democrats and Republicans," Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, told CNBC.

"This has historically been a bipartisan issue," McGuire said. "Democrats and Republicans generally pretty much agree that we shouldn't be sending our most advanced chips to China."

"If it happened in massive quantities, people want to know why, how, and they want to make sure that it doesn't happen again," he said. "And putting your thumb on the scale in this clear of a way seems like it could undercut those efforts."

The White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on the political dynamics of Trump's involvement in reinstating Huang.

Republicans will also have the first crack at investigating Huang next week before ceding power to Democrats in January. Democrats have vowed their own investigations into the Nvidia CEO and his ties to the president and his Cabinet. The Democrats who will lead those investigations are now hopeful that Republicans will join them.

"I've been seeing a lot of Republicans outraged and scandalized by what has just happened," House Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin, D-Md., said Tuesday on "Squawk Box," when asked if Republicans may support a bipartisan investigation of Nvidia.

"When else in American history can you think of a time when a president of the United States called up a corporate board of directors meeting and then essentially dictated to them who was going to be their new CEO right after they had fired the guy?"

Trump throughout his second term has stated repeatedly that achieving AI dominance is required for the U.S. to maintain global superiority. His administration has championed the rapid growth of the country's tech giants and forged close ties with its leaders, especially Huang, while removing constraints on the industry and opposing some new regulatory efforts.

At the same time, Trump has defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick against accusations of slow-walking export controls on China, the fiercest competitor in the AI race. And while Trump recently discussed AI during an in-person meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, he appeared to deliver few demands and achieved few assurances about Beijing's efforts to acquire U.S. chips.

"To say we can't do AI safety because of China, and then also be actively loosening the controls on China, makes no sense," McGuire said.

Some of Trump's fiercest allies have raised similar qualms.

"Reinstating Jensen Huang is going to blow back on our national security," MAGA activist Laura Loomer wrote in an X post Monday. "Eventually there has to be accountability for jeopardizing US national security. China didn't just get access to Nvidia chips by accident."

Newt Gingrich, the influential former Republican speaker of the House, in an X post after Huang's reinstatement, wrote "Congress should really dig into this as a national security issue of enormous proportions and also a significant failure in the federal bureaucracy."

Their criticisms sharply contrast with the praise coming from Silicon Valley and some of the Trump-friendly voices within it, such as Elon Musk, who wrote Monday, "The mistake has been corrected."

But that's a shrinking political constituency, McGuire said.

"The tech-right perspective is very out of touch with where most of the Republican Party is on these issues, to say nothing of the Democratic Party," he said.

"Those positions are just not going to be sustainable for much longer," he added. "I think they're already becoming unsustainable."

Imax Vet Craig Dehmel Joins Qube Cinema As Head Of Studio Relations

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Theatrical tech, distribution and management corp Qube Cinema has hired Imax and 20th Century Fox distribution vet Craig Dehmel as their new Head of Studio Relations.

Dehmel will be based in Los Angeles for Qube Wire, which has a global content distribution service which connects with over 7,000 theaters worldwide. In his new position, Dehmel will lead strategic customer development and strengthen senior-level relationships across Hollywood studios, independent distributors, and content owners.

Dehmel’s 35 years in the motion picture industry includes serving as Head of Global Distribution at Imax, where he led the planning, programming, and distribution of Hollywood studio, local-language, documentary, and special-content releases across more than 1,700 Imax screens worldwide. Related Stories Sports Imax To Screen Super Bowl In Deal With Disney, NFL News Imax Inks Largest UK Deal With Cinema Chain Acquired By Former Cineworld CEO Mooky Greidinger

Prior to Imax, Dehmel spent 16 years at 20th Century Fox, where he held senior roles in sales, strategic planning, and international distribution, culminating in his appointment as EVP, InternationalDistribution. He is a member of the Academy of Motion Picture Arts & Sciences, serving in its Executive Branch, and has been a guest lecturer at the UCLA Anderson School of Management for more than two decades. Watch on Deadline

“Qube Wire’s growth reflects the confidence that studios, distributors, and exhibitors place in our technology and services. As our global network continues to expand, strengthening our relationships with content owners and studio partners is central to our plans,” said Harsh Rohatgi, CEO of Qube Cinema.

“Craig brings an exceptional understanding of the global theatrical business and longstandingrelationships across the industry. His appointment underscores our commitment to our studio clients and will help us build on the momentum of Qube Wire, expand our engagement across the entertainment ecosystem, and create new opportunities for our wider portfolio,” he added.

Dehmel said: “I’m excited to join Qube Cinema at a time of significant growth for the company and continued evolution across the theatrical distribution landscape. Qube Wire has built an impressive global network and strong relationships with studios and exhibitors. Having worked closely with these partners throughout my career, I see considerable opportunity to build on that foundation, deepen industry relationships, and help expand the reach of Qube’s technology and services. I look forward to working with the team and our partners to support the company’s continued growth.”

Disney Really Needed A Hit With Hexed. The First Reviews Have Me Concerned

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hexed Was Originally Going To Star A Male Character. Why The Big Change Was Made

Billie From Hexed Has Just Landed At Disney Parks, But The Directors Already Have Bigger Ideas

Critics Aren’t Bewitched By Practical Magic 2, But Audiences Have Completely Different Thoughts

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In the last couple of years, when Walt Disney Animation has released a movie during its long-coveted Thanksgiving slot, it's been a sequel that became a $1 billion hit. However, it's been a long while since the House of Mouse released an original title that really cast a magical spell on audiences. I was hopeful about the next upcoming Disney movie, Hexed, especially after seeing the first reactions. But now that the reviews are here, I'm afraid we might have to wait longer for another real Disney winner.

It's never especially a great sign when a studio lifts its review embargo the day before a movie is set to release. As the reviews roll in this morning, there are a lot of complaints about it. Let's start with the thoughts of Roger Ebert's Peter Sobczynski:

On the one hand, it’s bright and colorful, with enough goofiness and action to serve as an acceptable diversion for family audiences over the long holiday weekend. But it’s hobbled by an overly familiar story that never really does anything to make it memorable.

Hexed is the first non-sequel to come out from Walt Disney Animation since 2023's Wish, which earned a 48 percent on Rotten Tomatoes from critics and made just $254 million worldwide. It's too early to call this movie's true RT score, but there's similarly middling reception so far. AV Club's Jacob Oller had this to say:

The basic beats of the ensuing adventure are painfully predictable, with all the rescue attempts, training sequences, familial bonding moments, and climactic showdowns coming right on cue. But these checkboxes aren’t just predictable, they’re the most obvious versions of themselves.

The movie features Hailee Steinfeld voicing a rebellious teenage witch named Billie, as she finds out she has magical powers. Rashida Jones voices her overprotective mom, and Jodie Foster voices the movie's villainous witch, Celeste. The movie was co-written by Walt Disney Animation's CCO Jared Bush and Moana 2's Dana Ledoux Miller.

Both of these reviews seem to sing a similar tune. They seem to find Hexed to be predictable and pretty unimaginative. The Guardian's Benjamin Lee had comparable thoughts, writing these words to go with his two-out-of-five-star review:

Like Wish, it’s a film clearly crafted by people who have deep affection for the Disney back catalogue but who can’t quite turn reverence for what came before into any real idea of where to take things going forward. Again, there’s something too calculated and businesslike, with nods to previous films and regurgitated life lessons, and while Disney adventures have always emerged from a Goliath-sized mega-corporation, the best ones can make you briefly forget that.

Then there's Variety's Guy Lodge, who pointed out how Hexed is perhaps particularly disappointing in the wake of the stop-motion movie Wildwood, which came out last month. Here are some of his thoughts:

The Hollywood Reporter's Angie Han had more positive things to say about Hexed, but made a point of saying the visuals dazzled her more than the storyline itself. In her words:

More than as a fairy tale, an inspirational fable or a complex exploration of the bonds between mothers and daughters, Hexed shines brightest as a travelogue through an enchanting world only accessible through the portal of a screen. If that’s not enough to make for a new cinematic classic, it’s more than enough to make for a pleasurably transportive trip to the theater.

It's certainly worth pointing out that Hexed will likely be much more of a hit with its target audience of young girls. That remains to be seen, but not all the reviews are bummers. The Wrap's Amelia Emberwing celebrated the movie's girl power and made these comments:

This is a film that is boldly sure of its own identity, and refuses to be anything other than blissfully witchy and womanly. The girl power aspects never extend into girl-bossery, and the film offers not just exciting normals that should be discussed more often in young adult media, but an ending that, while the logical conclusion of the story, also goes against traditional Disney norms and the standard teen movie tropes.

With regard to the box office, Hexed is projected to make around $40 million behind the new Hunger Games, which isn't great. As a lifelong Disney fan who wants to support original animation and see it thrive, I'm concerned. The studio really needed an original hit, and so far the news isn't trending up.

As with all reviews we talk about, we definitely recommend you check out the movie anyway, especially if you were already looking forward to it. Who knows, maybe you'll disagree? Oh, and Hexed has a Lilo & Stitch short before the movie not to miss. Hexed hits theaters on Wednesday!

What Cloudflare Doesn't Tell You

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cloudflare has arguably done more to push modern internet technologies than any other company. The work they have done to enable TLS, IPv6, post quantum encryption, and other modern standards for millions of websites deserves a tremendous amount of credit.

While this is great, Cloudflare also hides many of the shortcomings of the underlying infrastructure from both users and website owners. Yes, you can connect to a Cloudflare website and it'll allow for IPv6, post quantum encryption, and TLS. But Cloudflare's connection to the origin server might be entirely unencrypted and rely exclusively on IPv4. This creates a huge mismatch between what is being presented to users and the reality of the connection.

One of my biggest problems with this is the fact that Cloudflare doesn't do enough to notify website administrators about these issues. While Cloudflare provides tools to inspect these, comprehensive visibility is lacking. The Cloudflare dashboard should notify you about each of these issues where Cloudflare is following better practices than your origin server, without website administrators having to dig through and specifically look for this information. Clear warnings and proactive alerts to administrators would help greatly.

Not only that, but according to Cloudflare's own documentation, they actively prefer IPv4 over IPv6 when making origin server requests, which is particularly disappointing for a company that has done so much to advance IPv6 adoption:

For proxied DNS records that have both an IPv6 and IPv4 origin address, Cloudflare will prefer the IPv4 address when connecting to your origin server.

Even when website administrators invest in deploying IPv6 on their origin servers, Cloudflare still prioritizes IPv4.

I also believe the industry should consider adding functionality so CDNs and other intermediate networks can expose origin TLS connection information alongside their own. This would allow users and network researchers to better understand the encryption state of the entire path and hold website owners accountable for bad security practices. A similar mechanism could expose which IP version is being used to communicate with the origin server.

Cloudflare has an opportunity to not only make modern internet technologies accessible to users, but also encourage adoption of those technologies throughout the underlying infrastructure. Cloudflare should be a catalyst for modernizing the internet, not a mechanism that allows website owners to avoid modernization indefinitely. Improving transparency would be a meaningful step toward achieving that goal.

Why I Have Mixed Feelings On How 90 Day: Hunt For Love Addressed Razvan’s Exit

Conversely, Ally was very clearly showing ahead of their big reveal, to the point that I'm not sure it was even necessary for Jonathan to state it.

Can a Solar Generator Actually Keep Your Lights On During an Outage—or Is It Just Expensive Hype?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Winter storm season is fast approaching, with even Thanksgiving being primed for power outages across the country.

If you’d like to limit its disruption on your everyday life, generators may be top of mind.

As you do your research, you may come across solar generators and wonder how they compare to other options.

“A solar generator uses sunlight as its fuel. It also happens to be clean, quiet, and quick,” explains Ben Zook, founder and owner of Belmont Solar, a solar installation company in Gordonville, PA.

While a solar generator will likely be more expensive upfront, it could be a smart investment, depending on your location and goals.

By understanding how it works as well as its limitations, you can decide whether it’s the right backup solution for your home.

The name “solar generator” is a bit misleading.

This device is actually a portable battery system you can refill with solar panels and use when you don’t have power.

It includes a built-in inverter that converts DC electricity into AC electricity, which most household appliances use. In addition, it might come with USB ports to charge electronic devices such as cellphones.

So, how does the recharging process work?

“Put simply, solar panels convert sunlight into electricity. When sunlight hits the panels, it produces an electric current,” explains Fox Swim, senior industry researcher at Aurora Solar in Nanaimo, British Columbia.

Keep in mind that a solar generator doesn’t provide unlimited power. It’s limited by your battery’s capacity and how much electricity your panels can produce.

Fortunately, solar generators have evolved over the years and started to incorporate more powerful inverters and larger-capacity batteries.

“Some of the more expensive portable solar generators can now be wired into a home’s main wiring with a transfer switch and provide enough power to run a home's critical loads, like refrigerators, lights, and internet modems for multiple hours or even days,” says Daniel Moyer, a senior trainer with Briggs & Stratton Energy Solutions in Arcata, CA.

Portable gas and propane generators are usually cheaper than portable solar generators.

"For a high-quality portable gas or propane generator, budget around $700 to $1,000. Comparable solar generators are going to cost you closer to $1,000 to $1,500,” says Mike Feazel, CEO of Roof Maxx in Westerville, OH.

While portable gas and propane backups could save you some cash, they do come with drawbacks that are important to consider.

“These options are noisy, produce exhaust that must be vented properly, and demand regular maintenance. In addition, they require you to buy and safely store fuel,” Swim says.

Solar generations generally cost more to buy and install, but there are a few reasons for this.

“The panels are quieter as they have no moving parts, require less frequent maintenance, and don’t rely on fuel,” Fox adds.

One of the major downsides of solar generators, however, is that they may produce significantly less electricity during winter, depending on where you live.

Fox’s simulations show just how much production can vary.

In Boise, ID, a 1-kilowatt solar generator produces about 176 kilowatt-hours in a typical June compared to just 66 in December.

In Huntington Beach, CA, the same setup generates 156 kilowatt-hours in June and 127 in December.

So, how do you know whether solar backup is a smart resilience investment or overkill for your needs? It comes down to your unique situation and goals.

To make the most informed decision, ask yourself what you’d need to power if the electricity goes out and how often that would probably happen.

“If you only lose power a couple of times a year and mainly want the fridge, internet, and some lights on, a solar generator makes a lot of sense,” explains Feazel.

However, if your goal is to power your entire home and you live somewhere with frequent, prolonged outages, a solar generator may not be powerful enough. You might be better off with a whole-home generator.

“If you’re unsure, consult a reputable solar installer about your budget and your expectations for backup time and what you need powered during an outage. They can point you in the right direction,” adds Moyer.

Google files patent for AI-generated TV channel that uses your face and voice for shows

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google has filed a patent application for a personalized television channel that uses artificial intelligence to generate shows starring the person watching.

Streaming platforms such as YouTube and Netflix already use viewing habits to recommend content, while companies have also started experimenting with broadcasting entirely AI-generated entertainment.

Google’s patent application, titled “Creating a Channel of Video Experiences Tailored to a User,” was originally filed on May 13, 2025, before being published by the US Patent and Trademark Office on November 19, 2026.

First spotted by Patentlyze, the application describes a feature called “My Channel” that would appear within a television app and offer a selection of videos created specifically for individual viewers.

The system would use information including a person’s viewing history, location, language settings, and demographic data to determine what kinds of shows they might enjoy.

Google could then use photographs to create a digital avatar of the viewer and replicate their voice, allowing them to star in everything from romantic comedies to science fiction adventures.

One example included in the filing describes how the system could generate a video starring someone who had recently watched cricket highlights. The viewer could appear in the stands during the Cricket World Cup or interact with one of their favorite players.

Users wouldn’t necessarily have to request new content, either. The application outlines a scheduling system that could automatically generate videos on a weekly, monthly, or yearly basis. It also describes a mechanism for inserting advertisements into the generated content.

Google now lets anyone make & use their own Android apps using AI

New Microsoft patent could use AI to alter speech and lip movements in real time

YouTube tests prompt-based custom feeds that let you control your homepage

However, Google has not announced plans to launch the feature. According to Patentlyze, a patent examiner has issued a final rejection of the application, although the company can still amend its claims or appeal the decision.

The filing comes as AI-generated programming has already made its way onto streaming platforms, with Roku introducing a television channel dedicated to broadcasting AI-generated content around the clock.

Hong Kong wants banks to secure new approval before holding customers’ crypto

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The proposed legislation would extend SFC oversight to crypto custodians as financial institutions grapple with security and regulatory challenges.

Hong Kong plans to require banks to register with its securities regulator before offering crypto custody services to customers.

The government published the Virtual Asset Services Legislation (Amendment) Bill 2026 on Nov. 20, expanding its regulatory framework to cover businesses safeguarding, trading, advising on or managing virtual assets.

Under the legislation, banks and stored-value facility operators already supervised by the Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) would need additional registration with the Securities and Futures Commission (SFC) to provide covered virtual asset services. Independent providers would generally need an SFC license.

Hong Kong’s bill would generally require a license for standalone custodians and registration for HKMA-supervised banks and stored value facility licensees.

Both forms of authorization would come from the Securities and Futures Commission.

The diagram covers the proposed authorization routes. Exceptions and implementation timing depend on the legislation.

The two routes follow the government’s gazettal announcement and the earlier custody consultation. Existing bank standards follow the HKMA’s May 2026 guidance.

The diagram covers the proposed authorization routes. Exceptions and implementation timing depend on the legislation.

The bill, scheduled for its first reading in the Legislative Council on Dec. 2, would amend Hong Kong's Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Terrorist Financing Ordinance. Applicants would face fit-and-proper assessments and other regulatory requirements designed to strengthen investor protection.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The additional requirements build on existing regulatory obligations for banks entering the digital asset market.

In May, the HKMA updated its guidance requiring financial institutions offering digital asset custody to maintain appropriate security, governance and operational controls.

Banks intending to launch such services must consult the monetary authority beforehand and demonstrate compliance with its custody standards.

The regulatory expansion comes amid growing institutional interest in digital assets, although security concerns and regulatory uncertainty have slowed adoption.

A February report by Ripple and Quinlan & Associates found that 77% of 65 surveyed Hong Kong financial institutions had either adopted digital assets or planned to do so. However, only 25% had adopted them at the time of the survey.

The research found that 65% of respondents preferred outsourcing custody, rising to 80% among buy-side institutions.

Bank-affiliated custodians attracted the strongest institutional interest, with 64% of respondents expressing willingness to engage them. However, only 40% rated their readiness highly.

Hong Kong's proposed custody framework targets the security risks associated with controlling customers' digital assets.

In their December 2025 consultation conclusions, the SFC and the Financial Services and the Treasury Bureau identified the safeguarding of private keys and similar instruments that enable asset transfers as the central activity requiring authorization.

Regulators said businesses controlling these instruments face technological and operational risks that could result in the loss or unauthorized transfer of customers' assets.

Fund managers and other institutions that delegate custody to third parties without safeguarding private keys would generally fall outside the specific custody licensing requirement.

Similarly, noncustodial wallet providers that let customers retain independent control of their assets would generally not require authorization. Shared-key arrangements would be assessed based on the provider's ability to transfer assets and customers' ability to retain or recover access independently.

The proposed virtual asset custody regime would exclude tokenized securities, which remain subject to existing securities regulations.

Existing crypto custodians could face more immediate compliance pressures once the legislation takes effect.

During the consultation process, some industry participants requested transitional arrangements lasting between three and 18 months, citing potential operational disruptions and the time needed to meet regulatory requirements.

However, authorities rejected automatically granting existing operators temporary authorization, citing concerns about investor protection and confusion over their regulatory status.

Instead, regulators proposed expedited approvals for certain banks, bank subsidiaries and entities associated with licensed crypto trading platforms that had already undergone supervisory assessments.

Authorities also encouraged existing custodians to begin engaging with the SFC or HKMA ahead of the new regime.

While the bill is scheduled to reach the Legislative Council on Dec. 2, the government has yet to announce a commencement date. That decision will determine how much time existing custodians have to secure authorization and avoid disruptions to their businesses.

How to Move From Worrisome What-Ifs to Wondering What-Ifs

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 24, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

If you're tired of feeling like you're never quite enough, or that you're never going to get "there" (wherever there is), consider the following questions.

Instead of skimming through these questions the way you might read a typical blog post, I'd like to suggest that you wait until you have a quiet moment. Sit or lie down. Read the words silently or out loud, and really consider them. Try them on, the way you might try on a piece of clothing to see how they fit.

Notice what happens inside your body as you read. See if any of these ideas can sink in. If they can't right now, that's OK, too.

Sometimes old programming runs deep and can feel hard to change. With patience and practice, old beliefs can be questioned and updated, and new ways of thinking and being can take root.

So get comfortable, take a slow, deep breath, and consider these possibilities.

What if I remembered that self-critical thoughts aren't necessarily facts, and I didn't have to believe everything I think?

What if I could begin choosing new thoughts and beliefs that are kinder, more supportive, and actually feel good?

What if I got off my own back, got on my own side, and decided that I am a good person doing the best I can?

What if I remembered that kindness, compassion, and encouragement help people grow, while berating, pressure, and perfectionism often get in the way?

What if my worth didn't depend on how much I accomplished, how productive I was, or how well I did things?

What if I allowed myself to do things imperfectly, and let good enough be good enough?

What if I could let myself be a beginner, make mistakes, change my mind, and learn as I go, rather than expecting myself to get everything right every time?

What if I spent less time living in worst-case scenarios about the future and more time in the present moment?

What if I slowed down, ever so slightly, and sprinkled small moments of conscious breathing and quiet awareness throughout my day?

What if I stepped out of the never-ending race to get somewhere and spent more moments in actual, factual reality?

What if I softened my gaze when I looked at myself and softened my thoughts when I thought about myself?

What if I spent less time comparing myself to others, remembering that I know nothing about what their lives actually feel like on the inside, and instead offered myself more moments of grace?

What if I became curious about what enoughness feels like?

What if I decided that, in this exact moment, I am already enough? I can still do what I'm moved to do, pursue what matters to me, and take care of what needs to be done without staying trapped on the endless treadmill of never-enoughness.

What if I simply said, Thank you for what I have, kept the welcome mat out, and stayed open to whatever comes next?

What if, just for today, I let myself be enough?

Was there a really good song that just started that youâre annoyed youâre going to miss it to go do a mission? Maybe youâre knocking over a bowling alley and want Kaleidoscope FM as the background noise to add your own needle-drop moment to the scene? Mitsubishi Electric Research Lab CEO on How He Reached the C-Suite

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthony Vetro’s journey from multimedia pioneer to executive

Kathy Pretz is the editor in chief of The Institute, IEEE's member publication

At the 2025 IEEE Vision, Innovation, and Challenges Summit, IEEE Fellow Anthony Vetro discusses the need for open innovation models to keep pace with rapid technological change taking place at Mitsubishi Electric Research Laboratories.

The trajectory from researcher to top executive at a leading R&D organization doesn’t happen overnight. It took Anthony Vetro nearly 30 years to climb the corporate ladder at Mitsubishi Electric Research Laboratories, in Cambridge, Mass. He joined MERL in 1996, right out of college, and he has worked there ever since.

Vetro probably is best known for his research in multimedia signal processing, focusing on video compression.

Anthony Vetro Employer Mitsubishi Electric Research Laboratories, in Cambridge, Mass.Title President and CEO Member grade FellowAlma mater Polytechnic Institute of Brooklyn (now New York University’s Tandon engineering school)

Mitsubishi Electric Research Laboratories, in Cambridge, Mass.

Polytechnic Institute of Brooklyn (now New York University’s Tandon engineering school)

He became an IEEE Fellow in 2011 for his contributions to video coding, three-dimensional television, and multimedia adaptation.

Mitsubishi Electric has moved away from making televisions. It now focuses on developing systems including ones used for defense, electronics, the energy industry, and space exploration.

His ascension up the executive ranks began in 2018, when he was named a vice president and director of MERL. In 2023 he was appointed its president and CEO.

He also works closely with the company’s new U.S.-based innovation facility in Cambridge. And he recently took on a dual role as deputy head of Mitsubishi Electric’s global R&D activities, including in Asia and Europe.

Vetro credits IEEE with helping him develop his management skills after he served in several IEEE Signal Processing Society leadership positions. He also learned from his key roles in establishing international standards for digital image formats and video compression, he says.

“Between my IEEE activities, standards work, and some of the business shifts in Mitsubishi Electric, I eventually graduated to more management leadership roles,” he says.

Vetro is the son of immigrants. His father came to the United States from Italy and his mother from Sweden when they were both in their early 20s. They settled in New York City, on Staten Island.

Although neither parent went to college, his father stressed the importance of education to his son at an early age.

“My father started in the mailroom and worked his way up to eventually managing communication accounts and infrastructure for large financial companies,” he says. “My mother worked in banks, first as a teller and later doing bank reconciliation after pursuing some professional certifications.

“Through their jobs, they saw people with college degrees getting better positions and promotions, so education was something really ingrained in me.”

Vetro says he developed a passion for mathematics and engineering in middle school. He was accepted into the Staten Island Technical High School, a specialized pre-engineering institution. There he solidified his interest in pursuing a career in the field.

“A lot of students didn’t know what they wanted to major in at college, but I knew fairly early,” he says.

He was accepted into the accelerated bachelor’s and master’s engineering degree programs at the Polytechnic Institute of Brooklyn (now New York University’s Tandon engineering school). He began his bachelor’s degree program in 1992 and the master’s program in 1994.

“As part of the master’s degree program, I needed to do a thesis,” he says. “I wasn’t sure exactly what it would be on. Soon after taking my first course on image processing, I was certain that was an area I wanted to work in.”

It was an interesting time, he says, because broadcast television was going through a major transformation, shifting from analog to digital during the early 1990s. It was long before streaming services, he notes.

“I feel that industry-level engagement in IEEE is very vital to the organization in maintaining high relevance in the community and fulfilling its mission of fostering technological innovation and excellence for the benefit of humanity.”

“The idea of being able to come up with compact ways to represent this very rich information was really appealing to me,” he says. “I’m also a visual learner. I liked seeing images, and there was some nice math behind them, too.”

After he earned both degrees in 1996, Mitsubishi Electric hired him for its technical staff. He conducted research on multimedia signal processing, focusing on video compression.

“The opportunity to work on projects around digital TV broadcasting and image processing attracted me,” he says.

He conducted research in video conferencing, Internet and mobile streaming, and video coding technology, widely used for broadcasting, DVDs, and Blu-ray discs. He developed several technologies including digital TV receivers and displays, surveillance and camera-monitoring systems, automotive equipment, and satellite imaging systems.

Another advantage of joining the company, he says, was that it allowed him to pursue a doctorate in electrical engineering, also from Polytechnic, while working.

“That really sealed the deal,” he says. He received the degree in 2001.

After six years as a technical staff member, he was promoted in 2007 to manage the group working on digital video coding, processing, and streaming. Seven years later, Vetro became its deputy director, a top-level management position that he held until 2018.

“After working for about 10 years exclusively on video compression processing technology, there were some shifts in our company’s business. Its overall strategy began to change,” he says, “so I became interested in broadening my experience beyond video.”

He was asked to lead the lab’s speech and audio team. It was more development-oriented, he says, and focused on vehicle infotainment and speech interfaces before Alexa and Siri existed. He pivoted the team’s focus more toward research and established strategic paths tailored to emerging opportunities within the organization. Recognizing emerging trends in sensing and information security, he formed groups that worked on them.

To help spur innovation between Mitsubishi Electric and its U.S. customers, the company in June opened Serendie Street Boston, its first digital transformation hub in the United States. It’s colocated with MERL.

The new office is modeled after the first Serendie Street collaboration space, which Mitsubishi Electric launched in 2024 in Yokohama, Japan.

The word serendie is a combination of serendipity and digital engineering, according to the company’s website. Serendie is the digital platform designed to foster collaborations between data, people, and technology, it says. The space where people interact to extract ideas from the data is called “the street.”

The platform brings together researchers, startups, customers, and partners to identify new products, test them, run pilot programs, and scale them up.

Vetro says he’s excited about the endeavor.

“Serendie Street [Boston] aims to strengthen the bridge between our global R&D efforts and real-world industry challenges. The hub is intended to bring our partners and customers together to engage in more cocreation activities, where we listen to their problems and work together to solve them,” he says. “I think the company has come to recognize that we can’t just do it all internally by ourselves.”

The technologies being worked on include robotics, energy solutions such as integrating renewable energy into the grid, and more energy-efficient data center systems.

The decision to colocate the hub with MERL offices was deliberate, Vetro says.

“The area has a very rich ecosystem,” he says. “We have Harvard, MIT, and many other universities a stone’s throw away.”

Vetro joined IEEE in 1992 as a student member during an event the Polytechnic’s student branch was holding. He enjoyed reading the IEEE Potentials student member publication and other periodicals, he says.

“In the earliest stages of my career, IEEE was a place where I could attend conferences, read journals, and stay on top of the latest research and trends,” he says. “Over the years, I was able to build a professional network, which has been helpful. That facilitated a lot of research collaboration with university professors attending the conferences.

“In more recent years, IEEE has provided a great platform for industry to exhibit thought leadership and discuss real-world problems we’re facing. I think these discussions may have even set some research directions among the academic community.”

Vetro is an active volunteer for the IEEE Signal Processing Society. He has chaired several of its technical committees and conferences, and he served as an associate editor for some of its journals.

He says he’s most proud of establishing the society’s industry board and serving as its inaugural chair. The board formed a corporate engagement program, launched the Industry Signals newsletter, developed short courses tailored to industry, and holds career fairs at the society’s flagship conferences.

Vetro also had a hand in MERL sponsoring two IEEE technical field awards: the Flanagan Speech and Audio Processing Award and the Fourier Award for Signal Processing.

“Industry-level engagement in IEEE is really very vital to the organization in maintaining its relevance to the community and fulfilling its mission of fostering technological innovation and excellence for the benefit of humanity,” he says.

Kathy Pretz is editor in chief for The Institute, which covers all aspects of IEEE, its members, and the technology they're involved in. She has a bachelor's degree in applied communication from Rider University, in Lawrenceville, N.J., and holds a master's degree in corporate and public communication from Monmouth University, in West Long Branch, N.J.

1st-ever detection of a dying star's 'final nuclear heartbeat' challenges what we know about our sun's eventual death

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe
