Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"We've caught, for the first time, a star's final nuclear heartbeat, frozen in gas."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As ubiquitous as the sun is to us today, our star is not eternal; one day, albeit in billions of years, it will die. While massive stars die in supernova explosions, the sun and stars of similar mass die quieter deaths. But that doesn't mean these stars completely go out with a whimper.

New observations collected using the Atacama Large Millimeter/submillimeter Array (ALMA), the world's largest radio telescope comprised of 66 antennas in northern Chile, reveal that the sun's death throes may be different from what scientists had initially believed.

NASA Jet Propulsion Lab (JPL) researcher Raghvendra Sahai, who led the investigation, explained the shape the ALMA observations took: "It's essentially a physical snapshot of an event that, until now, we've mostly inferred from theoretical models. We've caught, for the first time, a star's final nuclear heartbeat, frozen in gas."

Sun-like stars "die" when they run out of hydrogen in their cores. This means that the fusion of hydrogen to helium ceases in the stellar core, and because this nuclear process is a star's main energy source, the star no longer has the outward push to support itself against the inward push of its own gravity.

As the star's core collapses to create a stellar remnant called a white dwarf, the outer layers where hydrogen is still fusing to form helium swell out. This expansion leads to a relatively short-lived "red giant" phase. Eventually, these outer layers will cool and disperse, leaving the central white dwarf surrounded by a so-called "planetary nebula" shell of debris.

During the red giant phase, as the stellar core is surrounded by shells of hydrogen and helium, these layers are occasionally rocked by flares in nuclear activity called thermal pulses. During these episodes, stellar material is flung into space.

To better understand this phase in stellar evolution, the team focused ALMA on a star called IRAS 06530-0213, located around 12,000 light-years away in the constellation Monoceros. This revealed a giant ring of gas that appears to be a frozen fossil of the star's last thermonuclear flare-up. This thermonuclear fossil rings an hourglass-shaped planetary nebula in the act of forming.

"Most of us think of stars as unchanging points of light, but this is a real-time snapshot of a star's death throes," Sahai said. "It's the brief, violent transition between a bloated red giant and a glowing planetary nebula, frozen mid-action."

The team was able to determine that this gas shell of IRAS 06530-0213 is around half a light-year wide and was ejected into space around 6,500 years ago. It represents the first direct evidence of the last thermal pulse before this kind of star enters the final phase of its life.

The team thinks that for around 2,000 years after the pulse, the star lost mass at a lower rate. This mass loss then increased sharply over the following 1,000 years. This led to the creation of a lumpy shell of gas now seen as a tangle of material filaments around the dying star. The fact that these filaments have a spiral shape could indicate that IRAS 06530-0213 has an unseen partner orbiting it, stirring the gas.

Meanwhile, the hourglass-shaped nebula immediately around the star began to take shape around 700 years ago, meaning that the red giant phase for IRAS 06530-0213 only ended very recently, in cosmic terms at least.

There are also unexpected elements to the ALMA observations of IRAS 06530-0213.

The team discovered an unexpected abundance of heavy versions, or isotopes, of elements like carbon, oxygen and nitrogen around IRAS 06530-0213.

This doesn't conform to models of how stars like IRAS 06530-0213 forge elements and mix them into their surroundings, though similar signatures have been seen around odd stellar objects, with violent bursts of nuclear fusion thought to be the cause.As well as seeing something unexpected, the researchers didn't detect something they expected to observe.

Dying stars like IRAS 06530-0213 have previously been known to have companions, typically sitting with them inside compact disks of dust. These disks are indirect evidence of material being dragged from the close companion star to the dying star. This is a process scientists have used to explain jet-sculpted hourglass shapes.

However, ALMA failed to detect such disk around IRAS 06530. The team suggests that this could be because a disk existed, albeit briefly, during which time it powered a short burst of high-speed jets that helped sculpt the hourglass, following this the disk simply faded away.

"Dying stars so often have hidden companions that reshape their ejected material into intricate, bipolar or multipolar forms instead of simple spheres," Sahai concluded. "IRAS 06530 shows that the story is more complicated than the tidy explanation we've been using."The team's research was published on Friday (Nov. 20) in The Astrophysical Journal.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

The biggest limitations of ChatGPT

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From fabricated citations to forgotten instructions, ChatGPT still has some frustrating blind spots.

It's no secret that ChatGPT can write up emails, become your software troubleshooter and even dumb down complicated questions in an instant. But it's also pretty capable of making up responses so polished that you might not think to verify the shaky facts behind them. If only most of us had the confidence that ChatGPT has when it is hallucinating. Even after OpenAI brought its advanced GPT-6 model to all ChatGPT users in October 2026, the chatbot still has the tendency to fabricate supporting evidence, misread an ambiguous question and even forget instructions amid a lengthy conversation.

Some shortcomings are tied to the model or version of it that you're using, along with any plugins you've added. And just because you're paying for ChatGPT Plus or the $100 ChatGPT Pro plan doesn't mean you're guarded against the chatbot's biggest limitations. To be fair, this isn't a problem unique to OpenAI, as most of these shortcomings are common in AI assistants we compared, like Claude.

Topping the list of ChatGPT's biggest quirks is its bad habit of hallucinating, or crafting details that seem legitimate but are entirely made up. Packing all the confidence of a subject-matter expert, ChatGPT will cite studies that don't exist and make them almost believable with imaginary quotations too.

OpenAI is quite upfront about this and warns that ChatGPT could invent quotes and cite fake sources. Using ChatGPT Search does make things better, but just because the response has some live links doesn't make the final statement correct. While an expert on the topic could probably catch the counterfeit sources upon closer inspection, this could be troublesome for someone looking for references in uncharted territory.

ChatGPT can also follow detailed instructions and acknowledge a mistake you've pointed out, only to ignore it a few prompts later. One user on OpenAI's community forum reported that ChatGPT reused earlier details in a report and presented them as verified, then repeated the mistake after admitting it.

But then, that's one person's experience and not exactly strong evidence of this being a problem across the board. That said, researchers found that the reliability of language models tends to go down as input length increases, even if the tasks aren't inherently complex. The researchers tested 18 different models, using older versions of OpenAI's GPT, Google's Gemini and Anthropic's Claude, so the findings may not be an accurate depiction of how GPT-6 performs. And just because you've fed your AI model an enormous amount of context doesn't guarantee it will make the most of it.

If you're working on a lengthy project across several days, being mindful of the changes requested and repeating the given instructions is part of the play. Sure, it can get frustrating that a tool that should be saving you time needs so much babysitting, but the alternative is far more time- and labor-intensive. OpenAI also acknowledges that ChatGPT's Memory feature doesn't retain every detail from every conversation anyway.

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

US Attorney General Todd Blanche has backed Nvidia as a "good corporate citizen" while refusing to comment on whether it was being investigated by the administration.

Blanche said he had no information about Jensen Huang's security clearance status, as lawmakers demand answers about President Donald Trump's involvement in Huang's reinstatement as the AI microchip maker's chief executive.

Huang was given his job back less than two weeks after being forced to resign over revelations of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Asked several times on Tuesday if the justice department or FBI had investigated Huang, Blanche responded: "I don't know what you're talking about."

Huang is still set to appear before Congress next week, where he will be questioned over his relationship with a former executive for Megaspeed - the alleged China spy - due to national security concerns.

The US commerce department has also announced it was preparing to blacklist tech company Megaspeed, the Singapore-based Nvidia customer that has been suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

In a joint statement last week, the commerce and justice departments said they began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

But speaking to reporters on Tuesday, Blanche said the latter department has "hundreds, if not more" export investigations occurring at any one time.

"So when you say the expert investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about," he said.

Asked if he was satisfied Nvidia had been co-operative with any efforts to get to the bottom of whether the company or Huang potentially knew about chips being diverted to China, Blanche said: "I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen."

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target or was subject to some criminal investigation or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that that the president is saying to the American people ... It shouldn't be what my message would be either," Blanche said.

While Nvidia's stock market value surged on the announcement of Huang's reappointment, some of the gains were reversed in volatile Tuesday trading.

The company's share price was down more than 7% in early afternoon trading, but remained well above the level it closed at on Friday, before Huang's return was announced.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

The company's board reinstated Huang on Monday in a shock U-turn on its original position. Harvey Jones, chairman of Nvidia's board, said Huang's return was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

On Monday, Trump said he spoke to the company's directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

There has been bipartisan political anger over Huang's reinstatement.

Republican representative Don Bacon said Nvidia should have "ignored the Administration's inexplicable desire to save Huang", while Republican senator Pete Ricketts said Huang's behaviour "doesn't absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips".

Democratic representative Robert Garcia, the incoming chairman of the House oversight committee, said Trump's "direct involvement" with Nvidia's decision "raises serious national security and corruption questions", and Democratic senator Ruben Gallego said Congress needed to "investigate what stock positions took place before the announcement".

The Savage Model 99 Is Still One of the Greatest Lever Action Rifles of All Time

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 2:47 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

In the early years of the twentieth century whitetail populations were making a comeback and deer hunting was a growing sport. Lever action rifles ruled the woods for at least the first half of the century and beyond. Even in my early years of hunting in the late sixties, lever actions seemed to fill the gun rack in every deer camp. The most popular was the Winchester Model 94, usually in .30-30, and this rifle dominated the deer woods.

While the 94 was the gun for the masses, the gun guys, the hunters who “knew stuff” had a different pick. The Savage Model 99 rifle was often their choice. While the tube magazine on the Winchester and Marlin lever actions limited the rifles to use with blunt nose bullets, with its rotary box magazine the Savage 1899 could use pointed bullets for a ballistic advantage. It was also strong enough to handle powerful cartridges using that newfangled smokeless powder.

The lever action Model 99 featured an internal hammer and many advanced features like a cocking indicator and even a counter to show the number of cartridges in the magazine. It was simply a more sophisticated design than the other lever actions. The introduction of the .250 Savage cartridge for the Model 1899 in 1915 ushered a new world of deer cartridges. At the time most cartridges barely broke the 2,000 ft/s barrier, but this one hit 3,000 ft/s with an 87-grain bullet. The savviest hunters recognized the advantage over the big, slow, hard kicking, straight walled black powder cartridges that were still in vogue at that time.

Five years later in 1920, Savage changed the name to the Model 99 and introduced the .300 Savage, a cartridge that at the time matched the ballistics of the .30-06. With these advanced cartridges the Savage Model 99 rifle became the thinking man’s deer rifle.

Prior to WWI bolt action rifles were almost unheard of for deer hunting and the Savage Model 1899 lever action rifle led the industry in innovation and performance. Even after the war when bolt action sporting rifles began to emerge into the market, the Savage remained one of the most popular hunting rifles on earth.

So how did this rifle come to be? As with many of our great firearms in history it was because of one visionary, Arthur William Savage.

Savage was born in Kingston, Jamaica on May 13, 1857. He received an education in the United States and in England, which was probably designed to have him follow in his father’s footsteps and become a government bureaucrat.

But Savage wanted adventure, so as soon as they handed him his diploma he hightailed it for Australia to look for black opals and interesting experiences, as Teddy Roosevelt called them.

At some point he was captured by aborigines and held captive for a year. One version of the story says that his captors demanded a ransom, but his family refused to pay. The historian was unable to provide details as to why, but I suspect that Christmas dinners with his family were a bit tense after his escape.

Savage explored the wild Australian interior in a covered wagon. Later he became the owner of the largest cattle ranch down under. He married Annie Bryant while there and eventually had four sons and four daughters. After eleven years in Australia, Savage returned to Jamaica at the age of 34 to grow coffee.

Savage loved firearms and machinery. He was always tinkering on new ideas. He improved the British service rifle. Among his inventions was a torpedo that he sold to the government of Brazil. He also developed the recoilless rifle and the detachable rifle magazine. Savage soon recognized that the future was in America, not Jamaica.

He traveled to New York where he took a job managing the Utica Belt Line railroad in Utica, NY. In his spare time he developed a hammerless, lever-action rifle. He patented the design in 1893 with an unwavering belief that this gun would lead the way into a new world of firearms. With a few investors, he founded Savage Arms in 1894.

It took courage to open a gun company in a field that was dominated by Winchester, Remington, and Colt, but Savage recognized that the newly invented smokeless powder was about to change the rifle world and that the low-pressure, big-bore, rifle cartridges were going to become obsolete. Europe was already using smokeless powder in some cartridges and here in America in 1892, the Army had adopted the .30-40 Krag as our first smokeless powder cartridge. Some sporting rifles were chambered for this new cartridge and Savage could see the future was with small bore, high velocity smokeless powder cartridges.

The first Savage rifle was the Model 1892, although it never got into production. Savage built it to compete for a military contract, which ultimately went to the .30-40 Krag bolt action. Savage continued to work on the design and it evolved into the Model 1895. That rifle won a contract to supply rifles for the New York National Guard. But politics prevailed, as it always has and probably always will in NY, so the contract was cancelled. The poor soldiers were sent off to fight in the Spanish-American war with obsolete single-shot trapdoor Springfield rifles.

With the Model 1895 rifle Savage also introduced a new cartridge, the .303 Savage, a bottle-necked, .30-caliber (.311 diameter bullet) cartridge designed for smokeless powder. Savage created the cartridge with a military contract in mind, but that never developed so he turned to the sporting rifle market.

The Model 1895 rifle, which was reported to have been built by Marlin Firearms Company for Savage, was revolutionary as it was designed to use modern, bottle-necked, smokeless powder cartridges and could handle pointed bullets. The new, smaller .303 Savage cartridge had to compete with the physically larger and more popular cartridges of the day, like the .45-70 Government. At the time the public still equated killing power with physical size and convincing them that technology would allow this diminutive cartridge to perform better than those claiming brute size as their biggest asset would define the struggle for success.

Proving he understood marketing as well as design, Savage started running testimonials in his catalog about the effectiveness of the new cartridge. In those days the catalog not only displayed the products, but it was a source of information for hunters and gun owners in the era before internet, television, or radio. Savage recognized that and ran first person accounts of one-shot kills with the .303 Savage on moose, deer, caribou, sheep and even grizzly bears. But the clincher was from an Alaska resident who killed a whale with a single shot from the .303 Savage.

The Model 1895 evolved into the Model 1899 rifle. That went on to become of the most successful hunting rifles in history. On March 22, 1960 Savage presented the NRA with the one millionth rifle made. No one is quite sure about the total sales number, but it would continue to be made for 38 more years.

A little known fact is that Savage did much of the development work on his early rifles using the machinery at the Remington factory. It’s said that he offered his lever action design to Remington.

Remington and Winchester were owned by the same company at the time and it’s thought that Remington was being locked out of the lever action market by upper management. Winchester didn’t want the competition. It’s what led to Remington developing their semi-auto and pump action line of hunting rifles.

But if you think about it, if those in charge had a little more foresight and had allowed Remington to dip a toe into the lever action market the Model 99 would have had the Remington name on it and Savage Arms might never have existed.

Savage was a restless soul and in 1904 he sold his interest in the company to a group of businessmen and headed west to California. There he started the Savage Tire Company, which grew to employ as many as 2,000 people at one time. It’s said that he invented the radial tire. Later he would have citrus groves, drill for oil and mine for gold. He died in 1938 at the age of 81. His was a life well lived and his lasting legacy would be the Model 99 rifle.

Savage Arms of course continued on without Arthur Savage and by the 1920s it became the largest firearms company in the free world.

In what was probably the start of a Savage tradition, the Model 1899 and 99 rifles were made in a dizzying number of models and configurations. At one time there were more than 700 SKUs of the Savage Model 110 rifle. I am not sure the Model 99 matched that, but it sure tried. Entire books have been written about the various models of this rifle.

There were octagon and half octagon barrels. Carbines and rifles with barrel lengths from 20 inches to 26 inches. The military musket had a 28-inch barrel. The 1895 had a 30-inch tube. There was even a model with a .410 shotgun barrel. Some had a saddle ring and others had deluxe wood. Still others had deluxe wood and fancy engraving. Some rifles had cut checkering and many had no checkering at all. In later years as they tried to cut production costs and ultimately ruined the rifle, some of those stocks had pressed in rifling. Some 99s had straight levers and grips, but most had curved levers with a pistol grip. The forends ranged from Schnabel to a plane Jane straight forend. Some rifles were take-down and others were not. Some came with tang mounted peep sights and others with a very innovative scope mount. It wasn’t until 1950 that Savage started drilling and tapping the rifles at the factory for scope mounting. Some carbine models had a barrel band, but most models did not. The rotary magazine was at some point modified to remove the counting window and the traditional brass was replaced with steel. In 1965 they introduced the “C” model which featured a detachable “clip” magazine. (Yes, they called it a clip. No matter what they tell you on the internet, “clip” was a commonly used term to describe removable magazines in sporting rifles for decades.)

The Savage Model 99 features a hammerless design with a much faster lock time than a traditional lever action. The lever is connected to the breech bolt by a long, wide, curving piece of metal. This gives the rifle a distinctive profile when the action is open. The breech block locks into the action as the lever is closed creating a strong action design that is suitable to modern high-pressure cartridges. While it’s not often noted, the Model 99 action is a controlled round feed design. That means that the extractor captures the cartridge as it exits the magazine and holds it against the bolt face during its travel into the rifle’s chamber. There is a cocking indicator in the form of a pin that sticks out of the top of the action when the gun is cocked and recedes inside when it is not.

The stock is two pieces with separate forend and buttstock. The bottom of the metal action is rounded which makes this rifle a delight to carry in your hand.

The safety is a small catch on the right side of the lever. Pushed forward it locks the lever shut and prevents the rifle from firing. Pulled back it is in the fire position. This is opposite of the more common safeties seen on modern rifles where back is safe and forward is fire. Shooters were smart enough to know the difference back then. Sometime in the mid-sixties a top-tang shotgun style safety was added on some models, but the traditional style lever locking safety remained more popular.

The .303 Savage was the first cartridge introduced in 1895. It was more or less a ballistic twin to the .30-30 introduced the same year. These were the first sporting cartridges designed for smokeless powder and it’s debatable which one was first. The .303 Savage is a handloading-only deal now, but I have always wanted one just because. My friend Lane Pearce just bought one and called to gloat. I hate him.

The .22 Savage High Power was next. It was designed by Charles Newton and introduced in the Model 99 in 1912. It was based on the .25-35 Winchester cartridge necked down to take a .228-diameter bullet. With a 70 grain bullet and a muzzle velocity of 2,800 ft/s it was marketed for big game. In fact, Savage ran testimonials of foolish feats like a 400-pound Tiger taken by Reverend H. R. Caldwell with .22 Savage Hi-Power. Or W.M.D. Bell, the demigod of inadequate rifle cartridges, shooting a Cape buffalo with one. I am sure that probably sold some guns and no doubt got some people killed. But, the truth won out and the cartridge lost favor. Not saying I don’t want one; I do, but not for hunting big game.

Next came the Newton designed .250-3000, which is simply called the .250 Savage today. Newton designed it with a 100 grain bullet, but the marketing folks at Savage wanted that “3000” in the name and reduced the bullet weight to 87 grains to achieve that goal. Terminal performance was erratic until the ammo companies finally listened to Newton and made ammo with a 100 grain bullet. Sadly, he never saw it come to fruition as he died just before the ammo was introduced.

The .300 Savage is the flagship. It was 1920 and the war was over. The doughboys coming home had seen the .30-06 in action and they were impressed. Savage knew that the cartridge would not work in the Model 99, so they created one that would. They shortened the .30-06 case, made a short neck and a sharp shoulder so they could pack all the powder possible into the case. The result was a 150-grain bullet at 2,700 ft/s. That matched the sporting load for the .30-06 at the time. (The velocity was later increased in the .30-06 to 2,900 ft/s.) The .300 Savage soon became one of the most popular hunting cartridges and many gun makers other than Savage chambered it in their rifles. In addition to my Savage Model 99 I have a Remington Model 760 pump action and a Remington Model 722 bolt action rifle in .300 Savage, two of the more popular rifles in that cartridge.

When the military was looking for a new short action cartridge they looked at the .300 Savage. In the end they modified the case to create the 7.62 NATO which was introduced to the sporting world as the .308 Winchester. The pity is that the .308 killed off the .300 Savage. It wasn’t long before the .308 was outselling the .300 even in the Model 99. I don’t think any major rifle maker offers the .300 Savage today. Not even Savage.

The Model 99 has been chambered for a lot of cartridges over its life span. See the list below. In addition to the .303 and .22 HP my bucket list includes a .358 Winchester and the very rare .284 Winchester. The trouble is, the folks I have encountered selling them are asking prices that make a gun writer question his career choices.

The cartridges chambered in the Savage Model 1899 and Savage Model 99 over the years.

The early guns were not clearly marked with model information and trying to figure out which model a gun is can drive a Baptist Deacon to a hard drink. Mine is a 99-EG as far as I can figure out. It was made in 1936 and has smooth, non-checkered wood, which adds a premium to the value. But, it hardly matters now. At some point the gun was drilled and tapped for a scope, which detracts from the collector’s value but adds to the “usability” of the rifle. There is a Weaver 1.5-4.5 Wide Angle scope on it with Weaver mounts. This TV screen scope was introduced in 1973 to compete with the Redfield Widefield scopes and it makes me think it was added to the rifle in the mid-seventies. Sling swivels have also been added and at some point a skilled craftsman repaired a cracked stock. Which means this is no collector’s rifle. That suits me fine; I bought it to hunt with, just as Arthur Savage intended.

My rifle has what I think is the 1926 style front sight ramp that is integral with the barrel. I have to wonder what that would cost to reproduce today. The rifle sold in 1939 for $47.00. So I am guessing that when mine was made three years earlier it was maybe $45.00? That barely buys a box of ammo today.

It was a sad day in 1998 when I got a call from the marketing director of Savage to inform me that the Model 99 was being discontinued. I had a gun on loan for an article which with that call changed its focus. The gun was shabbily made and unlike the elegant 99s of the past, it felt like a dead fence post in my hands. The truth is, if this is all they could produce, it was time to stop. I should have bought it at the very good price he offered, but I didn’t. I returned it to Savage and mourned the end of a dynasty.

Read Next: The 300 Savage: A (Nearly) Perfect Mid-Range Hunting Cartridge

Savage experienced some hard times shortly after the Model 99 was dropped. Ron Colburn took over the company and made a drastic move to drop every single product except the Model 110. Everybody said he was crazy, but he saved the company. Savage today is healthy, vibrant, and is building good guns.

Once Ron’s brilliance became apparent and Savage began growing and prospering we all asked when he would bring back the Model 99.

“Never,” he said with his Irish brogue. “It’s just too expensive to make because it requires a lot of hand fitting of parts. Besides, the market isn’t there anymore. I wish it wasn’t true, I dearly love the rifle, but that’s reality.”

Ron never wavered from that until the day he retired.

The same question came up with the current owners during a press conference in early 2020 and the Savage folks provided the same answer almost by rote.

Most recently I contacted Jessica Treglia, Sr. Brand Manager at Savage Arms. Here is what she said:

“We have no current plans to bring back the M99. The cost to manufacture this gun would push the price above $1500 and would not be well received by our customers and thus the volume would not cover the cost to tool up to make the rifle. That said, a lever action is not in our current plan but is on the long term one. It will not, however, be a M99.”

I am of the belief that this “conventional” wisdom is wrong. Much has changed since the Model 99 stopped production. The “high cost due to the labor of hand fitting the parts” is no longer an excuse.

With modern computer controlled machinery and new manufacturing techniques Savage can reduce greatly the labor cost. I am of the belief that the rifle could be made and sold at a price the market will accept.

The question is simply that, the market. Is it there to support a return of the rifle? Sadly, I think the answer is no. The world has moved on. There is a bit of a nostalgic return of interest in lever-action rifles and surprisingly some movement to using them as self-defense rifles in states with harsh gun laws. But, that is aimed at the more traditional lever actions. Sure, a lot of the diehard gun guys would buy a new Model 99 if it was properly made. But, sadly, there just are not enough of us to justify the cost of bringing it back. However, with Savage’s new ownership … who knows what the future might hold.

The OL editors pick the best deer hunting rifles on the market.

Google DeepMind’s 544-Prompt HFE Test Exposes LLM Consistency Gaps

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google DeepMind on November 24, 2026 launched Humanity’s First Exam on Kaggle, a benchmark of 544 elementary text and image prompts designed to test whether large language models answer routine questions consistently, not just correctly once.

The accompanying technical report evaluated 18 frontier and fast-tier models. Its primary measure, family pass^4, estimates the probability that all 16 sampled attempts across four related prompts are correct; family pass@4 instead asks whether at least one of those attempts succeeds.

The report ranks GPT-6.2 Astra first at 92.4% family pass^4, followed by GPT-6.2 Sol at 86.9% and Gemini 4 Neon at 85.8%, although all three reached 100% family pass@4. The report calls the difference the Family Reliability Gap: 7.6, 13.1 and 14.2 percentage points, respectively. That difference matters for automated work in which an occasional correct answer is insufficient.

The 544 prompts form 136 families, each containing a seed and three controlled variants that preserve the underlying task while changing entities, clause order or visual attributes. The 20 subcategories include object counting, color identification, spatial reasoning, arithmetic, constraint satisfaction and false-premise verification. Of the total, 316 prompts combine text and images, while 228 are text-only.

One public example asks for the colors, in order, of the third letter of each word in a neon sign. The reference answer is green, yellow, white, blue, blue. Such tasks are deliberately easy to explain, while their variants test whether a correct answer depends on a particular phrasing or image.

Researchers evaluated the models zero-shot in a single turn without external tools, drawing six independent answers per prompt and grading the visible final answer with a deterministic Gemini 3.1 Pro judge. The analysis found that among the top 12 models, 61% to 85% of families with any failed rollout had just one problematic prompt while the other three were solved on all six runs.

The report’s failure analysis attributes 73% of wrong-answer rollouts to visual perception, including 31% involving position, direction or paths in images and 23% involving object counting. Among the top nine models, family pass^4 ranged from 83% to 97% on text-only families but fell to 64% to 89% on multimodal families.

The report’s reasoning-effort tests show that moving Gemini 4 Carbon from low to high effort raised family pass^4 by 17.4 percentage points overall, with a 42.4-point gain on Math and Algorithmic tasks versus 3.1 points on Vision tasks. The pattern indicates that additional test-time computation can catch symbolic and procedural slips while leaving visual-perception and variant-robustness failures largely unresolved.

The report’s screening process began with 315 complete five-prompt families. The researchers retained only families whose seed was solved by at least three of four independent human raters, audited ambiguity and variant fidelity, and standardized each surviving family to four prompts. Single-rater human accuracy on the 136 retained seeds was 90.8%. Raters could not use LLMs, web search, calculators, code or writing assistants.

Only 112 prompts in 28 families are public under CC-BY-4.0; another 432 prompts in 108 families remain private to reduce contamination and overfitting. The Kaggle benchmark uses an isolated evaluation environment without public internet access and a 16-model leaderboard. It separately documents configuration and sampling differences from the paper’s 18-model evaluation, so their scores should not be treated as equivalent runs.

The authors caution that tasks were adversarially selected to expose model blind spots rather than sampled uniformly from everyday queries, and list synthetic imagery, reliance on one Gemini autorater family and seed-only human testing as further limitations. HFE therefore functions as a stress test of elementary reliability; its scores do not estimate how often models will fail across ordinary use.

Google DeepMind is also inviting submissions through a three-week Kaggle competition ending December 15, 2026, asking for everyday questions that current models still answer incorrectly so HFE can be refreshed. The central deployment question is whether a system can reproduce an elementary success on every call, particularly when small wording changes or basic image interpretation can break it.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

The Wall Street Journal joins a growing list of publishers disallowing bots by default

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

Many publishers are responding to a new reality on the open web: Bot traffic is rivaling traffic from human readers.

One of the world’s largest business publishers is shutting the door on unwelcome bots. In May, Dow Jones overhauled its bot strategy and now disallows all bots by default, the company told Nieman Lab. The new bot permissions apply to all of its free and paid consumer news sites, including The Wall Street Journal, Barron’s, and MarketWatch.

As part of the shift, the company revised each site’s robots.txt file — a webpage that tells bots which parts of a site they can and can’t access. Rather than reactively blacklisting bots through robots.txt, Dow Jones now operates with a whitelist for select crawlers and scrapers.

“As bot traffic has shifted from discovery-oriented use cases toward systems that can ingest, summarize, or otherwise benefit from our content without a relationship in place, we have moved to a more deliberate signal,” said Nolly Evans, chief development officer at Dow Jones. “[This] signals to third parties that they must negotiate and partner directly with us for content access.”

The Wall Street Journal’s whitelist contains more than 60 bots, including search engine crawlers and three scrapers operated by OpenAI, which has a $250 million-plus licensing deal with Dow Jones’ parent company, News Corp.

“We’re seeing astronomical growth in bot traffic while human referrals and traffic to news sites are being curtailed,” said Evans. “That’s a clear signal to publishers that the old norms are no longer working, and that something needs to change.”

Robots.txt files are voluntary guidance for all bots, including those deployed by AI companies. They’re not hard technical blocks or legally binding contracts. Some AI companies, like Perplexity, have used “stealth crawlers” to bypass robots.txt files while evading detection. Still, The Journal isn’t alone in using robots.txt to disallow bots by default, whether to create friction for scrapers, as leverage to negotiate for content licensing deals, or to minimize the burden on their servers.

An analysis of the robots.txt files of 1,000 leading English-language publishers websites by the bot analytics company Known Agents shows that about 2% of those sites now disallow all bots by default, including Reuters, Bloomberg Law, Publishers Weekly, and the British technology news site The Register.

Many of these publications are owned by News Corp, including several of the company’s Australian newspapers (The Australian, The Daily Telegraph in Sydney, the Herald Sun in Melbourne) and its marquee U.K. papers (The Times, The Sun, The Scottish Sun).

“One of the most important issues for us is making sure that the IP journalists create is respected by the AI companies,” News Corp CEO Robert Thomson said in an interview with CNBC last week. “The principled companies want to have an agreement, and the unprincipled companies don’t. We’ll try to convince you to be transactional and pay us for our IP and pay us for our journalism, or we’ll sue you.”

More and more publishers are opting to restrict bots using whitelists. Late last year, The Atlantic told Digiday that it only allows bots associated with its licensing deals or those that drive significant traffic to the website. In June, Digiday first reported that Reuters and Time had started issuing whitelists of their own.

People Inc. has been vocal about its decision to go beyond robots.txt files and hard-block bots by default. When the company made the switch at the beginning of this year, it went from blocking 2,000 unique bots to more than 30,000.

Many of these publishers are responding to a new reality on the open web: Bot traffic is rivaling traffic from human readers. Over the past 90 days, around 34% of pageviews across more than 5,000 randomly selected websites came from bots, according to an index maintained by Known Agents. About 29% of those bots were “AI-related” (e.g. AI agents, AI chatbots, and AI training scrapers), as opposed to more traditional bots like search engine crawlers and archivers operated by the Wayback Machine.

Despite some publishers wanting to restrict AI-related bot traffic, many aren’t making full use of their robots.txt files to do so, according to Gavin King, the CEO of Known Agents. The company started as a free crowdsourced database to help identify bots, but now offers publishers analytic tools and other products to monitor and control bot traffic.

Known Agents currently tracks more than 2,000 AI-related bots, but the majority of English-language news publishers only restrict a fraction of those on their robots.txt files.

“Robots.txt is the easiest first line of defense,” said King. “The fact that people are reaching out to [internet infrastructure companies to sign] six-figure year contracts instead of updating their robots.txt for free is such a miss.”

King acknowledged that robots.txt alone can’t stop content from being scraped without permission or compensation. But he also pointed to clients who have reduced traffic from known AI-related bots by more than 90% by updating their robots.txt file with a more comprehensive list.

“It’s been such a manual process,” said King. “Everyone is doing it individually, in-house instead of automating the process.”

Slate is one of the publications that has leaned on Known Agents’ “Automated Robots.txt” to try to stem the tide of AI bots to its site. Slate’s small product team uses the free version of the tool to access Known Agents’ API weekly and review any newly discovered bots or renamed agents that have been added to the company’s database. The tool automatically revises its robots.txt file based on that review.

“We have almost 30 years of content, which is a long history of being online,” Greg Lavallee, Slate’s VP of technology, said. “We’re seeing no value from bots and it costs us bandwidth fees, so we decided to tell them not to bother.”

Lavallee noted that since September, preliminary data shows that several bots have ignored the publication’s robots.txt and continue to attempt to crawl its website, though he declined to name the exact bots. Slate is exploring “server-side paywall strategies that will make it more difficult for scrapers to access full content,” he said.

Slate currently has no licensing deals with major AI companies, despite active outreach to buyers in the past.

“It’s very easy for us to take that robots.txt list and go look at the [server] logs and say, ‘We told you not to access and then you did,” said Lavallee. “I feel like you have to [update your Robots.txt file], but it’s definitely not enough. Something more needs to happen than asking nicely.”

Cognition Releases SWE-2.1 With Major Coding and Cybersecurity Gains

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cognition released SWE-2.1 on November 24, 2026, reporting a Terminal-Bench 4.1 score of 50.0, up from 24.2 for SWE-2. Alongside the base model are a Cerebras-backed Lightning variant and SWE-2.1 Cyber, a more permissive version rolling out to Devin Security.

Like SWE-2, the new model was trained with pure reinforcement learning on Kimi K3. Cognition attributes much of its coding improvement to targeting general intelligence and long-horizon workflows; its FrontierCode 2.0 score reached 41.3, compared with 24.1 for SWE-2. The same table puts Claude Opus 5.5 at 47.3 on FrontierCode and 65.8 on Terminal-Bench, leaving SWE-2.1 behind that model on both tests despite its gains over SWE-2.

Cognition expanded its training tasks and environments across short- and long-horizon workflows, spanning software engineering, science, performance engineering and cybersecurity. New post-training algorithms control rollout length during training. The company says reducing length oscillations stabilized training on tasks lasting from a few minutes to many hours, while performance on held-out evaluation tasks improved steadily over many days.

That recipe also changed how the model uses additional computation during a run. On ExploitGym, Cognition reports a log-linear increase in cumulative solve rate as normalized rollout time lengthens, with a steeper test-time-scaling curve than the base model. In practical terms, the model continued finding exploits as runs extended for hours. The chart normalizes speed with a fixed tokens-per-second rate, one second of time to first token and actual tool-execution time, so the sustained-run results reflect a simulated time budget rather than raw serving latency.

Cybersecurity is central to the release. Cognition reports 80% on ExploitBench, up from 36.1% for SWE-2, and a 27.3% ExploitGym success rate for intended exploits, up from 12.1%. Its broader flag-capture rate reached 44.8%, compared with 18.1%; that metric also counts successful real exploits unrelated to the vulnerability specified in the initial prompt. The results cover both vulnerability discovery and completion of full exploitation chains, often during several hours of autonomous work.

Those capabilities also changed the training-security requirements. Cognition red-teamed its training setup, especially its sandboxes, added continuous safety monitoring and built a canary evaluation that alerts researchers after a successful sandbox escape. The model discovered new sandbox vulnerabilities four times during training despite that red-teaming; Cognition says researchers were alerted and responded immediately each time.

The deployed model adds safeguards aimed at a narrow set of high-harm activities, which Cognition says are calibrated to leave most legitimate work, including cyber defense, unaffected. Because some defensive tasks require restricted capabilities, SWE-2.1 Cyber will use more permissive safeguards in Devin Security. It will power normal scans, while existing deep scans use frontier cybersecurity models such as Mythos, pairing a less restricted defensive model for normal scans with the established deep-scan option.

Cognition’s demonstration shows SWE-2.1 Lightning generating 600 tokens per second, compared with 60 for standard SWE-2.1 and 200 for SWE-2 Flash. The Cerebras partnership provides the same model at 10 times the standard rate, which matters because the model continues improving with additional test-time compute: higher throughput increases how much investigation and iteration can fit into an urgent fix or active incident. Lightning is free through December 8, 2026, and available in Devin Desktop, Cloud and CLI at a discounted price.

The benchmark numbers also depend on Cognition’s evaluation choices. It uses upstream or self-reported scores when available and otherwise tests models internally, with closed models in their developers’ preferred harnesses and open and SWE models in Devin CLI. ExploitGym uses pass@1, meaning one evaluated attempt, over 869 tasks with a six-hour timeout and mitigations disabled, while ExploitBench combines captured flags from five runs per task under a three-hour simulated clock. The release’s practical significance therefore rests on two connected questions: whether the longer-horizon gains hold outside these evaluation settings, and how much Lightning’s additional throughput converts them into completed engineering and defensive work under real deadlines.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

Her friend died after giving birth. Now she’s writing legislation to save other moms

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home / Health & Wellness / Women's Health

By Cassie Shortsleeve Updated Nov. 24, 2026

Mothering America is a regular column from Motherly and Chamber of Mothers sharing moms’ stories from across the country, showing the power of coming together to make change. Each piece is part of a larger effort to ensure every mother and family in the U.S. has the support and care to be well.

In 2008, Lauren Underwood was in graduate school at Johns Hopkins, earning a master’s in public health, when she met a woman with a big, bright smile named Shalon Irving.

She never would have imagined the impact that friendship would have on her life’s work. Underwood would eventually become a U.S. representative for the state of Illinois, fighting to protect moms like Irving, who died before she could see her friend take office.

At the time, Irving, who already had her PhD, was coming back to school for additional training in public health. “We were just like regular girlfriends, passing notes in class and everything,” Rep. Underwood says.

The two—Rep. Underwood, a registered nurse, and Irving, an epidemiologist—stayed in touch through the years. In January 2017, Rep. Underwood served as a senior advisor at the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, and Irving worked for the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). She had also given birth to a beautiful baby girl named Soleil.

Three weeks later, though, Irving wasn’t feeling well. “She went to the doctor several times, explaining that something wasn’t right,” Rep. Underwood remembers. On January 28, Irving died of complications related to high blood pressure. She was 36.

“Shalon had a career focused on ending health disparities, and she was so excited to become a mom,” Rep. Underwood says.

Today, Rep. Underwood is the architect of a series of 14 bills called the Momnibus, which was reintroduced to Congress this spring. The Momnibus addresses “every clinical and non-clinical factor” leading to preventable maternal deaths and disparities in this country; it’s evidence-based, data-driven, and offers states, communities, providers, and nonprofits the resources to help save moms like Irving’s lives. For Rep. Underwood, the Momnibus is personal.

While a member of Congress, Rep. Underwood is also a woman and a friend who, like too many in this country, lost a dear friend and felt a fire to make change.

Rep. Underwood recalls Irving’s funeral as devastating—particularly because many of those in attendance held positions of the highest authority in public health in this country. She remembers the head of the CDC speaking at Irving’s funeral and expressing bewilderment at her death.

For Rep. Underwood, it was no mystery why mothers like Irving die in childbirth and shortly thereafter. As a clinician, she had seen firsthand health disparities in action; as a Black woman, maternal mortality was an unfortunate “phenomenon where it’s just so common when you talk about the maternal crisis.”

Today, we know about 84 percent of pregnancy-related deaths like Irving’s are preventable—meaning that most of the moms who aren’t here today should be here, and could be here, with the proper supports in place. Black moms are more than three times as likely to die in pregnancy or the postpartum months than white women—a disparity that persists across income and education levels.

“People think higher education or higher income is protective. They think that if you are a healthcare worker, you’re going to get the best healthcare. If you know the risks, are self-aware, confident, and have agency, and you can speak up for yourself, then these kinds of things won’t happen to you. Shalon and her death fly in the face of all of those assumptions, and it breaks your heart.”

When she made the decision to run for Congress, Rep. Underwood knew that if she won, maternal mortality would be an issue she’d work on.

In 2018, Rep. Underwood became the youngest Black woman ever elected to Congress. She teamed up with Rep. Alma Adams (D-NC), who also had personal experience with maternal health: Adams’ daughter had experienced a very challenging labor and delivery, known in medical terms as severe maternal morbidity, or an experience that has lasting impacts on a mother’s health.

Together, in April 2019, the two formed the Black Maternal Health Caucus. At a crowded press conference, Rep. Underwood fought back tears. “To be in this moment with the dome of the Capitol behind us and these cameras and people asking questions and it’s this diverse, multi-generational, multi-ethnic, male and female moment of speaking about an issue that was so personal to me—I was just overcome,” she says. “It was a moment of power and resolve. It was an invitation. We were planting a flag, saying, ‘We’re here. We’re going to do this work. We know that there’s an opportunity to bring change.’”

Rep. Underwood didn’t expect the Black Maternal Health Caucus to grow into one of the largest bipartisan caucuses on the Hill. “We left that press conference and our phones started ringing, and it never stopped.”

Rep. Underwood has dedicated her career to ensuring everyone—moms included—has equitable healthcare so we can all feel our best. She’s also dedicated her career to ending the maternal mortality crisis. “We know exactly what to do. We just need to invest, have the resolve, and to say, ‘This is unacceptable. Let’s fix this issue.’”

She also reminds us that Congress works on the issues that members raise. “If a Member comes in and says, ‘This is important to my community, and this is an essential issue that we tackle,’ that’s how solutions advance.” Many of her ideas for bills, she says, come from your stories.

“Often, people think that their story doesn’t matter, and when they come in to advocate, they think they need to have statistics or be a walking encyclopedia,” Rep. Underwood says. “But we are in a relationship business, and connection is currency. The fastest way to build a connection is to share your humanity, tell your story, and invite space for that vulnerability.”

What that means for you: If you have a story to share, Members of Congress are home in their districts through the end of the month, with offices open Monday through Friday and available for meetings. (Not sure how to set up a meeting? Check out Chamber of Mothers’ ‘Moms at Recess’ guide, which includes step-by-step plans and tips for hosting a meeting with Members of Congress.)

Rep. Underwood also underscores the importance of being loud about solutions and progress in maternal health. She highlights the power of doulas, lactation consultants, and community support in maternal health—and the fact that, to date, one of the Momnibus’ 14 bills has been signed into law. Others have passed the House. The Momnibus has also unlocked more than $250 million for states, community organizations, researchers, and providers to save lives. (You can find out which projects are being funded with Momnibus dollars in your community through the Caucus’s Momnibus Money Tracker.)

“We want the news of a pregnancy to be a time of joy and hope and eager anticipation and wonder,” Rep. Underwood says. “This can’t just be a conversation around problems. We want people to know that we’re working on this, that we have their backs, and about the opportunities, help, and the resources that are available.”

FHFA maps out FICO 10T rollout with broad eligibility by late 2028

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

FHFA has approved the FICO 10T credit score model for Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac. It has also outlined a multiyear road map to phase it in while retiring Classic FICO by late 2028.  AI Summary

The Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA) has approved the FICO 10T credit score model for use by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac. It also released an implementation road map that would make FICO 10T broadly eligible and retire Classic FICO by September 2028.

The staged rollout, announced Tuesday, mirrors the VantageScore 4.0 timeline and is meant to give lenders, mortgage insurers and loan origination system providers time to analyze historic FICO 10T data, rework pricing and risk models, and update technology before a full transition, according to the agency.“The timeline below prepares lenders, mortgage insurers and loan origination system providers for the final step in credit score modernization: lender choice between FICO 10T and VantageScore 4.0,” the agency said. At the Federal Housing Administration (FHA), VantageScore 4.0 and FICO 10T are expected to go live alongside the existing Classic FICO in January.

The FHFA initially validated both FICO 10T and VantageScore 4.0 for use at the government-sponsored (GSEs) enterprises in October 2022. In July 2026, Fannie and Freddie released historical performance data for FICO 10T along with additional VantageScore 4.0 data, a key prerequisite for lenders and investors to back-test new score models and recalibrate credit policy.A FICO 10T pilot is expected to begin around December 2027. In this “launch and refine” phase, lenders will deliver a subset of loans underwritten with FICO 10T while FHFA, the GSEs and industry stakeholders monitor data quality, pricing and delivery performance.

Based on pilot results, FHFA expects upfront pricing grids and private mortgage insurer eligibility requirements, or PMIERs, to be updated to reflect the new score model. That step will be critical for secondary market execution and risk transfer structures that currently rely on Classic FICO.

A limited FICO 10T rollout is targeted for April 2028, followed by a scale-up and transition phase that leads to broad eligibility around September 2028. During this final stage, FHFA plans to confirm industry readiness, fully implement Uniform Loan Delivery Dataset (ULDD) Phase 5, expand FICO 10T use across GSE channels and retire Classic FICO.

FHFA emphasized that dates after November 2026 are targets and are subject to change.

In October, Fair Isaac Corp. said it will hold its wholesale mortgage price at $10 for a bundle of its credit scores for lenders and brokers enrolled in its Preferred Program. The $10 price is available through participating tri-merge credit report resellers to lenders that commit to FICO under the program. It covers both Classic FICO, which has been the mortgage standard for nearly three decades, and FICO Score 10T, the company’s trended-data model.

FICO is also preparing a new subscription-based pricing model for mortgage credit scores in 2027, sources told HousingWire.

VantageScore 4.0, by comparison, has been offered at 99 cents per score, or at no additional charge when purchased alongside a FICO score through Equifax, Experian and TransUnion, which jointly own VantageScore.

Bank of America Global Research, using data from Recursion, found that more than 170 lenders now pull VantageScore 4.0. Despite that breadth of use, only a small share of conventional loans are actually priced with VantageScore 4.0. In October, 20% of issuance reported a VantageScore 4.0, yet only about 8% of total issuance used the score model for pricing, according to the research.

WebMD Ignite brings OpenAI’s ChatGPT to platform for healthcare advertising" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Healthcare growth and engagement solutions provider WebMD Ignite is bringing in OpenAI’s ChatGPT to support health systems in advertising.

WebMD Ignite says it works with more than 650 health systems—including 95% of the 150 largest in the U.S.—and 80% of the 20 largest payers.

In Costa Rica, simple rope bridges offer sloths a lifeline as forests disappear

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Costa Rica has been celebrated for reversing decades of deforestation.

PUERTO VIEJO DE TALAMANCA, Costa Rica (AP) â Behind a beachfront hotel on Costa Ricaâs Caribbean coast, conservationists are installing rope bridges between trees, creating pathways for sloths in a landscape increasingly shared with tourists, hotels and other development.

For sloths, even a small gap in the forest canopy can be dangerous. Unable to jump between trees like monkeys, the slow-moving mammals are often forced to descend to the ground, where they risk being attacked by dogs, struck by vehicles or electrocuted on power lines.

To help them move safely through the treetops, the Sloth Conservation Foundation has installed approximately 410 rope bridges often across areas divided by roads and buildings.

âThe wildlife bridges that we install are just made from single ropes, which donât look very elaborate, but for arboreal species, they work,â said Rebecca Cliffe, a British zoologist who founded the organization and has spent nearly two decades studying sloths.

âIt is a lifeline to cross a gap that they otherwise couldnât cross.â

Costa Rica has been celebrated for reversing decades of deforestation, with forest cover increasing from an estimated 21% of its territory in 1987 to 59%, according to a 2023 report by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development. But the recovery has not eliminated habitat fragmentation, as development and tourism infrastructure continue to put pressure on coastal ecosystems.

Sloths spend much of their lives suspended from branches, relying on familiar routes between trees to find food and shelter.

Even a gap of 2 or 3 meters (about 7 to 10 feet) between neighboring tree canopies can force a sloth to find another way across, a phenomenon Cliffe calls âmicrofragmentation.â

âTheyâre creatures of habit,â she said, explaining that sloths tend to follow routes learned early in life, even as their surroundings change.

Cliffe began experimenting with wildlife bridges after working at the Sloth Sanctuary nearly two decades ago, where she repeatedly saw animals arriving with injuries from dog attacks, vehicles and electrical cables.

âI thought, well, the sloths just need a safer way to get somewhere,â she said.

With little funding, she began installing single ropes between trees. Cameras monitoring some of the bridges have since recorded more than 22,000 wildlife crossings, including animals other than sloths, Cliffe said.

Today, her team travels to sites where sloths have been spotted, including hotels and other properties along the Caribbean coast. At one beachfront hotel in Puerto Viejo, workers installed bridges among trees near an area frequented by tourists.

The project also involves planting trees and working with property owners to restore natural connections between neighboring plots. But the bridges are not permanent. Cliffe said ropes deteriorate after about four or five years in tropical conditions, requiring replacement.

Although Costa Rica has regained substantial forest cover, much of that growth has occurred on former pastureland within landscapes fragmented by decades of agricultural expansion, said Carlos Morera Beita, a geographer at the National University of Costa Rica.

Morera said sloths have demonstrated greater adaptability than previously believed, using tree-lined pastures, living fences and riverside vegetation to move between forest patches.

But these connections remain essential. Without them, even recovering forests can leave sloth populations isolated, limiting their access to food, mates and shelter.

Rope bridges can help animals cross specific obstacles, Morera said, but long-term conservation requires protecting existing forests, restoring biological corridors and ensuring new development does not further fragment habitats.

At the Sloth Sanctuary of Costa Rica on the outskirts of the small town of Penshurst, tourists observe sloths that cannot return to the wild because of severe injuries, disabilities or the loss of their mothers before they learned the skills needed to survive independently.

Inside, founder Judy Avey-Arroyoâs bedroom is filled with sloth memorabilia, from stuffed animals and paintings to lamps and ornaments. She and her daughter even have sloth tattoos.

Just beyond a connecting door is a nursery where rescued and orphaned baby sloths are cared for in separate enclosures. Many arrive after their mothers are killed by cars, electrocuted on power lines or attacked by dogs.

The youngest are kept in incubators and fed milk, while older babies are given leaves, outdoor exercise and opportunities to learn to climb.

But Avey-Arroyo said replacing a mother is nearly impossible. Young sloths spend about a year learning which leaves are safe to eat, which plants to avoid and how to navigate the forest along familiar routes. Mothers also teach them which leaves to seek out when suffering from parasites, she said.

Without that knowledge, even apparently healthy orphaned sloths can struggle to survive in the wild.

For Avey-Arroyo, whose work began after she and her husband rescued an orphaned sloth named Buttercup, knowing that some animals will never return to the forest is the hardest part of running the sanctuary.

Climate change could worsen the effects of fragmentation, as rising temperatures and prolonged droughts alter the conditions sloths depend on.

Small forest patches are more exposed to heat, wind and lower humidity than continuous forests, Morera said, reducing habitat quality even where trees remain standing.

Sloths rely on their surroundings to regulate body temperature and metabolism, moving between shaded and sunlit areas of the forest. Cliffe said prolonged periods of extreme heat can deplete their limited energy reserves, while cold conditions can disrupt digestion.

The concern has prompted conservationists to look beyond rope bridges toward broader habitat restoration, including planting trees that provide shade.

âItâs not just bridges that are going to save sloths,â Cliffe said. âYouâve got to provide the connectivity, you also have to provide the thermal refuges.â

Despite their popularity as symbols of Costa Ricaâs wildlife, scientists lack reliable estimates of how many sloths live in the countryâs forests or how their populations are changing.

Cliffeâs organization is experimenting with thermal imaging drones, trained detection dogs and field surveys to improve estimates. She said two rescue centers in the region receive approximately three sloths a day, although historical data is needed to determine whether rescues are increasing.

In coastal communities like Puerto Viejo de Talamanca, conservationists hope the bridges will eventually be replaced by trees growing close enough together for sloths to move freely through the canopy.

For Cliffe, protecting sloths ultimately comes down to the collective impact of small actions, whether planting a tree, preserving a branch or installing a simple rope bridge.

âItâs the cumulative power of many individual actions,â she said. âThatâs what really gives me hope.â

President Abelardo de la Espriella said that the men were âcommon criminalsâ who fled into the supermarket after a failed attempt to hold up an armored truck.

Footage shared on social media showed dozens of people rushing out of the Exito supermarket, which is located in a mall in Cartagenaâs La Castellana neighborhood, while local media have broadcast images of snipers on nearby buildings in what appears to be a standoff between the armed men and police.
